# DECISIONS.md

A decision belongs here when a later session could reasonably choose differently **and** the wrong choice would be invisible. Mechanisms with one obvious implementation, and anything a test already enforces, do not.

A decision is identified by its **bold name**, cited exactly. In code: `// see: Code owns every number`. In a document: `(see: Code owns every number)`. Same string either way, so `decision-resolves` covers both. Names carry no terminal punctuation, because a name ending in a period invites the paraphrase the check exists to reject.

A decision is changed only by another decision. Work that changes one writes a new decision, names what it supersedes, and moves the old entry to **Previously decided** in the same commit with its reasoning intact. Nothing here is struck through.

---

## Scope

**The universe is the S&P 1500's three indices with each member tagged by its index, and membership is fetched** Ruled by the operator with phase 15's brief of 2026-10-04 and its plan, approved on 2026-10-05: "The universe is the 500 plus today's 400/600 members, each tagged with its index." A published index is a fixed list rather than a filtered one, which keeps this a reporting tool rather than a screen, and membership is stored with join and leave dates so a name added last month is not shown as present in an older window. The S&P 500's members are the provider's, whose answer carries each span with its dates, and the S&P 400's and 600's are read from their funds' holdings files (see: The S&P 400's and 600's members are read each night from their funds' own holdings files), which carry today's holdings and no dates: a 400 or 600 span opens on the first night its fund lists the name, that night's session its join date, and closes on the night the fund stops listing it. The three indices hold no company in common, so a name a fund's file still lists after it joined the S&P 500, as IJH listed TWLO before its effective date of 2026-10-06, is read as the 500's member alone and its span in the fund's index closes. Every stage that stores or computes what each member needs reads the three indices' members: the bars, their backfill and their adjustment, the calendar, the reported quarters and their readings, the indicators, swings, profiles, levels, moves and facts, and the news pulse, a name's group read among its own index's members. Every stage that ranks, lists, gates, records or keeps a book reads the S&P 500's alone until each index is given rules of its own, and so does the ladder, whose builder a rule version's code version pins. A fund's file carries GICS's sector names where the provider's S&P 500 rows carry the provider's own, so a fund's row stores no sector, since a reader taking a ticker's sector off any of its rows would mix the two; a 400 or 600 member's sector is read from its company. The night's cost grows by the two files and each new member's year; the bulk file, the calendar and the news query stay one request each whatever the universe.

Supersedes **The universe is the S&P 500, and membership is fetched, not maintained**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but the universe it named.

**An announced index change takes effect on its effective date, and a joining name is stored from the announcement** The membership feed carries a rebalance before it takes effect, with the dates it takes effect on, and until the phase 5 sign-off every nightly stage and the read surface took a name for a member whenever its row had no leave date. The rebalance effective 21 September 2026 was on the feed by the 10th, so three names still in the index were stored nothing for and listed nowhere, and four names not yet in it were listed. A name is a member on a session when its join date is on or before the session, or is unknown, and its leave date is after the session or absent, which is the reading the membership loader already gave a past date. The stages that list, count or show the index read that. The stages that store what later stages read, the bars and their adjustment, read the wider set of every name that has not left by the session, which includes a name announced to join. That half is the choice a later session could reverse without seeing why: a backfill runs once, for a name holding no bars, so a name backfilled on its announcement and then not stored until its effective date would carry every session between the two as a hole nothing fills, and storing it early costs no nightly request, since the bulk file already carries it.

**A ticker the index feed stops listing leaves the index on the night it goes unlisted** Ruled on 2026-09-18, when the operator was given this and the alternative below and asked for everything to be fixed. The feed dates a leave when the index drops a name, and dates nothing when the provider renames a ticker: it lists the new ticker and stops listing the old one, with no end date on either. EQR became VMRK on 2026-08-18, and the joiner of 2026-09-21 that the price file carries as P was listed as P, then PSTG, then P_old, so a loader that closed only the spans the feed dated kept every one of them open, and from that rebalance the store's index would have held 506 tickers where the feed listed 503. So the night whose feed does not list a span still open on its session closes that span on the session, whether the ticker is gone from the feed or listed under another join date, and the upsert that writes a span the feed lists again reopens it. The alternative was a map, kept by hand, from the feed's tickers to the ones the price file carries, which would have kept the joiner's prices under P and is membership maintained rather than fetched; taking the feed as the index makes the joiner a member as P_old, holding no prices until the provider's two files agree, and its page says so. A feed that stops listing more than ten tickers in one night is read as a fault in the feed rather than as ten departures, since the index changes a handful of names at a rebalance and dates them, and a provider renames one company at a time: that night closes none, and the membership step ends partial with every ticker named, which puts it on the run page's stale-and-failed region.

**One universe now, the seam for more built now** Every stored row belongs to a name, not to a universe, and every screen takes the selected universe as a filter. That is a table and a where clause today, and retrofitting it later would mean reconstructing membership for every stored night.

**Long-only share plans** The operator does not short and does not trade options, so the ladder builder has no branch for either.

**Rules choose the stocks, and the AI writes the reports** The operator ruled on 2026-10-03, with phase 14's plan: "Rules choose the stocks, and the AI writes the reports." No model's output decides, filters, ranks or orders a pick, a family, a variant, a gate or a record. Every stock a list draws, and the order it draws them in, comes from rules over stored prices, filings, counts and dates; a model writes the research reports, and labels the news for the reader and for the reports alone. The reason is the test the picks are held to. A rule can be replayed over the stored history as each night stood and scored, and run again it gives the same answer; a model's judgment can be neither replayed as it stood nor reproduced, so a pick resting on one could be neither measured nor trusted to be the pick the record measured. It carries to every family, variant and check a later phase adds the line section 1 already drew for tonight's list, chosen on the chart and the plan with no model in the decision, and it is why the news check on the pullback was ruled to read only a score shown to be a fixed word list's (see: Six context checks are not adopted, each for the reason it states). `rules-choose-the-stocks` holds it: every component that decides a pick, a list, a family, a variant, a gate or a record declares no read of the research sections, the theme sections or the news labels and no model or search among its feeds, and no source under the folders their code runs through names any of those tables.

**Section 4 of the architecture defines the report region by region, and nothing outside the corpus does** The regions the name page draws, in the order it draws them, what produces each, whether each costs money and how often each changes are stated there, each named as the page's contents names it. A region is added, removed, renamed or reordered by amending that table, and `read-surface` reads the table against the contents of the name pages drawn over the fixture: every region a page draws is a row, in the rows' order, every row is a region some page draws, and the count the section states is its count of rows. A hand-written example illustrates the rules and is never the thing to be reproduced: where an example and a rule disagree, the rule is right.

Supersedes **Section 4 of the architecture defines the report, and nothing outside the corpus does**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but two statements: that the report has eleven sections, when the page had come to draw up to nineteen regions while the table stood as it was, and that `architecture-conformance` checks the code against the table, which no check did, the phase report placing section 4 outside the claim scope. The operator found the table out of date on 2026-09-24.

## Cost and when work happens

**The nightly run is arithmetic only** No model calls and no per-name network requests. Bars arrive in one bulk file and news in one feed request, so a night costs the same whether the universe is fifty names or five hundred.

**The night's zero-model-call rule bounds the arithmetic, and the overnight queue is carved out of it by name** Section 14's list runs to seventeen steps and step 17 calls the local model for every listed name whose research is missing or stale, while section 17's row read zero model calls in the nightly run, section 5's band said no model, and section 14's own closing note said no model is called. Four statements, and one of them is the step. The rule is kept and the step is carved, which is what 1.2 did for the backfill against the per-name limit rather than loosening a rule to fit its own exception. What the rule bounds is the arithmetic, being steps 1 to 16: those make no model call at all, and a night costing the same for fifty names as for five hundred follows from that rather than from anything the queue does. Step 17 runs after the arithmetic has closed and recorded its counts, makes local calls only, spends nothing, and no part of the arithmetic depends on it, so a queue that did not run leaves every figure above it unchanged and the run page says it did not. The carve is by name rather than by scope, so the scan that finds a model client keeps reading every other shipped file and the file the queue's client sits in is listed where a reader can see it (see: The outward-request scan names the files that may hold a client rather than dropping the patterns). A paid call from the queue is not carved and does not become so: the queue's own limit row says it makes none, and the local lane refuses a key, so the enforcement sits on the lane rather than on the step.

**Everything expensive happens when a name is opened** Reports are not generated for names nobody reads. A name never opened costs nothing beyond its share of the nightly arithmetic.

**Bars come from EODHD, bulk nightly and per ticker for the backfill** The provider is named in the architecture and the runbook and was never a decision, which left the route through it open to being reversed by a later session reading only the zero-per-name-call rule. The arithmetic is what settles it and belongs here rather than in a runbook table: bulk end-of-day for a whole exchange costs 100 weighted calls and a single-ticker historical request costs 1, so the nightly fetch takes the bulk endpoint at 100 against 500, and the one-year backfill takes the per-ticker endpoint at 500 against the 25,000 that replaying a year of past sessions through the bulk endpoint would cost. The two run opposite ways round for the same reason, which is that the bulk call is priced per request and the historical call is priced per name.

**Research is a stored document with an as-of date, not a cache** It costs money and produces a different answer each time it runs, so discarding it would change the report for no reason and leave nobody able to tell whether the analysis moved or the wording did. Every version is kept.

**Nothing expires on a timer** Research is rewritten when a filing appears, an earnings date passes, the name's news volume jumps above its own baseline, or the operator asks. A timer either refreshes work nobody needed or serves analysis written before a print.

**Deciding not to spend must not cost anything** The four staleness questions are answered from data the nightly run already computed, which is why the news pulse counter runs nightly and for free.

**Research goes stale on an event dated after the section was written, and a news spike is dated by the session it began** The two decisions above name the four things that make research stale and say none of them may cost anything, and neither says what "after" means, which is the whole of the judge. So each question is a date compared with a section's own as-of date, per section rather than per record because a record is written and dated per section, and a section written after a filing arrived is not stale because another was written before it. A new filing is a stored filing dated after the section. An earnings date has passed where the newest event on or before the night is dated after the section, or on the section's own date unless the provider filed it before the open: a section dated the day of an after-close print was most likely written before the numbers came out, and the cost of reading it that way is one rewrite where it was not, against a report standing on numbers that had already changed. A refresh makes every section stale. The judgement is as of the newest night the store computed for the name, read off its facts rather than off the wall clock, so a name opened on a Saturday is judged on Friday's close.

The news trigger is section 17's figures read as windows. The window is the last five sessions held, and it is above where it holds at least five articles and at least three times the baseline, which is the median of the five-session sums for every window ending in the ninety days before the current window begins, so a spike does not raise the baseline it is measured against. No earlier window is no baseline and no reading, because the floor alone would fire on every name the market writes about, and the pulse table begins the night the counter first ran, so for its first ninety days the baseline rests on fewer windows than it will and carries its count beside it the way a reading over fewer than twelve quarters does. A spike is dated by the session its unbroken run of windows above began, which is what makes section 17's claim true that the five-session window stops a story breaking over several days from firing twice: a section written during the run was written after the spike began, and the windows that stay above because they still hold the story do not make it stale again. A second story after the run ends is a new run with a later date.

A record is at least one accepted section. A name whose sections all fell back has rows and no research, and for staleness it is missing, because nothing written can go stale. (see: Nothing expires on a timer) (see: Deciding not to spend must not cost anything) (see: A research record is written and dated per section, not as a whole)

**Industry research is per theme, not per name** The passage about an industry's own pricing cycle is the same for every company in it, so one paid pass serves them all and refreshes when the first name needing it finds it stale.

**A theme is the industry the index names for a member, and one theme pass serves every member it names** The decision above says one paid pass serves every company in an industry and refreshes when the first name needing it finds it stale, and leaves open what an industry is, which a later session could draw anywhere with nothing showing it. The index's own constituents payload, which the membership loader fetches every night, names an industry for every current member beside its sector at no extra request, so a theme is that industry: the membership row carries it from 6.9, a theme pass searches for it by the name the index gives it, and the theme record lists it as the industry that maps to the theme. The company financials name an industry too, but only for the names someone has opened, so they cannot say which members an industry holds, and a theme has to be answerable for every member rather than for the ones read so far. A configured map folding several industries into one theme was the alternative, and the corpus's own example of a theme, memory-chip contract pricing, is narrower than an industry as the index names them, which are of the order of Consumer Electronics and Scientific & Technical Instruments; it was not taken, because a map with no measurement behind it is a guess at which industries share a cycle, and the list holds one industry until a decision folds several on evidence. A name's industry cycle is therefore never one of its own sections. It is judged by the theme's newest version, refreshed by the theme research runner before anything of the name's own where it is missing, left out on an earlier day, refused, or accepted before a trigger the staleness judge fired for the name, and read by the name page off the theme store for the name's industry. The triggers are the name's because the theme has none of its own, so a member opened for the first time fires nothing and reads the theme its industry already holds, and a member rewritten on a filing that arrived after the theme was written refreshes it for every member. (see: Industry research is per theme, not per name)

**The membership row carries the company's name the index feed states** The membership row carries the company's name, read from the span the index feed lists for the ticker, beside the sector and the industry it already carries. Two things read it. The name page states it beside the ticker, which it could not until now: the operator's first sight of the screens, on 2026-09-18, found a name page headed by a ticker alone. And a research pass reads it to tell an article about the company from one that only mentions it, because the provider attributes a document to every company its text names: the first live pass handed NVDA's calendar section another company's product launch, which the provider had attributed to NVDA alone and whose title named Xeal and not NVIDIA. The span rather than the snapshot object, because the spans carry a name for every ticker they list, current and departed, where the snapshot carries current members alone; coalesced as the sector is, so a night whose span states none keeps the name the row holds. The name is the provider's, with its legal suffixes, and anything reading it takes what it needs from it rather than the store holding a shortened form nobody can check against the feed.

**A theme section states no figure, because nothing the store holds is computed for an industry** The number rule holds every figure in prose to the facts file, and a facts file is one per name, so as 6.4 built the checker a theme section is held against none and every figure in one is refused, which 6.4 carried to the checkpoint that writes the first theme section. The lane table says every figure in the industry cycle is one code computed for the industry. The store holds prices and filings for companies and the index's membership, and nothing about an industry's own prices: contract prices, order books and capacity are published by research firms and trade bodies and arrive as prose inside the documents a search returns, so no code in this system owns one. A theme's facts file is therefore empty by rule rather than by omission, the checker holds a theme section against it, and the section is asked for with no figure and no full date: which way the industry's prices are moving and what is moving them, in words, every sentence citing a document in the theme record. The alternative was a facts file for a theme made of medians over the facts files of the members it covers, and it was not taken because that is a figure code computes about the members' shares rather than about the industry's prices, so a sentence quoting it would be true of something other than what the section is about. (see: Code owns every number) (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file)

**A pass for a name with no facts file refreshes its industry's theme and says so** The theme is the industry's and every member reads it, so a pass that reaches a name the night computed no facts file for still refreshes the cycle its industry is read under, and the cost is the twelve searches and the one call a theme pass makes, under two cents at the off-peak rate. What was wrong was not the order but the page: the name page said the pass did not run while a paid call had been made inside it, so the one surface a reader has said nothing happened and something had. It now states what the pass did and that nothing of the name's own was written, because no facts file is stored. Measured at 6.11 on AAPL, whose bars end before the fixture's night, in each of the three runs whose store is in hand: one theme call or two. Ruled at 8.0.

**A theme refresh runs off-peak, and a name opened at peak is written without one** Research rates double inside the peak windows the research model's configured prices name, and paid work nobody is waiting on runs outside them (see: Queued work runs off-peak, and every schedule is written in UTC). A theme refresh is started by one name's open and serves every member of the industry, most of whom nobody opened, so it is the part of an interactive pass that nobody is waiting on. The theme research runner therefore does not start at peak: its row on the run log says the pass was at peak and names the UTC instant the windows next close, nothing is searched or written, and the stored theme record is left as it was. The name's own sections are written as they would be, with its industry cycle named as not written with that line, and the next open of any member of the industry after the window closes refreshes the theme. Holding the name's whole pass until off-peak was the alternative, and it would leave an operator reading a name after the close waiting hours for sections that do not depend on the theme, which is the failure section 18 already refuses for a theme refresh that fails. The windows are read from the prices the configuration states, so a provider whose windows move is a change of configuration and not of this rule.

**The spend cap is a stop, not an allowance** At the cap, research pauses and the page says so. A cap that can be exceeded is not a cap, and one that fails silently is worse than none.

**The spend cap counts a UTC day and a UTC month, and refuses a call that could take spend past either** The decision above says research pauses at the cap and leaves open what a day is, what a month is, and what "at the cap" means for a call whose price nobody knows until it has been made, and each has a reading a later session could pick that would let the cap be passed with nothing showing it. A day is a UTC calendar day and a month a UTC calendar month, because the research model's own peak windows are written in UTC and a day read in the operator's zone moves against them twice a year (see: Queued work runs off-peak, and every schedule is written in UTC). A rolling twenty-four hours was the alternative, and it has no instant at which research resumes that a page can state; a calendar period has one, the next UTC midnight for the day and the first instant of the next UTC month for the month, and where both caps stop research the month is named, because it resumes later. Spend is summed from the run log's own rows by the instant each started, so the ledger is what the store holds rather than a figure a caller kept. The two caps refuse on their own. And a call is judged by the most it could cost as well as by what has been spent: a gate asking only whether the cap had been reached would let the call that reaches it spend past it, since a price is known only once the provider has counted the tokens, so a call is refused where spend plus its ceiling would pass either cap. The ceiling is computed before the call from what cannot be exceeded, being every byte of the prompt counted as a token, the answer's whole budget, and the peak rate, which is the bound a tokeniser over bytes permits. The cost is research refused inside the last call's worth of a cap that a real call would have fitted into, and that is the cost of a stop. (see: The spend cap is a stop, not an allowance)

**Every paid call is made through the spend cap, which holds each paid job's model** The decisions above say research pauses at the cap and how the cap is counted, and neither says where the check sits, which is the choice that decides whether the cap can be passed at all. Checked by each runner before it calls the model, the cap holds only while every caller remembers to ask, and the runners are components written at different checkpoints. So the cap is a component, and it is the only one that holds a paid job's model: a runner hands it a request and gets back an answer or a refusal, and has no model of its own to call instead. The model it holds is the one the job's profile names, so a job whose profile changes has its calls made through a cap holding the new model, and every paid call, whichever job and whichever model makes it, counts against the same day and month caps. Before a call it reads what the run log says was spent today and this month and judges the call by the most it could cost; after it, it writes what the call did cost on a run log row of its own, under the pass's run, one row per section asked. The ledger the cap reads is therefore the rows it wrote, measured off the store rather than a figure a runner kept, and a pass stopped at the cap is a row the run page shows rather than a pass that quietly did nothing. The alternative of a check inside each runner was rejected for the reason a feed holds its client: the rule belongs where the thing it governs happens. (see: The spend cap is a stop, not an allowance) (see: The spend cap counts a UTC day and a UTC month, and refuses a call that could take spend past either) (see: A paid job names its model profile in one word the operator switches, and a profile is priced at its configured rates at its call's own timestamp)

Supersedes **Every paid call is made through the spend cap, which holds the research model**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but that the one model it holds is the research model's.

**A research pass does not start where the research model does not answer** Section 18 says the pass does not start and the stored research still renders. A pass that found out at its first paid call would already have fetched its documents and written its local sections, and it would have stopped rather than not started. So a pass whose paid lane has a section to write asks the provider for its list of models first, a request that bills nothing and answers only where the address and the key both do, and does not start where it does not answer: nothing is fetched, nothing is written, and the pass's own row on the run log says why, which is what the name page reads. A pass whose every section is in the local lane asks nothing of the research model and is not stopped by it, because that lane costs nothing and its sections do not wait on the paid one (see: The overnight queue writes the local lane's sections that rest on no document for every name, and the paid model is for names you get serious about).

**A name opened again on the day its research pass ran starts no second pass unless the page asks for one** Section 17 says opening a name twice must never cost twice. A pass decides what to write section by section, off each section's newest version, so a second open writes nothing where every section the first pass warranted got a row. A section the first pass found nothing to write from gets none: no document was handed for it, or no document it was handed was published inside a move, and nothing was inserted, because an empty section citing what was handed would be accepted where it cited an admitted document and would say no admissible source was found where it cited none, which is not why it was not written. A second open would then fetch the year's news and the release again to find the same nothing, which is a second pass costing requests for no section. So a plain open on the session a pass for the name ran to the end starts nothing, and the run log's row for it says why. A pass the cap paused or the research model did not answer did not run to the end, so a later open starts one, since the cap resumes and the model returns. The page's two explicit asks, a rewrite and having the paid model write the local lane's sections, start a pass whatever ran that day, because each asks for something the first pass did not do, and what it writes is still decided section by section, so neither pays again for a section written that day. (see: Everything expensive happens when a name is opened) (see: Deciding not to spend must not cost anything)

**A paid job names its model profile in one word the operator switches, and a profile is priced at its configured rates at its call's own timestamp** The operator ruled on 2026-09-28 that each job calling a paid model names the profile it uses by one word, and that changing the word is the whole of switching its model. The shipped configuration holds one profile per model under `EquityBrief:Models:Profiles`: its wire format, the address its provider answers at, the model, the section of the secrets file its key sits under, any options the provider takes with a request, the provider's rates, and the earliest date the provider publishes for retiring the model with the day that was read. Each job's own section holds `Use`, the one word naming its profile, beside the job's answer budget and how long one of its calls may take, which belong to the job because a label and a report section need budgets of their own. Which profile a job names is the operator's to switch at any time: a switch is recorded as a ruling in PROGRESS with the day it was made and the operator's words, and is not an entry here, because an entry fixing the model would have to be superseded at every switch. The research job named `claude-sonnet` from 2026-09-29, and `deepseek` again on the operator's word the same day, to set the two reports on MDT side by side, with its answer budget back at the 32768 tokens DeepSeek ran on, since DeepSeek counts its reasoning inside the answer. None of it is in code, and the feed that sends a request is named for its wire format rather than for anybody's model. A call's price is its response's own counts at its profile's rates: the prompt tokens read from the provider's cache, those neither read nor written, those written to it where the provider charges apart for a write, and the output, multiplied where the call's own instant falls in a peak window the profile names, because the provider's clock decides what it bills and this machine's does not. DeepSeek's profile names two windows and Claude's none. The instant is the creation time the OpenAI format carries in its body, and the `Date` header of Claude's interface, whose body carries none. Reasoning is billed as output and both formats count it inside the output, so nothing is added for it. A model with no prices is refused at startup rather than called, because a call recorded as costing nothing is spend the cap cannot see, and a response carrying no counts is refused for the same reason. The identity a section records is the model with the options it was asked with, because the same model asked two ways is two writers and the recording key has to tell them apart. DeepSeek's rates are its page as read on 2026-09-13 at 14:57 UTC, and Claude's are https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing as read on 2026-09-29 at about 02:45 UTC; when a provider moves a price, the profile is what a change edits, and a call already made keeps the price its run log row carries. The scan that keeps a model off the night reads every shipped source file for the provider's name and for each format's wire, and finds them in the feeds alone. (see: Code owns every number) (see: Queued work runs off-peak, and every schedule is written in UTC) (see: A paid model is one interface with an implementation per wire format, and a job never falls back from the profile it names)

Supersedes **A paid job names its model profile in one word, and a profile is priced at its configured rates at its call's own timestamp**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but that it fixed the research job on Claude Sonnet 5.5.

**A profile carries its provider's earliest retirement date, and the run page names it from thirty days before** The operator ruled on 2026-09-29, the plan having found that Anthropic commits to Claude Haiku 4.5 only until "Not sooner than October 15, 2026", about two weeks away: each profile carries the earliest retirement date its provider publishes, read from the provider's models page with the day it was read, and from 30 days before that date the run page's list of anything to worry about names the profile, each job using it and the date. A provider publishing none, as DeepSeek's pricing page read on 2026-09-29 publishes none for `deepseek-flash`, leaves both empty, and no line is drawn for it. On the day the model stops answering, the job's own check that its model answers stops it with its plain line, and no other profile answers for it (see: A paid model is one interface with an implementation per wire format, and a job never falls back from the profile it names). Thirty days is the operator's lead time rather than a measurement, and section 17 pins it.

**A report costing more than two dollars is named on the run page the morning after it was written** The operator ruled on 2026-09-29 that the 0.84 to 1.30 dollars a report the plan estimated on Claude Sonnet 5.5 is an upper figure from DeepSeek's token counts, and that each of the first five reports written on it is recorded in PROGRESS with its actual cost from Anthropic's usage figures beside that estimate, as the labeller's first five nights are beside its own table. A report whose pass ran on a night's session and cost more than two dollars is named on that night's run page, in its list of anything to worry about, with its cost, so an expensive report is seen the morning after rather than in the month's spend. Two dollars is the operator's figure, above the estimate's upper end, and section 17 pins it.

**The six reports a night are taken in turn across the three indices, one at a time in the page's order** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-04, reviewing phase 15's plan: "The six reports: taken in turn across the indices, one at a time in the order S&P 500, S&P 400, S&P 600, repeating, so each index gets two a night when it has picks; within an index, the page's family order. An index with no picks passes its turn to the next." After its close and its overnight queue the night reads each index's page in its own order, the S&P 500's swing families' picks and then the stocks its sector heavyweights bought that night, and each of the S&P 400's and 600's lists and then its own heavyweights' buys, and takes one name from each in turn in the order S&P 500, S&P 400, S&P 600 until it has six, a page with no name left passing its turn and a name on two pages taken once; it writes one request for each, marked as asked by the night, a name holding a request outstanding or being written getting none, and the passes are the drain's own, paid at the off-peak rate as every pass is (see: Queued work runs off-peak, and every schedule is written in UTC). Where the S&P 400 lists nothing and the S&P 600 one name, the turns go S&P 500, S&P 400 for its one, then the S&P 500 four times. Six as before: the count is a constant section 17 pins, the paid passes a night bounded by it and never by the indices' size, and the night makes no model call for them and no request. It supersedes the ruling of 2026-10-01, which took the first six names of the S&P 500's page alone and is kept under Previously decided.

**The overnight queue drafts the three indices' lists before every other member, the S&P 500's first** Built at 15.1's third pull request on phase 15's plan, which has the queue take "the S&P 500's list, then the S&P 400's and then the S&P 600's, each in its page's order, before every other member, the S&P 500's first among those", once the first night over the three indices had stored a facts file for each of their members. The queue is bounded by an hour, which covers the S&P 500's members and not 1,500, so its order decides whose drafts a night writes: the pages' picks are the names a person opens first, and each index's are drafted in its page's own order, the S&P 400's before the 600's as the night takes its reports in turn. The S&P 500's names one gate short and its other members follow, as they did before, and then the S&P 400's and 600's other members, each index's in ticker order, the queue reading no list of their names close to a buy point. A name on two lists is drafted once, at its first place. A night that reads no wider index hands the queue none, and the queue reads the S&P 500's alone.

**The news labeller is a process of its own the night starts after the close, and its calls and its spend are its own** The hard rule has the night make no model call, and the operator's brief of 2026-09-28 asks for every stock on tonight's list to have its news labelled by a paid model. The two meet the way the night's own report request does: after the close the night starts the labeller as it starts the drain, a process of its own under a run of its own, so every call and every dollar sits on the labeller's rows and never the night's, and the night's rows, its list and its order are the same whether the labeller ran, stopped or failed. It is bounded by the list's length and not the index, by its own time limit, by its own month limit judged before each call by the call's ceiling, by the day and month caps every paid call counts against, and by a peak window of its profile; what a stop leaves waits for the next night, which takes what the window still holds. A night run again for an earlier session starts none, as it asks for no report, since the labels it would write are tonight's and not that night's. Ruled on 2026-09-29 in the 12.6 ruling, as the fifth carve-out of the hard rule.

**The news labeller waits for the end of a peak window rather than stopping at one, and its time limit counts the time it labels** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-07: "Actually fit the labeller addendum within phase 16", the addendum their queue ruling of 2026-10-05 named as the news labeller waiting for the end of a peak window as the drain does, where it stops at one now. The night starts the labeller after its close, and the news profile's peak windows run from 01:00 to 04:00 and from 06:00 to 10:00 UTC on weekdays; on the night of 2026-10-06 the labeller started at 01:35 inside the first window and labelled nothing, so a night ending inside a window labelled none of its list. So before each call, a retry's among them, where a peak window of the profile is open the labeller waits for it to end, as the drain does, and then asks: no call starts inside a window, which is how the decision above bounds it, and a window is no longer where it stops. Its time limit counts the time it spends labelling and not the time it waits, so a run started inside a window labels for its limit after it. The store's copy, which waits for the labeller the night started until its last row or its time limit and ten minutes more, reads the limit the same way: it waits until the latest instant a labeller started with it could end, its limit counted over the hours outside the windows, and ten minutes more, so it never copies while a labeller that waited still writes. This narrows the labeller's decision and the copy's, and both stand for everything else (see: The news labeller is a process of its own the night starts after the close, and its calls and its spend are its own) (see: The store is copied once the night and every process it started have finished, and the newest three copies are kept after each is opened and read).

**The news labeller reads the S&P 500's list alone until the operator widens it** Ruled with phase 15's plan, approved by the operator on 2026-10-05, which keeps widening the labeller to the S&P 400's and 600's lists among what is the operator's. The labeller labels the stored articles of the names on the S&P 500's list each night and reads none of the S&P 400's or 600's lists, so its calls and its spend stay what they were with three indices on the page; each index's picks are drawn with no counts of labelled stories until the operator widens it, which a ruling records and a change to the list the labeller reads makes (see: The news labeller is a process of its own the night starts after the close, and its calls and its spend are its own).

**The night asks for the estimates of each member a rule reading them passes on everything else, once a member a night** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03, "yes to the carve-out (the names the pullback passes, 1 to 3 a night, 10 calls each)", and registered on 2026-10-04 (see: The pullback's sector leaders' rule is retired and the analysts' revisions variant registered in its place). The revisions variant reads an estimate the provider keeps no history of, so the night asks for it. In the swing filter's step, once every member's gates are evaluated and before any verdict is written, the night asks the provider's fundamentals for each member a standing rule reading estimates passes on every gate, exclusion and condition but the estimates, once a member a night at the endpoint's weight of 10, and hands each answer to the verdicts (see: A member's estimates are raised where its current fiscal year's consensus earnings estimate stands above its level 30 days before). While the variant stands at the live filter's settings the members asked for are the pullback's own passes, a median of one a night and at most three over the seven live nights stored, so the request follows what the pullback passes and never the index. Each answer is stored with its run and read back by a night run again, which asks for no member it holds; a night run again for an earlier session asks for none, since the estimate it would store is today's; an ask that would take the night past the day's allowance is not made; and a member the provider refuses, does not answer in time or answers in a form that cannot be read is read as not raised and named on the stage's row, stopping nothing. The fetcher's own row each night the step runs states the members it read and asked for and the requests it made. It is the sixth carve-out the first hard rule names: a request a name on the night, bounded by the names a rule passes and never asked for every member.

## Numbers, claims and the models

**Code owns every number** Every figure in a report is computed from stored data or copied from a provider payload with its filing date. No model is ever asked for a number, a date or an estimate.

**Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file** Enforced by the claim checker. This is what lets a reader trust a paragraph a model wrote: any number in it traces to a computed value.

**A re-run replaces a night's facts file where the store now computes a different one** Until the phase 5 sign-off the assembler inserted with the conflict ignored, so the first file written for a night stood whatever a later run of that night computed, and its comment called that keeping the night idempotent. It was the opposite: a second run exists because the first went wrong, and on 2026-09-10 the attempt that stopped at the change detector had written every name's file from band sets a refetch had doubled, the run that completed after the band repair computed clean files, and every one of them was dropped on the conflict while the run log counted it written. NVDA's file for that night went on naming its immediate support twice. The facts file is what every number in a report is checked against, so it has to be what the store computes rather than what the first attempt computed. The assembler compares the hash of tonight's file with the stored one and, where they differ, deletes that row and inserts tonight's, so a re-run over an unchanged store writes nothing and keeps the change list beside each file, and a replaced file starts with no list until the detector writes it again. The alternative a later session might reach for, an update of the payload, would give the table a second updater, which the rule of one writer per operation forbids; the delete was nobody's. A re-run replaces only the newest night, because every stage computes over the store as it stands, so a night already followed by another cannot be recomputed by running it again.

**A name's facts file is assembled again for its night when an open fetches its fundamentals** A name's fundamentals are fetched when it is opened and its facts file is assembled by the night, so the first open of a name reads a facts file written before its quarter was stored. Over the fixture's KEYS that file held 55 facts and no revenue, margin or segment figure, every section quoting the quarter was refused for figures the file does not hold, and the segment commentary was left out in all three passes of 6.8's first comparison. So where an open's fetch stores a filing the night had not seen, the verb that runs the pass assembles the night's facts again and reads the night's changes again before the pass starts. A re-run replaces only a file that now differs, which is the opened name's (see: A re-run replaces a night's facts file where the store now computes a different one), and the change detector's column on that row is written again rather than left empty. It is arithmetic and no request, so it costs an open seconds rather than money. The alternative was to write from the night's file and let the sections needing the quarter fall back until a later night carried it, which makes a name's first pass its worst and spends on sections that cannot pass.

**Every researched claim must name a stored source document** The claim checker drops any that does not, and the report's source list is generated from what was stored. Numbers are checkable against the facts file; claims are not, so the only available discipline is provenance, and the failure mode of automated research is a confident wrong sentence that nothing else would catch.

**Transcripts are opportunistic, never a dependency** The earnings press release filed with the results announcement carries the guidance, the segment tables and management's prepared framing, and it is free. A verbatim transcript adds the analysts' questions, which is where management is pressed hardest, but only some companies file one. Where it exists it is read from the same free fetch; where it does not, the section is written without it. No provider is bought for it.

**A facts file carries the latest period of the segment table, and says which period it is** The assembler carries the newest period the archive supplies and names the months it covers, so a company whose newest filing is its annual report carries twelve-month segment figures rather than none. It carried only three-month periods until 8.0, and 6.11's production run found the consequence: MSFT's report for the year to 2026-06-30 files a segment table of twelve-month columns alone, so its facts file carried no segment figure and both sections that quote one fell back for a quarter. No fourth quarter is derived from the year and the nine months before it, because those nine months are not stored: the assembler reads the newest filing's own table and nothing else. A filing stored while the archive could not be read keeps its parts marked unread until a newer filing is stored, which is stated as the limit it is rather than repaired, since a stored fundamentals row is never updated and the next filing ends it within a quarter. Ruled at 8.0.

**The facts file carries a quarter's growth, its earnings against the estimate and the filing's own tables with their year-earlier columns** The facts file carries, from the newest filing's row, the quarter's earnings per share and the estimate for it, the quarter's growth on the same quarter a year before and on the one before it as the fetcher computed it, and from the filing's own tables the latest period and the same months a year before, each group's growth on it, and the tables of revenue by market, product or region beside the segment table, each named by what its title says it groups by (see: A quarter's growth is computed on its own row from the filings the provider returned) (see: A group's growth in a filing's own tables is computed from the columns the table states) (see: The filing's other tables of revenue by a grouping are kept beside its segment table). It carries the company's name the membership row holds as well, which every prompt lists and a pass reads to rank a document whose title names the company. The first live research pass, NVDA's, was refused for revenue up 106%, data center revenue of $89.0 billion up 117%, $27.0 billion billed to customers in Taiwan and earnings of $2.22 a share, and nothing it held carried any of them; a figure in prose has to exist in the facts file, so a section can quote what a release headlines only where the file carries it (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file). Every figure is read off the stored row and none is derived here, which is the assembler's rule; the growth figures are the fetcher's.

**The readings join the facts file for a member holding them, and nothing is added for one without** Ruled on 2026-09-27 with the fundamentals item. A member's facts file carries the night's readings of its reported quarters where its night stored any: the state and the quarter it was read from, the trajectory's two quarters of sales growth with the margin and its year-earlier figure, the record's counts, the quality's ratio and the valuation's multiple with its range, each as the stored reading holds it and none derived here, which is the assembler's rule. A member holding no quarter adds nothing, since absent is never a failure and a file of empty readings would read as figures (see: Four readings of a member's reported quarters are worked out every night by rules the measured split settled, and its state is read from sales and operating margin alone). No prompt changes: a written section may cite a reading because the file carries it, and a figure in its prose must exist in the file as any other must (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file). The readings join now rather than when a section is asked to cite them, because "What the numbers say" draws them on the page a written section sits on, and a section that could not quote what the page beside it says would be refused for the one figure a reader would check first. The fixture's night holds no quarter before its readings step runs, so its facts file does not change and no recording keyed on it moves (see: A re-run replaces a night's facts file where the store now computes a different one).

**A segment figure held for a period longer than a quarter is asked for by that period and refused where its sentence names a period of another length** A facts file carries the newest period a segment table files and names its months (see: A facts file carries the latest period of the segment table, and says which period it is), and that name was all that stood between an annual report's figures and a sentence calling them a quarter's. The segment commentary was still asked what each segment reported for the quarter, and the claim checker matches a figure against the values the file holds, so a year's figure written as a quarter's matched and passed, while the same figure written for its 12 months was refused for the 12. A wrong period is caught by the figure beside it failing to match that period's value (see: A claim is a sentence, and every sentence in a researched section names the document it rests on), and where the file holds a figure for a longer period alone there is no other period's value for it to fail against. So over a table that files no quarter the segment commentary is asked for the months and the end its figures cover, and told to name that period by its months and never as a quarter, a half or any other period. In every section, a figure whose every matching fact is a segment figure for a period longer than a quarter is refused where its sentence names a period of any other length: a quarter by its word, its adjective or Q1 to Q4, a half as a first or second half or as H1 or H2, and three, six, nine or twelve months in words or in digits. The months of a period the file names, the 12 of 12 months, are read as a period and not as a figure, as the twelve months in words already were. The quarter's ask stays word for word, because every recording of the section is keyed on the request it answers. The rule ends in three places. A year named by no count of months, the year to date and a half written alone are not read as periods, the last because it is as often a share. A figure that also matches any other fact passes under any period's name, because the match is on existence rather than attribution. And over a quarter's table no period's name is read, so a quarter's figure called a year's passes as it did. A sentence naming its own period beside another, as twelve months set against a quarter, is refused, which costs a retry and writes no wrong sentence. Nothing a night computes changes: the rule reads only a facts file carrying a period longer than a quarter, and read after the night of 2026-09-16 the operator's store held no fundamentals row and none of its 3,033 facts files carried a segment figure. Ruled at the 8.0 correction.

**The segment table is the first of the filing's segment reports to state revenue by segment** A filing renders several reports whose names mention segments, and the route reads up to four of those in the Details category in the order the filing lists them. It takes the first that states revenue under one of its groupings, and only where none does, the first that carries any figure by segment. It took the first carrying any figure until the first live research pass found the consequence on NVDA: that filing lists a narrative table first, carrying depreciation by segment, the counts of segments and a customer concentration share, and its schedule of reportable segments second, so the name's page showed depreciation and a concentration share as its segment table, with no segment revenue at all, and the segment commentary had no revenue to write about. Revenue is read off the concept a row is filed against, in the three spellings the captured tables file it under, and never off a label, which is the filer's own wording. The preference moves no other captured filer's choice, because the first table each of them takes already states revenue. The same narrative table wrote its share as percentages under a title stating millions, and a row whose every figure is written as a percentage carries the unit % and is left as filed rather than scaled, as a count of segments already was. A filing whose candidates state no revenue by segment costs up to three more page requests than before, on a pass the operator started and never on the night.

**The filing's other tables of revenue by a grouping are kept beside its segment table** A filing states revenue by more than its segments, and the figures a results release headlines are often in those other tables rather than in the segment table. The first live research pass found it on NVDA: its segment table states two segments, while the quarter's data center revenue of $89.0 billion, the figure its release led with, is in the table by market platform, and the revenue billed to customers in Taiwan, $27.0 billion, is in the table by region. The sections written that day quoted both, and the claim checker refused both, because nothing the store held carried them. So past the segment table the route reads the filing's segment reports whose names mention revenue or sales, at most three, and keeps each that states revenue under a grouping, in the segment table's own shape and on the newest filing's row beside it. Read by name rather than by table, which the segment table is not, because these are read only once the segment table is found and a filing naming no such report costs no further request: the two other captured filings name none, so their reads are the pages they were. The cost is a filer whose table by product sits under a name that does not mention revenue or sales, which is not read, and its row says the archive served no such table rather than that there is none.

**An answer that comes back empty or cut short is asked for once more** A model's answer that arrives and cannot be stored, with nothing in it or stopped at its budget, is asked for once more with the same request, and a section whose second answer is unusable too is left out with a reason a person reads on the name page: that the model's answer came back empty or cut short twice. What the model sent, its finish reason and its token counts, stays on the run log's row for the call. The first live research pass found both halves of it: the research model spent a call's whole answer on its reasoning and returned nothing for NVDA's cause of each large move, 3,156 of 3,157 completion tokens reasoning, and the pass left the section out on that one call with the provider's own sentence on the page. An answer spent on reasoning is not a property of the section, since the same request answers on another ask, where a refusal or a model that does not answer is, so those are not asked again. Once and not more, because an unusable answer is billed, and each ask is a call the spend cap judges and the run log prices under a stage of its own. The claim checker's one retry is a different thing and unchanged: it asks again with the reason a draft was refused, where this asks again for a draft that never arrived.

**Guidance is stored as management's own prose, and the facts file carries each figure the passage states as the claim checker reads it** Supersedes **Guidance is stored as management's own prose and never parsed into a figure**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but its last consequence. The archive stores the located passage, its heading, the exhibit and the date it was filed on, and the numbers section shows management's own words, as before. What changes is that the facts file carries every figure the claim checker reads in that passage, each as the checker reads it, a magnitude times the scale its unit word carries, named for its place in the passage and never for what it guides. The first live research pass found the want of it: NVDA's release guided the next quarter's revenue to $108.0 billion, plus or minus 2%, and its gross margin to 74.0%, and the two cases quoting management's own figures were refused for both, because a figure in prose has to exist in the facts file and the passage's figures were in no fact. The superseded entry refused a guided-quarter figure because a pattern that finds a range in one filer's wording misses it in the next, and reads a range as whatever the pattern takes it for; that stands, and nothing here says which figure is revenue, which is a margin or which is a bound. The claim checker's match is on existence and not on attribution, so a sentence that quotes the passage's $108.0 billion as revenue passes, and so would one quoting it as anything else, which is the cost the checker already states for every figure it reads (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file). A passage no heading located carries no figure, because nothing says which paragraph is management's guidance. (owes: The computed fundamental panel, and whether a fundamental state may fire a reason or gate a tranche)

**The local lane's scope is a setting, and whatever it holds is written for nothing** Section 13 assigns work per section rather than per pass, so which sections the local model owns is configuration. On a machine with 24GB of graphics memory that is the short-prompt work, and which of it stays local is what the fixture comparison measures (see: The fixture comparison moved the cause of each large move into the paid lane on this machine). Synthesis is not in it, because a model small enough to leave room for the whole evidence set on that card is the case already named as producing something fluent that says nothing. On a machine that can hold a large model with a long context, synthesis moves into the local lane by changing the setting, and a pass writes it for nothing from the documents that pass fetched. The overnight queue writes the part of the lane that rests on no document, and a synthesis section rests on documents, so moving one left does not put it in the night (see: The overnight queue writes the local lane's sections that rest on no document for every name, and the paid model is for names you get serious about). The build does not change between those two machines, and the fixture comparison rather than the memory figure decides whether a section should move. Supersedes **The local lane's scope is a setting, and the overnight queue writes whatever is in it**, moved below with its reasoning.

**A claim is a sentence, every sentence in a researched section names the document it rests on, and a window written in words is read as its number** The two decisions above say every number in prose exists in the facts file and every researched claim names a stored source, and neither says what a claim or a number is when a checker reads a paragraph, so a later session could draw either line anywhere and nothing would show it. A claim is a sentence. Every sentence of a researched section carries a citation, written as a D and the document's position in the section's own source list in square brackets, and the citation has to resolve to a stored row admissibility admitted. A position rather than an id, because a model copying a thirty-two character id into prose is a model given the chance to get one character wrong. The key under each figure is the one section held to the number rule alone, because the lane table says it is a fixed explanation over known values, and asking it to cite a document would be asking it to invent a source for arithmetic.

A figure is a rounding of a value the facts file holds, at the precision the prose itself states: half a unit at the last decimal the prose writes, scaled by the prose's own unit word, so $109.4 billion is a rounding of 109417000000 and $110 billion is not. A percentage is also tried against a value stored as a fraction, which is how the margins are held. The match is on magnitude and not sign, because the sign of a move is carried by the words around it and a signed match would refuse every "fell 3.2%" written from a stored fall, and it is on existence rather than attribution. Some numbers are not figures. A four-digit year standing alone carries no claim about a value. A quarter or half label, a fiscal year, a time of day and an ordinal from first to tenth say which period or which place, and a wrong period is caught by the figure beside it failing to match. The length of a window, the 200 of a 200-day average, has to be a number the facts file carries in the name of a figure or as a count it holds. A date has to be one the file holds. The S&P 500 and the twelve months a filing reports over are named as the two phrases whose digits and words are neither. A number written in words from eleven up, or a scale word standing on its own, is refused as a figure nothing can match, while one to ten are ordinary language and are not read, which is a stated cost: "two segments" where there are three passes. A window written in words from eleven up, "the twenty-day average" or "fourteen periods", is read as the number its words name and held to the facts file as a window in digits is. The local lane's key under each figure named the averages' lengths in words, and over the operator's store on 2026-09-18, 427 of the 455 reasons it gave for the 151 drafts of that section it refused were a number written in words, most of them an average's length the facts file carries in a figure's name; read this way, 90 of the 151 would have passed, and the rest state a value in words or a figure the file does not hold.

The checker writes two outcomes and two waypoints. A first refusal is `rejected` and the writer may try once more as the next version; a second consecutive refusal on the same as-of date is `fallback` and the section is left out with both attempts' offending text kept on their own rows. A researched section whose source list holds nothing admitted goes to `fallback` on its first check with the reason that no admissible source was found, because a rewrite cannot create a source. A version following a fallback is a fresh first attempt.

The reader was measured before anything rested on it, over the four captured articles, the two filed releases and 411 articles from one live news request, being 15,412 sentences and 11,727 figures, and two findings from that measurement are the reason this entry states its limits in numbers. The sentence reader holds: of the 15,412, 700 are three words or fewer and the ones read are real short sentences and page furniture rather than splits at an abbreviation. The measurement found three misreads and each is repaired and asserted: a percentage before the word week read as a window, a time of day read as two figures, and a strength of 2 matching 200% through the fraction reading. And the number rule is a strong test for a figure stated with decimals and a weak one for a whole number. Against AAPL's facts file of 38 values, 1,392 of the 11,727 figures, written by nobody with that file in mind, pass by coincidence: 1,301 of 7,939 whole numbers, 76 of 2,091 at one decimal, and 14 of 1,547 at two, some of which are AAPL's own figures. A model inventing "revenue grew 12%" passes where any value near twelve exists. That is not repaired here, because what would repair it is attribution, a figure tied to the fact it rounds, and the prompt that could require prose to name its facts is written at 6.6, which is where the first model-written sections exist to measure it against (owes: A whole-number figure tied to the fact it rounds, measured against written sections). (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file) (see: Every researched claim must name a stored source document)

Supersedes **A claim is a sentence, and every sentence in a researched section names the document it rests on**, moved below with its reasoning.

**A cause of a move rests only on a document published inside that move** The decision above requires every sentence of a researched section to cite a stored document that admissibility admitted, and says nothing about whether that document could support the sentence. For every other section that is the provenance discipline the corpus asks for. For the cause of each large move it is not enough, because the sentence is about a dated event and the document is dated too: a release published months after a move cannot be what moved it, whatever it says. The first pass a local model wrote at 6.6 showed it on real prose. Handed the Q3 release KEYS filed on 2026-08-18 and asked for the cause of each move, its first draft said the move of 2026-02-24 was driven by that release, and was refused only because the filing date it quoted happened not to be a date the facts file holds. Its retry cited the same release for eight moves and named no cause at all, and it was accepted, because every sentence cited an admitted document and every figure was in the file.

So a sentence of the cause section names a move by the session it ended on, and every admitted document it cites has to have been published on or after the session the move's change was measured from and on or before the session it ended on, for every move the sentence names. A sentence naming no stored move's session is refused, since there is nothing to check its citation against. The facts file carries each move's start for this, read as the stored session as many sessions before the end as the move spans, which is the close the annotator divided by; before 6.6 it carried only the end, and a span with one edge cannot hold anything. Both edges are inside, because a publish date carries no time: a release filed after the close on the start session is dated that session and is what moved the next one, which is the fixture's own case, and the same reading admits a document dated the last session and published after its close, which cannot have moved it. That is the cost of a date without a time, stated rather than guessed around.

Which document falls inside which move is worked out by code and handed to the model, rather than left for the model to compare. Told each move's span and asked to compare the dates itself, the model cited the August release for the five moves in February and March and wrote nothing for the one move it falls inside, which ran from 2026-08-17 to 2026-08-24; handed the pairs, it wrote one sentence for that move and the checker accepted it on the first draft. Comparing two dates is arithmetic, and code owns it (see: Code owns every number). A cause section whose admitted documents fall inside no move is not sent to the model at all, because asked anyway it wrote a paragraph explaining why it could not write one, which cost a call and the section's one retry.

One limit stays, and it is the one this rule cannot reach. The checker holds when a document was published, what it is, and every figure in the sentence; it does not hold that the document says what caused the move. The accepted sentence reads that the fall into 2026-08-24 was driven by record third quarter results, which is the model's reading of a company's own release about a move of minus fourteen per cent, and a release is where a company states its results rather than why its shares fell. What would carry a stated cause is reporting, which the research runner hands in from 6.8, and nothing here claims more than that the document was there to be read (see: A claim is a sentence, and every sentence in a researched section names the document it rests on).

**A dated calendar item's date rests on a document the sentence cites and falls after the night the facts were computed for** The number rule holds a date in prose to a date the facts file holds (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file), and the facts file carries the provider's next earnings date and nothing a document dates, so as 6.4 built it every date the dated calendar items exist to state was refused, which 6.6 found and carried to the checkpoint that would write the first of them. 6.8 wrote them. The research model's first draft copied four dates from the documents it was handed, two being investor conferences after the night and two being the days the announcements were published, before it; all four were refused, and its retry wrote a sentence saying nothing could be written. So a date in that section is held to the documents and to the night rather than to the facts file: it has to be a date an admitted document the sentence cites states, read by the reader that reads the prose so that the two forms compare, and a full date has to fall after the night the facts file was computed for, which the prompt now states. Reading such dates into the facts file was the other form, and it would have had code choose which dates in a document are events, which is the judgement the model is asked for, in a file whose rule is that everything in it is computed or copied with the filing date it came from. The first pass written under this rule had the section accepted on its first draft, naming the two conferences. A year standing alone is still not a date and is still exempt, so a sentence about a standard timeline set for 2029 passes, which is stated rather than guessed around.

**A research record is written and dated per section, not as a whole** On the current machine some sections are drafted locally overnight and others are written by the paid model days later when a name is opened, so a single as-of date and a single model name for the record would be false. Each section carries its own state, its own date and the model that wrote it, which is what lets the report state exactly what you are reading and stops the staleness judge treating a half-written record as complete.

**The overnight queue writes the local lane's sections that rest on no document for every name, and the paid model is for names you get serious about** After the nightly arithmetic, the local model works through every name in the index whose research is missing or stale, the names on tonight's list first in order of how many reasons fired, and writes the local lane's sections that rest on no document, at no cost (see: The key under each figure is dated by the night whose figures it explains, written for every name each night, and drawn only beside that night's figures). The night fetches nothing for a name: step 17 is carved out of the zero-model-call rule alone and reaches the local model and nothing that fetches (see: The night's zero-model-call rule bounds the arithmetic, and the overnight queue is carved out of it by name). A section that rests on documents rests on ones a pass fetched and stored, and the source store ties a document to a name through nothing but a section citing it, so the queue hands the writer no document and asks it for none of those sections. In this machine's lane what the queue writes is the key under each figure. What the company sells and the segment commentary are written by the pass an open starts, which fetches the company's own filing, so a name nobody has opened opens in the morning with its key written for the night and the rest written when it is opened. A name whose only outstanding work is a section resting on documents is not queued at all, rather than counted every night as a pass that could write nothing. Handing the writer the documents a name's stored research already rests on was the alternative, and it was not taken: a stale section rewritten tonight from documents older than the event that made it stale is dated after that event while reading nothing the event produced, so the judge would read it as research that stands (see: Research goes stale on an event dated after the section was written, and a news spike is dated by the session it began). Opening anything else, or pressing refresh on a name whose local draft is not good enough, runs a paid pass on demand, and the record stores which model wrote each section, so the page offers the paid model's rewrite of a local draft while saying only when each section was written (see: Every part of a page states where it came from and as of when, and a written section when it was written rather than which model wrote it). Supersedes **The overnight queue writes the local lane's sections that rest on no document, and the paid model is for names you get serious about**, moved below with its reasoning, the queue then working through the names on tonight's list alone and the report saying which model wrote what you are reading.

**The key under each figure is dated by the night whose figures it explains, written for every name each night, and drawn only beside that night's figures** Supersedes **The key under each figure is written for each night's facts file**, moved below with its reasoning. That entry wrote the key again each night for the names on tonight's list, and drew a key the queue had not written again beside the night it was written for. After the night of 2026-09-18 the operator's store held that night's key for 424 of 503 names, an earlier night's for 78 and none for one: 70 of the 78 were not on that night's list, so the queue never asked, and the checker refused both drafts of the other eight, so each of those pages drew a paragraph about figures the page above it no longer showed. Three things change. The overnight queue writes the key for every name in the index, the names on tonight's list first in order of reasons fired, which the queue's hour covers at the slowest pass 6.10 measured (see: The overnight queue is bounded by its own limit rather than the night's deadline, and starts no pass once the limit has passed). A key's row is dated by the night of the facts file it was written from rather than by the day it was written, because a pass run in the day writes it from the night before, and dated by that day it would be drawn beside the next night's figures as though it explained them. And a page draws the key only where that night is the session its figures are from; anywhere else the key's card names the night it was written for and says it is not drawn. This is still not a timer: the event is a night's facts file, which is the only thing the key is about (see: Nothing expires on a timer). It costs the local model a pass a name a night, and nothing is spent.

**The key under each figure is handed its facts as a reader reads them, rounded by code** The key explains figures to a reader, and it was handed them as the store holds them, `rsi14 = 47.740381`, and asked to round them itself. Over the 51 names whose first draft the checker refused on the night of 2026-09-18, the local model cut digits off where it meant to round, 271.9963 written as 271.99, and wrote a window in words or as RSI-14, which the checker reads as a figure; and a draft that passed had copied its figures to six places onto the page, 4.6 of them a key over 28 first drafts that passed. So the key is handed each fact as a reader reads it: an indicator named with its window as days before it, as the page labels the averages, money and counts in millions, billions or trillions with the word, a growth or a margin as a percentage to one place, and anything else to two places. Each reading is kept only where the claim checker matches it to the stored value, so a copied figure passes, and the checker still reads every figure in the prose against the file as stored (see: Code owns every number) (see: Every number in written prose must exist in the facts file). Every other section is handed the file as stored, since its recordings are keyed on it and none was refused this way.
