The sweep reads it by hand where a pull stored any print carrying a surprise, and where none is stored it reads the calendar's own year, today's members alone, flagged in its report as reading survivors, so the condition can then only be a variant. A print's reaction session is the earnings rule's: the report date's bar for a print before the open or of unstated timing, and the next bar for one after the close.

**The index's and the VIX's daily series are pulled beside the pulled bars, marked by their pull and read by no night** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-01 with the ideas' run, which tests switches reading the index against its own averages and the VIX against a level and its own past, and the store held neither series. The pull, `history-pull --market --from <date>`, asks the provider's historical endpoint once for each, `GSPC` and `VIX` under its index exchange, two requests at a weight of one whatever the span, and stores every session sent in `pulled_market_bar` with its open, high, low and close as sent, kept as text; each row carries the pull's run id and is removed whole with it, and a second pull adds only the sessions no earlier pull holds. No night, listing, score or page reads the table, for the reason none reads the pulled bars, so the night's cost and its one bulk request are untouched; a switch that came to read either series on the night would be a request a night and a sentence in the first hard rule, written at the freeze that adds it. A series the provider refuses, answers with no session or sends unreadable stores nothing and fails the pull, so a run reading it is not started over a series that is not there.

**The pulls behind the heavyweights and the context checks store into tables of their own and are read by no night** Ruled with phase 14's plan on 2026-10-03, which replays the sector heavyweights over the history and tests the drift's revenue growth over it, when the store held neither a company's value nor its sector as they stood nor any revenue as first filed. Four pulls, each on the operator's command and never by a night. `history-pull --companies --from <date>` asks the provider's fundamentals endpoint once for every name the index held on any session of the span, departed members among them, with a filter naming the fields it stores, at the endpoint's weight: the filer's CIK, the GICS sector, industry group, industry and sub-industry, the day a delisted company left its exchange, and every quarterly balance sheet's share count with the day the sheet was filed, into `pulled_company` and `pulled_shares`; each count is kept with the session the pull ran on, since the provider restates every count to that day's split basis. `history-pull --splits --from <date>` asks the per-name splits endpoint once a name and stores each split of the span in `pulled_split`, which is what puts an older close on a count's basis. `history-pull --sector-etfs --from <date>` asks for the eleven sector funds' daily series, one request a fund, into `pulled_market_bar` beside the index's and the VIX's, in the adjusted form a member's bars take. `history-pull --revenue` asks the SEC's archive, which is free and asks no key, for each pulled company's filer's whole facts, one request a filer at no more than its fair access allows, and stores every figure stated under each of the revenue concepts the reading names with the day it was filed, its form and its accession in `pulled_revenue`; a concept a filer never used is no figure and no failure. The archive's endpoint for one concept is not asked: it answers an empty set of dollars for some filers whose facts hold the figures, Coca-Cola's revenue among them, and the first pull of 2026-10-04, which asked it, left 11 such filers with no revenue; corrected in place by the 14.2 correction of 2026-10-04, since what this entry rules is unchanged. Each row carries its pull's run id and is removed whole with it, and a second pull adds only what no earlier pull holds. A name, fund or filer not served, not answered in time or answered in a form that cannot be read stores nothing and is named on the pull's row while the pull goes on, as the night's series fetch reads an answer it cannot read since 2026-10-03; the companies pull also names every company filing no sector, no count with its date or no CIK, and reads the fourteen moves of 2023-03-17 against the sectors the provider files. No night, listing, score or page reads any of the tables, for the reason none reads the pulled bars, so the night's cost and its requests are untouched.

**A company's value on a session is the newest share count filed before it times the session's close on the count's split basis** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03 as reading 4f of phase 14's plan. The heavyweights rank a sector's companies by value as it stood, and the provider's weekly market value starts on 2021-09-03 for AAPL and cannot rank a session before it. Every quarterly balance sheet the provider files carries the day it was filed, so a session reads the count of the latest quarter among the sheets filed before it; a sheet filed on the session itself is not read, since nothing says it was filed before the close. The provider restates every count to the split basis of the day it is asked, NVDA's for 2019-03-31 filed at 24.76 billion shares, forty times its count then, so the count is multiplied by the session's unadjusted close divided by what one share became at every plain split after the session and on or before the day the count was asked, which puts the two on one basis. The provider files a spin-off's and a merger's price adjustment as a split too, GE's 1,281 for 1,000 at GE HealthCare's spin-off, and neither changes a company's shares, so a split is read as one only where it is plain: its ratio lies within a ten-thousandth of whole shares for one share, as two for one, of one share for whole shares, as one for fifty, or of two whole numbers each at most ten, as three for two, within because the provider files CSX's three for one as 959,692 for 319,897. Of the 148 splits the provider filed for the history's names since 2018, 80 are plain; of the 76 plain ones the counts on both sides can judge, the provider restated its counts across 75, and the company whose it did not, APH at its two for one of 2024-06-12, reads at half its value before it, the residue of the provider's counts. Until the 14.2 correction of 2026-10-04 every filed split was read as one, which would have read GE before its spin-off at about four fifths of its value; what this entry rules is unchanged, so it is corrected in place. The adjusted close the bars store is not used: it takes out the dividends paid since as well, and would understate a company paying four per cent a year by about a quarter over 2019's sessions against a company paying none. A company with no count filed before a session has no value on it and is not ranked on it.

**A company's sector on a session is the GICS sector the provider files, with the fourteen moves of 2023-03-17 read by date** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03 as reading 4g of phase 14's plan. The heavyweights are chosen inside each sector, and the store's own sector labels are another scheme, missing for 184 of the 692 names the index held since 2018. The provider files each company's GICS sector, departed companies included, as of its last update with no dated history. After the close of 2023-03-17 GICS moved fourteen members of the index between sectors, eight from Information Technology to Financials, three from Information Technology to Industrials and three from Consumer Discretionary to Consumer Staples, as State Street's note of February 2023 counts them citing S&P Dow Jones Indices and LSEG's analysis of March 2023 names the three that joined Industrials; so a committed table names the fourteen by the ticker the index holds each under and its CIK, each reading the sector it left on every session to that close, and the companies pull reads the table against the sectors the provider files and names any filed elsewhere. The eleven sectors were formed after the close of 2018-09-21, so a session before 2018-09-24 reads none, as does a company the provider files under none of them. What remains is a company moved on its own for a change of business, which carries today's sector on every session: no dated history removes it.

**Companies are ranked by CIK with one listing held, the class that traded the more dollars over fifty sessions** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03 as reading 4e of phase 14's plan. GOOGL and GOOG carry one CIK and each is filed with the count of every class, as FOXA and FOX and NWSA and NWS are, so a rank of listings would put Alphabet among Communication Services' largest twice. A company is ranked once, by the listing that traded the more dollars over the fifty sessions to the session, each session its unadjusted close times its volume, the ticker settling a tie, and its value is that listing's. A listing the provider files no CIK for is ranked as a company of its own, since nothing ties it to another.

**A quarter's revenue is read as first filed, a fiscal fourth quarter being the year less its first nine months** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03 as ruling 5 of phase 14's plan. The provider's statements hold one latest value a quarter, so a figure restated after a report reads there as if it were known when the report was made. The SEC's archive keeps every filing's value with the day it was filed, departed filers included, so a quarter is read as the first filing stating it states it, and never as a later filing's comparative or restatement. Most filers state no fiscal fourth quarter in a filing of its own, the annual report stating the year, so that quarter is the year less its first nine months, both as first filed under one concept, and first filed the day the year was; of 484 prints of 16 names from 2019, 89 were stated in no filing of their own, mostly fiscal fourth quarters. Where one filing states a quarter under two concepts the earlier in a fixed order is read, the one stating the whole of the revenue before one stating a part of it: a bank's revenue net of its interest expense, which banks file theirs under, then revenue, then revenue from contracts with customers excluding and then including the tax assessed on it, then a utility's operating revenue, then the concept retired in 2018. A period is a quarter, nine months or a year by the days it spans, wide enough for a fiscal calendar of 52 and 53 weeks and apart enough that none is read as another.

**The night asks for the market series' daily closes once a series, and keeps them apart from the members' bars** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03 with the two variants that read a market switch every night the families are evaluated, the index's close against its 200-session average and the VIX's close against its close ten sessions before (see: The breakout and the earnings drift each register a variant listing only on nights its market switch is open, each family registered again whole and its records replayed), and on 2026-10-04 with the sector heavyweights' freeze, which reads each sector's return from its fund and each leader's beta against the index (see: The sector heavyweights freeze at their sweep's proposal, the proposal's three passing neighbours registered beside them as variants). The night's fetch step, after the day's bulk file, asks the provider's historical endpoint once for each series over the 400 days before the night's session, `GSPC` and `VIX` under its index exchange and the eleven sector funds as the listings they are, thirteen requests at a weight of one a night whatever the index's size, and stores each session no night has stored in `market_bar`, its open, high, low and close as sent and kept as text, with the run that wrote it. A session held keeps its first row, but a fund's: the provider adjusts a fund's closes for each dividend it pays, so a fund's held session the answer states at another close is written again from the answer, and a fund's return over a year is read over closes on one basis, as the sweep read the funds it pulled whole; the index and the VIX pay nothing, so their closes are never restated, and a switch reads each session as the night first stored it. A table of its own and not the bar store, because no series is a member's and nothing that reads a member's bars should find them; and not the pulled history, which no night reads and a purge removes whole. The family evaluator reads the index's and the VIX's closes on the store's own sessions for each registered rule's switches, so a day the VIX is quoted on that is no session is passed over as the ideas' run passed it, and the sector heavyweights' book reads the index's closes for each stock's beta and each fund's for its sector's return. A series the provider refuses, does not answer in time, sends nothing for or answers in a form that cannot be read stores nothing, is named on the stage's own row and stops nothing: a switch reading it is closed on a night it cannot be read, as a gate fails on an absent value, a heavyweights' rebalance reading it waits for the first night the store holds its close, and the night goes on. The first hard rule says so, and the night's cost still does not grow with the universe.

**The night's switches are aligned on the funds' own sessions, so a year's window is read from closes the members' year of bars never reaches** The 15.2 correction of 2026-10-08, on the operator's ruling that morning: "read it from the market series and pulled history, so it is filled every night, before the ledger reads it, with a test that it is never null once 252 sessions exist." The member reader aligned the switches' closes on the sessions the members' bars hold, and the bar store holds a year, so the half-year windows read and the year's never did: a year's window reads the close 252 sessions before the night, which a calendar of 252 sessions never holds, and `ijh_year` and `ijr_year` were null on every night the readings were stored. The funds' own series are kept apart from the members' bars and held over the 400 days before the first night that asked for them and every session since (see: The night asks for the market series' daily closes once a series, and keeps them apart from the members' bars), 279 sessions on 2026-10-08, so the switches are aligned on the funds' own stored sessions and read a year's window once those hold 253; a night that is no session of the funds' reads none, as before. The pulled fund series are not read: the night's own series already hold a year and grow a session a night, so a reading from the pulled table would serve only a store holding fewer than 253 fund sessions, which a store with no pulled history is as well, and it would make the night a reader of a pulled table against two decisions that say no night is (see: The index's and the VIX's daily series are pulled beside the pulled bars, marked by their pull and read by no night); where the operator rules the pulled series read behind the night's own, that is the ruling that narrows them.

**The pullback freezes at the base with its reward-to-risk floor raised to 2, and the ideas' run waits until phase 13 is finished** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-02, asking why phase 13 could not be finished before the ideas' run and choosing to finish it first. Phase 13's freezes had the pullback taken first at the starting point the ideas' run would report, which would have put two more pull requests and a run ahead of the three setups' sweeps. The base it freezes at is today's swing filter with its reward-to-risk floor raised to 2 and every other setting as it stands: as the counting correction of 2026-10-01 recorded it over the stored history, an edge of 0.099 against today's rule's 0.065, and 0.081 against 0.057 without the five largest results by size, and before that correction the same comparison read better in 7 of the 8 scored years and in 2024 to 2026 together, the one change those figures showed steady. The ideas' run's own two pull requests, the run and its figures, wait with the twelve-hour rerun and the two more ideas until phase 13 is finished; the index's and the VIX's daily series it reads are pulled already. A starting point the ideas' run proposes later is a new freeze of its own, on the operator's go.

**A setup family's sweep replays its own rule over the stored history and proposes the best edge among the settings meeting its floors, or brings the strongest where none does** Ruled with phase 13's plan on 2026-10-01, each family going live on provisional settings and swept after. A family's sweep reads the history the pullback's sweep reads, read-only, and replays the family's own rule over every scored session at each setting of a small grid around its provisional values, the market check at the live filter's floor closing every list, the nights walked in order as the page lists them, in the family's own order with its names a night and one open trade a stock, each trade bought at the close under the family's own exit and read as an edge over the same plan entered on every member that night. A grid holds tens of settings, so each is read whole, and the best edge among the settings meeting the pullback sweep's own floors is proposed, ties to the fewest dials moved from the provisional setting, with its one-step neighbours as its variants; no plateau is searched, since there are not millions of settings to fit. A family no setting of which meets the floors proposes nothing and keeps its provisional settings, listing on them as before, and its report brings the operator the five strongest settings it read, each with the floor it falls short of and by how much, and what could be tried next: each dial the strongest reads at an end of its grid, the floors they miss and the ideas' run's tests that fit the family, nothing on the page moving until the operator rules (see: No family on any index is set aside or hidden by a test result without the operator's word). The report states the test the family's checkpoints read from its freeze before anything is frozen, and nothing is registered until the operator's go.

Supersedes **A setup family's sweep replays its own rule over the stored history and proposes the best edge among the settings meeting its floors**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but that a family no setting of which meets the floors is set aside.

**No family on any index is set aside or hidden by a test result without the operator's word** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-05, reviewing phase 15's plan: "No family on any index is set aside, retired or hidden because of a test result without my explicit word. Where a sweep finds no setting meeting the floors, the card keeps listing provisional picks, marked provisional with one line saying the sweep found no setting that passed, and the result comes to me with the strongest settings found and what could be tried next. Apply this to every family on every index." A sweep's report is evidence the operator rules on and not a step the system takes by itself. A family whose sweep finds no setting meeting the floors proposes nothing, registers nothing, and keeps its provisional settings and its picks; the report states the five strongest settings the sweep read, each with the floor it falls short of and by how much, and what could be tried next, each sentence written by code from a fixed pattern. A sweep reads no ideas' run, so the report names the ideas' run's tests that fit the family and says it cannot tell which of them have run. The card's line, "Its sweep found no setting that passed the floors", is drawn once a command run after such a sweep has recorded its answer, built at 15.4, and goes with the family's next freeze or sweep; nothing else on the page moves until the operator rules. It holds for every family on every index, the S&P 500's among them.

**A sweep passes where a setting it read meets the floors, and a card says none passed only where every design's newest answer does** Built at 15.4 under the ruling above, which puts a line on the card once a command has recorded a sweep's answer and leaves what counts as passing and which answer a card reads to the build. A run's answer is passed where its proposal meets the family sweeps' floors after costs, at least 300 trades and an edge above nothing in 6 of the 8 years, or where a setting its second stage crossed meets them, and none passed otherwise. The crossed settings count because they are a result the operator rules on as a proposal is: the S&P 400's pullback search proposed nothing while one of its ten crossed settings met the floors, and a card saying its sweep found none would have hidden that from the page. Each run states its answer in its own folder and the operator records it with the worker's `sweep-answer` command, so a card reads only answers a person chose to record and never a run made to try the code. A card draws the line only where the newest recorded answer of each design the family sweeps says none passed, since the sector heavyweights sweep two designs and keep the stronger and one design passing is a result; and only where no freeze of the family on that index was registered after the newest answer, a freeze at the answer's own instant counting as after it, since the freeze is what was done on the answer. The line says nothing of the picks the card lists, which stay drawn whatever it says.

**Bars are never interpolated** A gap stops computation for that name and is reported as a gap.

**A gap is a session the exchange traded and the store does not hold** Detection is against the exchange's trading calendar rather than against the rows themselves, because a run of stored dates is self-consistent whatever is missing from it and a store holding four days of a five-day week looks exactly like a store holding four days of a four-day week. Two behaviours follow and they are different things: the fetcher refuses to store a series arriving with an interior session missing, and every computation over a name whose stored series has a gap stops and reports the gap's date. The state is derived on read from the stored sessions against the calendar rather than kept in a column, so nothing has to be brought back into step with the bars after a refetch fills the hole. Two readings of the calendar serve the two behaviours and the difference is not incidental. The fetcher compares an arriving series against the union of the session dates the names in hand already hold, which is exact over a whole payload and can say nothing over one series. A computed stage runs per name, so it compares that name's own stored sessions against the closure table pinned in code, which answers over one series and refuses a weekday outside its range rather than guessing. A stage handed a series the table cannot place proceeds and counts the name as unchecked on the run log, because a series nobody compared reported as clean is the shape of a check that passes by having no population.

**A session the night finds missing is fetched in bulk before tonight's** A night that does not run leaves every name short the same session, and the calendar the store can observe is the union of the dates its names hold, so a session every name missed is one it reads as a closure: the hole is invisible exactly when it is widest. So the fetcher compares the newest session the store holds with tonight's against a table of the exchange's closures pinned in code, fetches each session between them in bulk before tonight's, and stores all of them in one transaction. The provider serves an older session's bulk file as prices, which the phase 5 sign-off measured rather than assumed. The cost is one request per missed session, which grows with the nights missed and never with the names, so it is not a per-name call. A missed session whose file cannot be fetched, or carries none of the index, stops the night by name, because a gap stops computation rather than being counted across. The table covers a stated range and refuses a weekday outside it rather than guessing, since a closure read as a session is a day asked for and never answered, and a session read as a closure is a hole nobody fills.

**The stored series is adjusted** One price set per bar and it is the provider's adjusted one, which is why `bar` carries four price columns and no adjusted variant. An unadjusted series never diverges from itself, so storing one would make the corporate action checker pointless and leave the levels drifting away from every chart the operator compares them against. The cost is that stored history is restated by the provider whenever an action lands, which is the thing the nightly check exists to catch (see: Adjusted history is re-fetched after a corporate action).

**Adjusted history is re-fetched after a corporate action** Splits and dividends change adjusted closes, so stored history silently diverges from the provider unless a nightly check catches it.

**A member that returns after sessions in no index the night reads has its year asked for again whole** Found on 2026-10-05, the morning after 15.1's members run. BLDR, TAP and TTD left the S&P 500 on 2026-09-21 and the S&P 600's file first listed them on 2026-10-02, the first night the night read it, so their stored years stopped on 2026-09-18. The backfill asks only for a name holding no bar and the fetch stores only the night's session, so each would have held a gap of the ten sessions it was away, and every computation over it would have been withheld for a year. The night also saw no action on a name while no index it reads held it, so one may have landed unseen and the stored year may sit on an adjustment the provider has since moved, which is why the year is replaced whole, as an action's is, rather than the missing sessions inserted beside the old ones (see: Adjusted history is re-fetched after a corporate action). The corporate action check asks for it, being the one component that replaces a stored year: on the first night a member holding stored bars misses a session the exchange traded between the latest close of its spans and that night, read from its first stored bar within the year the night keeps. It asks once. A name the check has asked for since its span closed is not asked again, so a stretch the provider cannot serve costs one request and then stands as a gap, named as any gap is (see: Bars are never interpolated), and a refetch that fails marks the name suspect with its count starting that night, as a failed refetch after an action does (see: A suspect name is asked for again on the five nights after it is marked and weekly after that, and its own page, its row on tonight's list and the run page say so until a refetch succeeds). A name the loader moves between indices closes one span and opens the other on one session, so the night stored every session and it is not asked for, and a name holding no bar is the backfill's. It is the corporate action refetch's carve-out from the nightly rule's per-name request rather than a seventh, bounded by the names returning rather than by the index (see: The nightly run is arithmetic only).

**A member missing a session the exchange traded has its year asked for again whole, once for the newest session missing** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-05: "P's missing days: refetch the same way." P, the S&P 500's member Everpure, held no bar for 2026-09-18, 09-21 or 09-22, because the day's price file did not carry it around its ticker change, and every computation over it was withheld from 2026-09-23 on with nothing asking for it again, since the backfill asks only for a name holding no bar and the fetch stores only the night's session. On the morning of 2026-10-05 it was the one member of the 1,506 missing a session before its newest stored bar, and three more missing every session since they left the S&P 500 were the members back after sessions in no index (see: A member that returns after sessions in no index the night reads has its year asked for again whole). The corporate action check asks for the year whole, as it does after an action, since one may have landed unseen while the file did not carry the name (see: Adjusted history is re-fetched after a corporate action): for a member holding stored bars that misses a session the exchange traded between its first stored bar within the year the night keeps and its newest, tonight's included. Sessions after the newest stored bar are a shorter history and not a gap, so a name the file stops carrying asks nothing on the nights it is not carried and is asked for once a later bar makes them a gap. It asks once for the newest session missing. A name the check has asked for on or after that session is not asked again, so what the provider cannot serve costs one request and then stands as a gap, named as any gap is (see: Bars are never interpolated), and only a session going missing after the night it was asked for asks once more. A refetch that fails marks the name suspect with its count starting that night, as one after an action does (see: A suspect name is asked for again on the five nights after it is marked and weekly after that, and its own page, its row on tonight's list and the run page say so until a refetch succeeds), and a name an action, a retry or a return asks for that night is left to that reason. And a year the provider has not yet brought up to the newest session the night stored replaces nothing, whatever the check asked for it for, and marks the name suspect as an empty year does: a shorter year would drop the sessions the fetch stored and leave the figures stored over them ahead of the bars, which stops the night at the listings, as a night after a caught-up file short of a member showed over the fixture, whose recorded years end before its night. It is the corporate action refetch's carve-out from the nightly rule's per-name request rather than a seventh, bounded by the sessions the price file did not carry rather than by the index (see: The nightly run is arithmetic only).

**A suspect name is asked for again on the five nights after it is marked and weekly after that, and its own page, its row on tonight's list and the run page say so until a refetch succeeds** Ruled by the operator at 7.0, on the item the phase 6 sign-off carried: nothing outside the run page read a suspect name's state, so its name page, its row on tonight's list and its exported report drew its figures with nothing beside them, and a name whose retries were spent stayed so until another action landed on it, which for a split on a name paying no dividend may not come. A name whose refetch fails is marked suspect and asked for again on each of the five nights after the one that marked it, counted on its series state row, and a refetch that fails on a night an action lands on the name starts the count again. The five stand for the reason they were first ruled, that every refetch already carries the retries a transient fault needs inside one night (see: A feed is tried three times with a doubling backoff, and the night has a two-hour deadline it cannot move), and the operator confirmed them. Once they are spent, the name is asked for again on the first night whose session is seven or more days after the session it was last asked for, counting on in the same column, until a refetch succeeds or the name leaves the index, which ends the asking because the check asks only for the names the night stores. Weekly rather than never, because the provider is the only source of the series and a failure it has cleared is then cleared without anyone acting; weekly rather than nightly, because a failure that lasts costs one request a week rather than one a night, so the night's cost stays bounded by the failures and never by the index. No second provider is taken, and none is needed for the case this covers. Its figures are still computed over its stored series, and this does not withhold them: what changes is that everywhere a person reads them says so. The name page opens with one line saying its prices may not reflect a recent dividend or split, when the refetch was last tried and why it failed, above everything it draws from those prices, and the exported report carries that line because it is the name page's region. The name's row on tonight's list says the same beside the name, with the instant and the reason as its title. And the run page's stale and failed region keeps naming the name with when it was last asked for and why on every night the check does not ask for it. Nothing stored changes shape: the check reads the instant and the count it already writes, and the read API reads the row as stored.

**A name the backfill stored nothing for is asked for again on the five nights after and weekly after that, and its page and the run page say so until one stores its year** Ruled on 2026-09-18, when the operator was given this and the alternative below and asked for everything to be fixed. The backfill asked for every member holding no bar, on every night, so a name the provider returned no year for was asked for on every night it stayed a member: P_old, which the membership feed lists as the joiner of 2026-09-21 and the price file carries under another ticker, was asked for on 2026-09-17 and would have been asked for on every night after, one per-name request a night on a path whose carve-out allows a new member's backfill once. It is asked for on the schedule the operator ruled for a failed refetch instead (see: A suspect name is asked for again on the five nights after it is marked and weekly after that, and its own page, its row on tonight's list and the run page say so until a refetch succeeds): on each of the five nights after the first that asked, and after those on the first night whose session is seven or more days after the session it was last asked for, until a request stores its year or the name leaves the index. The alternative was strictly once, which leaves a new member with no prices for good after one bad answer. The run log is the record the schedule reads, since each night's asking is what a run log row records: the backfill's row names every name it asked for, and every member still holding no year with how many nights it has been asked for and when it is asked next, and the stage ends partial on every night one is left, which puts it on the run page's stale-and-failed region. The name's page opens with a line saying no year came back, on how many nights it was asked for, and when it is asked next.

**A member's reported quarters are fetched on the night after it reports, and asked for again on the five nights after and weekly after that until the quarter is posted** Ruled on 2026-09-27 with the fundamentals item, and the fourth carve-out from the nightly rule's per-name request, beside a new member's backfill, the corporate action refetch and the night's request for one report (see: The nightly run is arithmetic only). After the arithmetic has closed and before the overnight queue, the quarter fetcher asks the provider for the fundamentals of each member due, at ten weighted calls, and where the answer carries the quarter awaited, for three years of the member's daily closes at one more, from which the valuation's multiples are worked out. A member is due on the first night after a report the calendar holds for it; again on each of the five nights after that while the answer does not yet carry the quarter, and after those on the first night a week or more after its last ask, until an answer carries it or a later report replaces the quarter awaited; on its first night in the index; and once at the start, every member, at most 260 a night after the members reporting and waiting, in ticker order, so the fill takes two nights. A member the provider returns no quarter for is marked absent and asked on the same schedule, and again on the first night after its next report. This is the schedule of a failed refetch and of a backfill that stored nothing (see: A suspect name is asked for again on the five nights after it is marked and weekly after that, and its own page, its row on tonight's list and the run page say so until a refetch succeeds), for their reason: the provider is the only source, and a quarter it has posted since is then stored without anyone acting, at one request a week rather than one a night. It is asked the night after the report and read the night after that, so a member reporting on a Tuesday is asked on Wednesday night and read on Thursday: the night's deadline bounds the arithmetic, and a peak reporting night asking its members inside the arithmetic would add minutes to a night that already takes ten of its fifteen on this machine's disk. The step is bounded by its own limit of fifteen minutes and by the day's allowance, read before every ask, and never by the night's deadline, the way the overnight queue is bounded (see: The overnight queue is bounded by its own limit rather than the night's deadline, and starts no pass once the limit has passed), and a member left at either is asked on the next night. A night run again for an earlier session asks for nothing, since what it would store is today's answer and not that night's. The cost follows the reporting calendar rather than the index: over the 262 weekdays before 2026-09-27 the calendar held 1,994 reports, about four a member a year, a median of two a weekday and 57 on the busiest, so an ordinary night asks for a few members, a fill night about 2,800 weighted calls, 3.1% of the day's allowance, and the busiest night the calendar holds ahead, 88 reports on 2026-11-04, at most about 2,200. The run page states every ask with why it was made and what came of it, each member still waiting with the quarter awaited, the nights it was asked on and its next ask, and the fill as it stands.

**A member's estimates are raised where its current fiscal year's consensus earnings estimate stands above its level 30 days before** Read with the revisions variant on 2026-10-04 (see: The pullback's sector leaders' rule is retired and the analysts' revisions variant registered in its place). The provider's fundamentals answer files under its earnings trend the consensus estimate of a company's earnings a share for each period it covers, the current quarter and the next and the current fiscal year and the next, beside the same estimate 7, 30, 60 and 90 days before and how many analysts raised or cut it. "Estimates raised over the last 30 days" is read as the current fiscal year's estimate standing above its level 30 days before. The fiscal year rather than the quarter, because the year's estimate is the one revision studies and screens read, and a quarter's moves most in the weeks before its report for reasons a pullback's few weeks do not wait on; the level rather than the count of analysts raising it, because a count says how many moved and not how far, and an estimate raised by one large revision and cut by two small ones stands higher. Above and not at: an estimate standing level is not raised. The current fiscal year is the period the answer files as `0y` with the latest end, since the trend keeps each past period under the label it last carried. A member whose answer files no such period, files either figure as none, or that the provider did not serve, is read as not raised and its verdict names why, as a gate fails on an absent value. Each answer is stored the night it is asked for with its run, read back by a night run again, and never asked for a session but tonight's, so no history of it is kept beyond the nights the variant ran.

**Fundamentals are stored with the filing date they came from** Providers restate. Keeping the filing date is what makes it possible to know later what you knew at the time.

**Reported quarters are stored per fetch, so every quarter a reading reads shares one fetch's per-share basis** Ruled on 2026-09-27 with the fundamentals item. The provider restates earnings a share and share counts after every split, as of the day it is asked, so NFLX's quarter to 2025-06-30 reads 0.72 after its 10:1 split, and a quarter stored from one fetch set beside a quarter from another would put two bases in one ratio. So each fetch's quarters are a set of their own, one row per member per fetch per quarter over the twelve newest the answer carries, and a reading reads one set: the newest fetch before its night. What needs the whole answer is worked out at the fetch and stored on the row: sales growth and the operating margin against the same quarter a year earlier, the same growth a year before that, the four quarters' earnings a share, and the close on the first session after each report, from three years of closes asked for with the fundamentals. Tonight's multiple uses tonight's close brought to the fetch's basis by the stored bars' own adjustment, the fetch's own close for its session against the stored bar's for that session, so a split or a dividend since the fetch moves neither side. The rows are kept forever and never updated: about four fetches a member a year, twelve quarters each, write about 25,000 a year after the fill's 6,000. The report's own fundamentals and their snapshot are not these rows and do not change: a row written there is read once for its archive's segments and guidance, it carries no operating income and no operating cash flow and can never gain them, and written nightly it would freeze last quarter's segments on the new quarter's row and change every member's facts file (see: The numbers open on a snapshot of the newest filing with every other filed figure folded beneath it, and the report names no provider). A read of the quarters over the store's own year of bars rather than three years of closes was the alternative, which asks nothing more and reads too few quarters for the valuation on every member for about a year.

**The fundamentals row carries the analysts' ratings the provider files, on the newest filing alone** The company financials endpoint files, beside a name's quarters, what the analysts following it say of it: the mean of their ratings on a scale of one to five, their mean target price, and how many rate it at each of five grades from a strong buy to a strong sell. SCHEMA's payload column has named ratings since 6.1 and nothing stored them, so the name page had none to state, where the operator's reference report gives the analysts a section of their own. They are copied as the provider files them and sit on the newest filing's row alone, for the reason the ratios do: they move with every note an analyst writes, and the row carries the instant it was fetched at, which dates them. They are not carried into the facts file, so no section quotes a target price or a count of ratings: a target is an analyst's estimate, and a section setting it beside the company's own figures would read the one as the other, which nothing the operator has ruled asks a section to do (see: Code owns every number).

**A quarter ends on the date the company's own filing states** The company financials provider labels every quarter with the last day of the month it ends in, and a company whose fiscal quarters end on a weekday ends them up to a week either side of that: NVIDIA's second quarter of fiscal 2027 ended on 2026-07-26 and Apple's third quarter of fiscal 2026 on 2026-06-27, which the provider labels 2026-07-31 and 2026-06-30. A label is not a date. The row stored the label as the quarter's end, the facts file carried it as the latest quarter end, and the first live research pass, NVDA's on 2026-09-18, wrote that the quarter ended on 2026-07-31, which the claim checker accepted because the file held it, while it refused the company's own date in two other sections because the file did not. The filings archive's index states the period each 10-Q and 10-K covers, so a row's quarter end is the period of the periodic report ending within a week of the provider's label, a week never reaching the next quarter, and the label stands only where the archive was not read or indexes no such report, with the row's source naming whose date it is. (see: Fundamentals are stored with the filing date they came from)

**A quarter's growth is computed on its own row from the filings the provider returned** Each stored filing's row carries how its revenue, net income and earnings per share compare with the same quarter a year before, and how its revenue compares with the quarter before it, each as a fraction of the earlier figure. The first live research pass found the want of it: NVDA's two cases were refused for revenue up 106% on a year earlier, and the facts file held the quarter's revenue and not the year-earlier one or the change. It is computed by the fundamentals fetcher, where the margin already is, because a change between two filings is a property of the filing and the provider returns every earlier quarter in the one request the fetch already makes; the facts assembler's rule is that nothing in it is derived, and a screen computes nothing (see: A screen reads and renders, and computes nothing). The earlier quarters are the ones the provider returned ending within a week of a year and of three months before this one's end, which is the tolerance a quarter's own end is matched with (see: A quarter ends on the date the company's own filing states), so a fiscal calendar ending its quarters on a weekday finds its own. A quarter with no such filing returned, or an earlier figure of zero or less, carries no growth rather than a guessed one, and the year-earlier and quarter-before ends are stored beside the figures so a reader can see what each change was measured against. Rows are never updated, so rows stored before this carry none.

**A group's growth in a filing's own tables is computed from the columns the table states** Each group of the segment table and of the filing's other tables of revenue carries its change on the same months a year before, computed by the fundamentals fetcher from the two columns the table itself states, on the newest row beside the tables. The first live research pass wanted it twice: NVDA's two cases were refused for data center revenue up 117%, and a segment's growth is what a reader of the segment table asks next. The table's own columns rather than the filings the provider returned, because the provider files no figure by segment and the table states the year-earlier column beside the quarter's, restated where the filer restated it. The newest period is the newest quarter the table files, or its newest period where it files none, against the period of the same months ending within a week of a year before it; a group is named by its position as well as its label, because two groups of one table can share a label. A figure whose year-earlier value is zero or below grows by nothing, which leaves every cost the tables parenthesise without a growth rather than with a sign a reader would have to undo (see: A quarter's growth is computed on its own row from the filings the provider returned).

**Twelve filings are stored and five are shown** Section 4's numbers table displays five reported quarters and one guided quarter, and that is a display decision rather than a statement about what the store holds. The fetcher stores twelve filings, because the endpoint returns years of quarters in one call, so twelve is the same request at the same weight as five and the difference is only what is written. Twelve rather than five, stated so a later session reading the table does not narrow the store to it: a year-over-year reading costs five quarters before it produces one value, so five stored quarters yields a single comparison, where twelve yields eight, which is enough for a direction to be a direction, and twelve makes a guide-against-actual record a count worth having rather than four observations. Not more, because a company's business changes over five years more than its numbers do, so the earliest quarters describe a different company and widen every range they sit in, which makes today's multiple read as mid-range when it is not. Two details carry the rest of it. The window is twelve stored filings rather than a date range, so a company that missed a filing does not silently get a shorter window than one that did not. And where a later reading is computed over fewer than twelve, it carries its count, the way an indicator row carries its bar count: a reading over four quarters and a reading over twelve are different readings and a figure that does not say which invites the wrong one. What this buys is not built here (owes: The computed fundamental panel, and whether a fundamental state may fire a reason or gate a tranche).

**A calendar event is fetched once for the whole index, and the calendar holds provider events only** Four components declare a `calendar` read, `SCHEMA.md` declared no such table and nothing wrote one, which is contradiction E. The table is written by one component on the nightly path, fetching the whole index's dated events in one request over the horizon rather than one request per name, because the earnings date is needed nightly by the ladder builder and the shortlist builder and a per-name call there would break the rule the whole design rests on. It holds what the provider files and nothing else. A `kind` column carries provider event kinds only and a researched event never acquires one: the report's Dates section draws on the provider calendar and on dated items from the research record, and those two meet at the report surface, which is where section 4 already has them meeting. A researched date written into this table would be a claim with a source that the one instrument built to verify sources cannot see, and it would give a nightly store a second writer against the rule that a store has one writer per operation. The `kind` restriction is stated rather than implied because the first person wanting a product launch in the Dates section would otherwise add a kind for it, and the second origin would arrive without a decision.

**The calendar holds each member's own listing's prints, and each night's answer replaces what its window held** The earnings endpoint answers for every market in one payload, and a ticker the index holds can be listed on another exchange as another company or as the member's own shares, each reporting on dates of its own. The fetcher read every row by its ticker without the exchange, so over a copy of the operator's store on the night of 2026-09-23, 180 of the 503 members held two or more prints for one fiscal period: Quest Diagnostics carried four prints of another company's, with estimates below zero on dates between its own, 432 of the 2,428 earnings reaction rows were other listings' prints, and 15 of the night's 97 earnings soon fires read another listing's date or one the provider had moved, three of them a print dated the night itself. And the fetcher only inserted and updated while the date is part of the key, so a date the provider moved left its old one inside the window. The calendar now reads the index's own exchange alone, and each night's answer replaces what its window held: a row inside the window the answer no longer carries is removed in the same write, a row the window does not reach is left for a window that does, and an answer that stores no member's print removes nothing, since a provider answering nothing for the index is not one saying nothing is scheduled. Judgement: the member's own listing is the only one whose prints are the member's, and no field in the payload tells a foreign listing of the same company from another company, so none is read. The cost is a member whose next print only another exchange's listing carried: over the same night 22 members lost an upcoming date that way and read not on file until the provider files their own, one of them inside the twenty-session horizon, and that one was dated the night itself. Rows written before the correction are kept as written, and nothing in a row tells one read over the old calendar from one read over the new but its session, so earnings soon on a session before 2026-09-24, the first whose night runs the correction, counts toward no share on the run page (see: A calendar event is fetched once for the whole index, and the calendar holds provider events only).

**Every computed table's writer is its own deleter** Section 16 states one year, recomputed nightly and kept for the harness, over indicators, swings, volume profile, levels, ladders and moves, and `SCHEMA.md` gave Delete to nobody for any of them, which is contradiction A and contradiction H a third time in a third table. The four that exist split on grain and the split is what makes this due now: `indicator` keys on ticker, session and name and `swing` on ticker, session and direction, so both replace with the series and grow only as it does, while `volume_profile` keys on ticker, as-of date and band and `level` on ticker, as-of date and low edge, so both write a new set every night and replace nothing. At the bands a name this fixture averages, five hundred names put something of the order of three and a half million rows a year into a store nothing was declared able to reduce. `ladder` is the fifth and keys on as-of date, so it grows the same way, which is why the ruling lands with the checkpoint that adds it rather than at 5.2 where the last of the six arrives. Each builder drops the rows that fall out of the window on the night they fall out, which is what `BarFetcher` and `NewsPulseCounter` already do for the two tables that carry a stated retention and an owner to enforce it. A retention window nobody owns is a table that grows forever while the file says it does not.

**Your own listing history is kept forever** Which names reached which levels on which nights, and what happened next, is a record no provider sells and the only evidence that will ever settle whether a reason is worth keeping.

**News is one dated query, paged to cover the day, and attributed to names locally** The feed is queryable by date with no ticker, which was probed on the operator's key at 1.5 rather than assumed: one request over a date range returns articles for the whole market, every row carries a `symbols` array naming the tickers it is about, and the response shape is identical whether or not a ticker was asked for. So a night makes one dated query and fans the rows out to names in code, and the per-name cost of news is zero calls rather than one call per name. The alternative, a request per ticker, would put five hundred calls on the nightly path for the same articles and would break the rule that a night costs the same for fifty names as for five hundred. The row also carries the article text, so the source document a claim rests on arrives with the row rather than needing a second fetch, which is what makes admissibility judgeable at all without spending again.

One request does not carry a day, and the pages are counted. 2.5 measured it: one dated request for a single session returned exactly 1,000 articles, which is the provider's cap, over 3,232 distinct symbols. A count taken from a capped request is a count over whatever the cap happened to include, which is a figure over a mixed population and is not stated at all. So the query is paged until the day is covered, every page is counted, and a day that reaches a stated maximum page count refuses and says so rather than reporting a truncated count as a whole one.

The page count is a function of the day's news volume and not of the universe, and that is what keeps the cost rule true. One request already attributed 3,232 distinct symbols against an index of 503, so the feed is market-wide and adding names to the universe adds no articles to it. A feed that pages counts every page, and a page count that grows with the index is a per-name call; this one does not grow with the index, and `nightly-cost` measures it over two universe sizes to show that rather than to assert it. What the measurement does falsify is the arithmetic written before it: section 14's step counting today's articles said one request, and section 17's weighted-call row priced news at one request weighing 5. Both now read per page, with the driver named, and ten pages at weight 5 is 50 against an allowance of 100,000.

**A stored price is chosen and ordered by its value and never by the text it is stored as** A price is a decimal in code and text in storage, so the store compares two of them character by character: 95.10 stands above 106.47 because 9 is above 1, and a band at 87 sorts below one at 117 and above one at 1,140. Every query that ordered by a price column, or took the greatest or the least of one, was therefore answering by how a number is spelled. Found on 2026-09-19 by reading a name's page, which stated a move whose high was below its own low: over the index that night the two figures were wrong for 35 of the 508 names carrying a move, 24 of them showing a high under the low, and the bands of 48 of 505 names were listed out of price order. So a query hands back the rows and the choice is made where the values are decimals, which is selection of the kind the read surface already permits and never a computation over what is stored. The same holds for adding them: an aggregate that sums a price column is the store reading text as a floating point number, which is the storage form this corpus refuses in the other direction. `price-storage-form` reads every query in the shipped source and fails one that orders by, aggregates or compares a column SCHEMA marks as money, so the rule is enforced where the queries are written rather than remembered where they are read. The cost is that a query carrying its own order now hands that ordering to its caller, which is the price of the order being a price rather than a string.

**Each print's reaction is read from the nightly calendar and the stored bars, and the earnings drift is the one rule that reads it** The calendar is fetched for the whole index in one request (see: A calendar event is fetched once for the whole index, and the calendar holds provider events only), which makes it the bulk source the fundamentals ruling says would be a new decision, so this is recorded as a new decision and not a reopening of that ruling (see: No reading of the fundamentals fires a reason, gates a tranche or draws a panel). A print with no filed estimate shows its actual and says no estimate was filed, and is never counted as a meet. A print's reaction session is the one the earnings rule takes, read by calling the rule's own function one print at a time, so a print whose timing the provider left unstated is handled exactly as the earnings rule handles it and no pinned source is edited to share the reading. The earnings rule itself keeps its two prints. The operator ruled the record on 2026-09-23, one of two additions an SNPS report asked for, as display alone, and on 2026-10-01 ruled the earnings drift family, which reads it: "The night reads the day's surprises from the earnings calendar it already fetches; state what that adds." It adds no request and no fetch, since the calendar's one request already carries each print's estimate, actual and surprise for the year behind and the move annotator already stores each print's reaction session, so the family evaluator reads each name's newest stored print and nothing new is asked of the provider. The six reasons, the swing filter's gates, the ladder's plan, the rule versions and every candidate evaluator still read none of it (see: The earnings drift buys a beat with a strong reaction within five sessions, stopped under the reaction session's low).

**The numbers section draws the dividend the provider files from the newest filing alone, and nothing for a company paying none** The forward annual rate, the forward yield as the provider states it, the payout ratio, the ex-dividend date and the pay date, all from the provider's SplitsDividends part, as the analysts' ratings are read (see: The fundamentals row carries the analysts' ratings the provider files, on the newest filing alone). No yield is computed at tonight's close. A company the provider files no dividend for draws no dividend part at all, on the operator's ruling of 2026-09-24, since a line saying so told a reader nothing the missing figures do not. A row fetched before the part existed still says the part is absent and why, because that absence is the store's and not the company's, and a payer drawn as though it paid none would be a wrong result. It is presentation in an existing table, which the fundamentals ruling leaves standing.

Supersedes **The numbers section shows the dividend the provider files, on the newest filing alone**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but its line for a company paying none.

**A news article is stored once per member with its admissibility judged, and a label is never overwritten** The night's one news query already returns every article of the day with its title, link, source, the tickers it names and its text, and the counter kept only a count. It now keeps every article naming a member, one row per member per article keyed on a hash of its link, its text cut at the instruction's length with its length as delivered kept beside it, each judged for admissibility by the same test a research pass applies as it is stored, so the labeller sends only admitted articles and a refused one is drawn unlabelled with its refusal. Articles are kept thirty-one days, one past the labeller's window, and dropped by the counter that stored them, their labels with them. A label is inserted and never updated: the key is the article, the profile and the instruction's version, a conflict leaves the row as it was, and after a switch the new profile writes rows of its own beside the first's, so a page can say which model wrote what it draws. A fill run by hand on the operator's word stores the last days' articles at once, one dated query a day, so the window is full from the labeller's first night. Ruled on 2026-09-29 in the 12.6 ruling.

## Feeds and the wire

**A feed is unavailable when it does not answer, and wrong when it answers with something else** Section 18 promised a behaviour for a feed that is unavailable and never said what unavailable is, which left four different events sharing one row and no test able to induce it. Unavailable is a refused connection, a request that passes its timeout, a rejected request rate, and a status the provider uses for its own faults. Every one of those is a night that did not happen, and the answer is the one section 18 already gives: keep last night's bars, mark every name stale, still serve the app, and say so on the banner with the data date. Wrong is an answer that arrives and parses and is not the answer asked for. That is a night that would look like it happened, and the answer is the opposite: refuse the payload, leave the stored series exactly as it was, and name what was wrong on the run log. The two are kept apart because folding them would give a payload carrying yesterday's session the "keeps last night's bars" behaviour, which is right, and lose the part that matters, which is that something arrived and was rejected. One is a fact about the network and the other is a fact about the provider, and only the second is worth a person's attention in the morning.

**A feed is tried three times with a doubling backoff and the night's news query waits ninety seconds a try, and the night has a two-hour deadline it cannot move** Every feed interface accepts a cancellation token and nothing supplied one, so a night that hung on a socket hung until somebody looked. At most three attempts per request, the first two failures waited out for two seconds and then four, and each attempt bounded by a thirty-second timeout, but the night's news query's, which is bounded by ninety. Thirty seconds because the bulk file for a whole exchange is the largest thing the night fetches and is a few megabytes: far outside a healthy fetch and far inside the night. Ninety for the night's news query because the provider's time to answer it grows with the articles a page carries, a thousand of a whole market's day: the night's news step took 5 to 11 seconds for its pages on 14 of the 20 tries that answered from 2026-09-15 and 24 to 40 on the other six, and failed every attempt of a try on the nights of 2026-09-28 and 2026-10-05 once each and on the night of 2026-10-06 on its first two tries, while the same page asked for by hand during the last answered after 45.2 seconds and a page of a hundred articles after 5.7. So the provider was answering slowly rather than not at all, smaller pages take as long for the day as a whole, and ninety is twice the slowest answer measured. The operator ruled it on 2026-10-06, "if it fails timeout needs to increase to 90 sec", and "90 sec fix needs to be permanent. I dont know when it will be slow and when it will be fast". One name's news is asked a month at a time, answers well inside thirty and reads a window it does not answer in time as unread, so it keeps the standard bound, which a slower bound would stretch a research pass by. Three attempts because the failure a retry is for is a transient one, and a fourth attempt is a slower way of learning what the third said. What is retried is a refused connection, a timeout, a rejected rate and the provider's own fault statuses; what is not retried is a rejected key or a route the provider does not have, because a wrong key is wrong three times and the retry only delays the message that says so. The night as a whole carries a deadline of two hours, which is three times the forty-minute wall clock the limits table states for the three indices' members, so the deadline stops a night that has hung rather than one that is merely slow. At the deadline the night stops, names the step it was on, and exits non-zero. The operator ruled both figures on 2026-10-04 with phase 15's plan, "Wall clock 40 min, deadline 2 hours": the last four scheduled nights over the S&P 500's 503 names reached the close in 580, 616, 640 and 701 seconds, which scale by 1,506 over 503 to about 29 to 35 minutes over the three indices. The figures stood at twenty minutes and an hour from the operator's settlement of 2026-09-28 over ten nights of the S&P 500 alone, and the first five nights over the three indices settle the forty minutes.

Supersedes **A feed is tried three times with a doubling backoff, and the night has a two-hour deadline it cannot move**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but the bound on each attempt of the night's news query, thirty seconds there and ninety here.

**The night runs at a fixed UTC instant, moved only when a night finds the day's file not yet posted** Section 14 scheduled the night with Task Scheduler after the US close, which named one platform's mechanism and a local time, against two hard rules at once. The schedule is a UTC instant, registered with whatever scheduler the machine has, and the application neither knows nor asks which. The instant is the one `RUNBOOK.md` states, after the close at both ends of the year with a margin for the provider to post the day's bulk file, and it stands while nights succeed. It is not settled by measuring the posting hour, because a night that finds the file already posted says only that it was there by then: five such nights bound the hour and settle nothing, and the obligation that waited for them had a trigger whose firing could not answer its own question. What moves the instant later is a night refused because the day's file carried none of the index at the scheduled instant, which is the provider not having posted yet. That refusal is on the run page's stale-and-failed region the next morning, and the session is not lost, because the next night fetches it as a missed session. The instant is a setting in the runbook with its reason beside it and not a figure in this entry, because a number written into a decision is the shape that gets cited later as though it were settled.

Supersedes **The night runs at a fixed UTC instant set after the provider posts the day's bulk file**, moved below with its reasoning. The line was missing when this entry was written, which the phase 5 sign-off reviewer found: the old entry was moved and nothing here said which it was.

**A night on a day the exchange did not trade fetches nothing and exits clean** The exchange trades on weekdays less its closures, so a night scheduled every day falls on a day with no session every weekend and every holiday. Until the phase 5 sign-off such a night asked the provider for the day's file, was refused at the fetch because the file was dated the session before or held none of the index, and exited 1: the scheduler recorded a failure every weekend, and a missed Friday caught up by a Saturday night was rolled back with the Saturday's refusal. The night now asks the closure table whether its session was traded before it fetches anything, and on a day that was not, it writes one run log row naming the day, fetches nothing, and exits 0. The row carries an outcome of its own rather than `ok`, which under the closing stage would say the night's counts were recorded, and rather than a failure, because nothing is owed on such a day. Registering the task on weekdays only saves the weekend's rows and is the runbook's business; the night does not rely on it, because a holiday falls on a weekday. A weekday the table cannot place is still refused, since the table exists to refuse rather than guess.

**A feed that pages counts every page, and a page count that grows with the index is a per-name call** Every feed interface counts its own requests and none said what happens when a payload arrives paged. The count includes every page, so a feed that starts paging reports the truth and the limits row stops reading as one. The limit itself is restated to say what it always meant: the count does not grow with the number of names. A payload that pages because it is large is bounded by the payload; a feed whose page count rises as the index does is a per-name call wearing a different name, and it is a defect under the same rule rather than a new exception to it.

**The night's cost is counted in weighted calls against the stated daily allowance** `RUNBOOK.md` states the provider's allowance as 100,000 weighted calls a day and gives the weight of each endpoint, and no code read either figure, so nothing stopped a night spending a month's allowance and nothing would have said it had. Each feed declares the weight of the endpoint it calls, the run log records the night's weighted total beside its request count, and a stage that would take the running total past the allowance does not start. It is denominated in calls rather than in money because this provider sells a flat subscription with a call allowance, which is a different shape from the research provider whose cap is money and whose rate doubles at peak. The local stop exists because the provider's own stop is a rejected rate, which arrives as an unavailable feed and loses the reason.

**The outward-request scan names the files that may hold a client rather than dropping the patterns** `nightly-cost` scans every shipped file for `System.Net.Http`, `HttpClient`, `WebClient`, `HttpRequestMessage` and the socket constructor and reports zero, which is true only while no feed reaches the network. The first live feed turns a green check red, and there were two shapes available. Deleting the patterns leaves a check that reports the absence of a scan as the absence of a client, which is the under-reporting failure `coverage-reported` exists to catch, arriving inside the check that carries the nightly path's own claim. So the exemption is named instead: one file per live feed, listed in the check, with every other shipped file still scanned and the scanned count floored where it is rather than lowered. The list is asserted to hold exactly the feed implementations, so a file added to it that is not a feed fails rather than passes quietly.

**The on-demand feeds are resolved in one place, as the nightly feeds are** The night's six feeds are one record resolved once from configuration, live against a capture, refusing a fixture folder that does not exist rather than resolving to the live path and refusing to fall back to a capture on a live run. The research work reaches five more surfaces, being the fundamentals endpoint, the filings archive, the search tool and the two model lanes, and not one of them is nightly, so none belongs on that record. They get a record of their own, resolved the same way and for the same reason: what a pass ran against is a fact the run log has to carry, and a client constructed inline is a choice nobody can read afterwards, which is where the night's own source selection lived until 2.6 and is why there was no selection. The overnight queue is the one research surface a night reaches, and it takes the local lane through this record rather than holding a client of its own, so a night's model calls are attributable to the same resolution every other call it makes is.

**A paid model on the OpenAI format is asked to stream its answer, because a connection silent for a minute is cut** From 6.7 the feed asked for each answer whole. The provider then sends the response's headers at once and nothing more until the model has finished reasoning, so a long answer is a connection that carries nothing for as long as the model thinks. Measured on the operator's store on 2026-09-30: of the 135 paid calls it had recorded, three failed with the connection closed while the answer was being read, GM's two cases on 2026-09-25 and BDX's risks and its two cases' retry on 2026-09-30, each after 60 or 61 seconds, and none of the 132 that answered took longer than 58. The calls run one after another, so it was not the rate of asking. Something on the way closes a connection idle for a minute, and every answer that needed longer was lost, after the provider may already have billed it. The feed now asks for the answer streamed with its counts at the end and reads the pieces into the response the provider would have sent whole, so one parser reads both, a provider answering whole is still read whole, and nothing a recording is keyed on changes. The answer streams as it is written, reasoning included, so the connection is never silent for long. A stream that ends before the provider says it is done is an answer cut short and is not stored. Claude's own messages interface is asked whole as before: no call on it has run past a minute, and the research job asks DeepSeek alone (see: Report generation asks DeepSeek alone, and no research setting names a Claude profile).

## Levels and the plan

**The biggest moves of a year are distinct episodes rather than overlapping windows of one** A move is measured over one session or five, and a five-session window slid by one day is four fifths the same five days. Ranking every window by size and taking the largest eight therefore answers with one rally described eight ways: it ranks near the top once for every day it can end on, and the rows it takes are rows no other move of the year can have. A window is taken only where it shares no session with one already taken. Measured on 2026-09-20 over the 508 names the store held: 507 of them had fewer than eight distinct episodes behind their eight rows, the mean was 3.8 and the fewest was one episode filling all eight, and 78 names named moves in one direction only where the year held both. The circles on the twelve-month picture stacked on consecutive sessions for the same reason, so a reader could not tell them apart, which is how the operator found it.

**Levels come from four sources: swings, moving averages, retracements of the last two swings, and heavy volume shelves** The first three are prices the chart has visited or arithmetic on them. The fourth is where the shares actually changed hands, which is the only one of the four that says how many people have a reason to act at a price. A support band holding a large share of the period's volume is defended by holders at their cost; a range above the price where almost nothing traded is one price moves through quickly. A level builder without a volume profile can state neither.

**A heavy volume shelf creates a band of its own and also strengthens one it coincides with** Section 9.1's first step collects every price band holding an unusually large share of the period's volume as a candidate, and a candidate is what a band is merged from, so a shelf with no swing, average or retracement anywhere near it is a band in its own right. Its last step adds a point where a band holds a shelf, which is the ranking half and is a different question from whether it creates. Both readings were available and they produce different band sets, so the level builder is a different component depending which is taken. Creating is the one the four-sources decision already names: a source of levels that could only rank would be three sources and a modifier. It is also the reading the volume argument requires, because the range where almost nothing traded is a statement the report can only make about a band that exists on volume alone. The cost is that a name whose volume is evenly spread produces no shelf band and loses nothing, while a name with one dominant shelf gains a band no other source would have found, which is the case the whole profile was built for.

**The volume profile accumulates over the same sixty sessions as the level window** One window and not two. The shelf is a candidate the level builder merges alongside swings and retracements, and those come from the last sixty sessions, so a shelf drawn from a different span would place a band on volume from sessions no other member of the level table can see. The profile's own share figures would then be shares of a period that is not the period the rest of the chart is drawn from, and two numbers on one page would silently mean different things. The moving averages remain the stated exception, reading the full stored year, and they are exempt because an average is a price rather than a share of a period and the limits row already says so. Written as a decision rather than left to the builder because `SCHEMA.md` gives the profile an as-of date and price bands and never says over how many sessions volume is accumulated, so nothing would have caught the two windows drifting apart.

**The volume profile is twenty bands across the window's own range** A fixed count of bands spanning the window's high to its low, rather than a fixed price width. The count has to be the same for every name because the shelf threshold is one number for all five hundred: "at least twice its even share of the period's volume" means a fifth with five bands and a fortieth with forty, so a threshold stated once and read against band counts that differ per name is a threshold that means something different for each of them. A fixed width in money has the same defect from the other end, putting three bands on a forty dollar name and three hundred on a four thousand dollar one. Twenty is the number section 15.5 already describes when it says a support band can hold a fifth of the period's volume in a twentieth of its price range, so the figure is read out of the document rather than invented beside it, and it is also what a histogram of twenty rows can be read by eye where two hundred cannot.
