The band edges are rounded to the four places the stored prices carry and every band takes two adjacent members of one array of twenty-one edges, so the bands tile by construction rather than by two roundings agreeing. Written as a decision because `SCHEMA.md` gives the profile a band low and a band high and never says how many bands there are, and section 9.1 settled the window without settling this, so the shelf threshold rested on a number no document held.

**A name with fewer than sixty sessions gets no volume profile** Not a profile over what it has. `share_of_period` is a fraction of the window's total volume and the column carries no denominator, so a row computed over twenty sessions sits in the same table as one computed over sixty and reads identically. That is a figure over a mixed population, which `.claude/rules/writing-tests.md` already forbids stating at all, and it would reach the level builder as a shelf whose share is a share of a different period. The absence is visible instead: the name has no rows and the run log's detail carries the count of names skipped, so a night where a backfill failed shows as a jump in that count rather than as a set of quietly shorter periods.

**A retracement is drawn between the last swing high and the last swing low** The window's most recent high and its most recent low, in whichever order they occurred, with the five fixed fractions taken across the distance between them. The phrase in the architecture, "the retracements of the last two swings", reads equally as the last two swings of any kind, and that reading produces a retracement between two highs or between two lows, which is a fraction of the distance between two prices that no one retraced. A retracement is a partial reversal of a move, so it needs the two ends of a move, which is one high and one low. Where the window holds no swing of one of the two kinds, the name has no retracements that night rather than retracements drawn from a substitute, because a substitute end is an invented move.

**A volume shelf enters the merge as one price, the midpoint of its band** Section 9.1's first step collects "every price band holding an unusually large share of the period's volume" as a candidate, and its key says a candidate is a single price with a reason to matter. Those two sentences describe different things and `SCHEMA.md` settles which: a member carries one kind, one price and one date, so a shelf reaches the merge as one price like every other candidate. The midpoint rather than an edge, because neither edge is where the volume is and choosing one would put the band systematically low or systematically high. The alternative, contributing both edges, is not academic: it makes the resulting band exactly the shelf's own width whenever nothing else is near, and a shelf's width is the window's range divided by twenty, which is an artefact of how the profile is cut rather than anything the price did. Measured on the fixture, two of the three names have a shelf narrower than half a typical day's move, where the two readings agree, and the third has one twice that, where they do not. The shelf's identity is not lost by this: the member record says it is a shelf, the strength step reads the coincidence, and the profile itself still carries the band's edges and its share.

**A member's date is the session its evidence occurred on, and a figure recomputed nightly has none of its own** A swing carries its own session, a touch carries the session that reached the band, and a retracement carries the later of the two swings it is drawn between, that being when the move it retraces ended. A moving average and a volume shelf are figures over the window rather than events, so they carry the as-of date because `SCHEMA.md` requires every member to have one. The consequence is the whole reason this is written down: section 9.1's strength score adds a point for "a member from the last twenty sessions", and an average dated as-of is always from the last twenty sessions, so awarding the point on date alone would hand it to every band holding an average, which is every band near the price on any night. A point every band scores ranks nothing. The point is therefore awarded on the three kinds whose evidence occurred, and the two computed nightly cannot win it.

**A touch is one visit to a band, counted only where the band holds no swing or retracement from that visit, and it never creates a band** A session that reached a band is evidence for it, which is how the report cites dated lows that are not swings in their own right, and it is evidence once. A visit is a run of consecutive sessions whose low or high lay inside the band, dated by the session it arrived on, and it is one touch; a visit holding a session the band already has as a swing or a retracement is that evidence already and adds none. How long the price sat in a band is not how many times the band held it, and counting sessions scored a band by the time the price spent in it: on the operator's store on 2026-09-25, HUM's band from 382.11 to 419.97 held 68 members, 52 of them touches, and scored 71 where the next strongest scored 5, and its band from 353.69 to 355.81 scored 5 on three sessions because the sessions of its two swing lows were counted again as touches. Over the thirteen stored nights the median name's strongest band scored 47, and 14 under this rule and the width below. A touch is still added after the edges are fixed, so it can never create or widen a band. Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-26.

**A band is no wider than two typical days' moves, and a chain that would be wider splits at its widest gap** Two candidates closer than the merge distance join one band because two prices that close cannot be told apart, and that holds between neighbours and not between the ends of a chain of them: joining each candidate to the one before let a chain of close pairs reach prices anyone trading them tells apart, 5.87 typical moves at the widest over the thirteen stored nights, where 6,705 of 39,288 bands were wider than one. So a chain wider than two typical days' moves splits at its widest gap between neighbouring candidates, the lower of two equal gaps first, and each part again until it fits; a width exactly at the maximum fits. Two and not one: measured over the thirteen stored nights on 2026-09-26, the swing filter listed 38 names at no limit, 2 at one typical move, 34 at two and 38 at three, because the swing trade's stop sits at its setup band's low edge and its target at the nearest resistance band's, so a narrower band set a nearer stop and a nearer target; at three the widest chains, HUM's of nearly three among them, stood whole. At two the list changed on most of the thirteen nights and a first tranche moved on 2,040 of 6,535 name-nights, which is the reach of the defect rather than a side effect of its repair. That the swing trade's risk is set by how wide its setup band merged is carried rather than changed here (owes: The swing trade's stop ruled apart from how wide its setup band merged). The width is proposed. Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-26.

**A tranche is a support band, and its stop is a daily close below the low edge of the next band beneath it** Close rather than touch, because a name moving six percent on a normal day dips through levels intraday every week, so a stop on a touch is taken out by noise and a stop on a close is taken out by a decision. The next band down rather than just below the tranche, because anything closer than a typical day's move is inside the noise.

**A moving average is a level on the chart and never an anchor for a tranche** A short average follows the price by construction, so a band anchored only on one sits near the price regardless of what the price has done. Measured on the worked example's own bars, the close was within half a typical day's move of its 20-day average on seventeen of thirty-one sessions, so a tranche placed there would make the first condition fire about half the time on nothing having happened. Averages stay on the chart because a reader wants to see them; they simply do not decide where to buy.

**A support band whose low edge is below the price carries a tranche, even when the band contains the price** A band the price is already trading in is exactly where the first step of a staged purchase belongs, so eligibility turns on the low edge rather than the whole band. In the worked example this is the band from 900 to 940 with the price at 933 inside it. The band keeps its full width, because a fill can land anywhere in it.

**The trailing stop is the higher of the band beneath and the last swing low, and a stop inside a support band moves to that band's low edge** In a range the stop is the range floor, which is the low edge of the next support band beneath the tranche; in an uptrend it trails, and what it trails to is the higher of that band and the most recent swing low beneath the tranche; in a downtrend there are no tranches. Kaminski and Lo, Journal of Financial Markets, 2014, show a stop reduces expected return under a random walk and can add value where returns trend, so one stop rule for both cases is wrong in one of them. Taking the higher of the two rather than the swing low alone is what keeps the trailing half meaningful. Read literally, trailing the last higher low puts the stop wherever that low happens to be, which on a name that has run a long way is far below the band beneath and is looser protection than the range rule gives, and a trailing stop that can sit below the range floor is not trailing anything. The committed fixture holds the case: MSFT's third tranche has its last swing low a hundred points under the band, and the literal reading would put the stop there. A stop that lands inside a support band, above its low edge and at or below its high edge, moves to that band's low edge, and again where that edge sits inside another, because a close the band still holds would end a trade the band was placed to protect, and a stop on a band's top edge is taken out by a close inside it. The operator ruled it on 2026-09-26, reading the ladder's stops over the pulled history: of 2,815 names its first tranche listed over the 1,996 sessions from 2018-10-16 to 2026-09-25, 878 had the stop inside a band, every one of them a trailing stop on a swing low that is the band beneath's own member, and moved, 2,353 names list with none inside a band. "It is section 10's intent applied exactly: a stop sits below a band, never inside one. It is also the same rule the list's trade now uses, so the report's plan and the list agree about where a stock's stop is." The other reading, the chain the width cap split taken as one zone with the stop below it, was rejected because it leaves 15,208 plans with no stop at all. A swing low in the gap between two bands still sets the stop, so the rule trails where there is room between the bands to trail; the committed fixture's three trailing stops, KEYS's first and third tranches' and AAPL's second's, each sit inside the band beneath and move to its low edge.

Supersedes **The trailing stop is the higher of the band beneath and the last swing low**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but that the stop is the higher of the two wherever the swing low sits.

Supersedes **The stop rule depends on the trend state**, moved below with its reasoning. The code has run this rule since 4.5 and no spec said it: figure 10.1 read that the stop trails the last higher low, the superseded decision said the same, and the phrase "higher of" appeared only in two records, a source comment and an expectation note. A decision is changed only by another decision, so until this one the rule the code ran was a rule the corpus did not hold.

**An exit closer than two typical days' moves is listed but not traded** A move smaller than two ordinary days is inside the noise, so the level is worth naming on the chart and not worth paying a spread to act on.

**The plan places a position and never sizes one** The sizing paragraph is worked arithmetic from a risk budget the reader chooses.

**A pick's card sizes its plan from the operator's own settings, and the report's plan still sizes none** The plan above is the report's, and the report still sizes no position. A pick's card sizes its plan from the account's size, the risk a trade and the position cap the operator keeps on the account page: the shares the risk buys over the stop's distance, rounded down, and no more than the cap allows, and a holding with no stop an equal share of the account across the most holdings its book can hold, which the operator asked for with the phase 16 plan on 2026-10-05. Where the settings are not kept, the card draws its plan in prices and risks with a line linking the page, and an exported report carries no part of it.

**A pick's concentration line counts the operator's open trades in its sector across the three indices and names them** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-05 as the fourth ruling of the phase 16 plan. A position's sector risk does not stop at its index: over the stored year the mean correlation of daily returns between two S&P 500 members was 0.254 within a sector and 0.104 across sectors. So the sixth line of a pick's card counts the operator's open taken trades in the stock's sector on the S&P 500, the 400 and the 600 together, the stock's own left out, and warns at two or more. It is the one figure on a card reading every index, and its words name the three, so every figure still names its index (see: Every page reads one index at a time chosen under Universe, and every figure names its index).

**The second book is keyed to a dated event, and an earnings print is the only kind on file** It has its own setups, its own stops and a horizon of days, and it never merges with the position book, because holding a momentum entry through a print is the thing the separation exists to prevent. What is new is what the book is keyed to. Section 10 said the second book is keyed to dated events, of which an earnings print is one, and left where the other kinds come from to be settled here. They come from the calendar and from nowhere else, so the kinds the book can carry are the kinds the provider files, which today is the print. A dated item a research pass found is a claim with a source and lives in the research store, and it reaches the report's Dates section without ever reaching this book. Supersedes **The earnings trade is a second book**, whose name said earnings where its own body already said the separation is about carrying a momentum entry through a date.

**A ladder row is written for every index member every night** Carrying the trend state, and a plan that states why it is empty where it is empty. Section 18 says a name with no eligible band produces no ladder and the report says why, and a row that is absent says nothing: an empty plan stating its reason and an absent plan are different objects and only the first is readable on a screen. The same shape as the listings row written for every name whether or not a reason fired, and for the same reason. Tonight's label is compared against last night's by the trend-changed reason, so a name whose band went ineligible has no yesterday unless a row was written on the night it had none, and the transition into and out of a downtrend is exactly the one that changes the whole plan. The cost is one row per name per night, which is the order the listings table already accepted.

**The trend state is read from the averages and the last two swings, and a name that cannot be classified says so** Uptrend when the close is above the 50-day average, the 50 is above the 200, and the most recent swing low is above the one before it. Downtrend is the mirror, close below the 50, the 50 below the 200, and the most recent swing high below the one before it. Range when every input is present and neither holds. Not classified, with the reason recorded, when an input is absent: fewer than 200 bars, so there is no long average, or fewer than two swings of the kind the rule reads. The fourth value exists rather than a default because the corpus already refuses this trade elsewhere: an average of sixty bars labelled 200-day is a lie, and a name labelled range because nothing could be measured is the same lie one level up. A name that is not classified carries no tranches, so the label decides a plan and cannot be a guess. The swings are read as of the date through the same reader the level builder uses, so nothing sees a swing confirmed later.

**The trend classifier returns its label to the ladder builder** The catalogue said it returns the label to the facts assembler, which is built at 5.3, a phase after the screen that draws the label. It writes nothing, `LadderBuilder` stays the single writer `SCHEMA.md` already declares for `ladder`, and the facts assembler reads the label off that row when it arrives. A classifier that wrote would give one label two homes the moment a ladder row also carried it, which is the second statement of one fact this corpus has spent three phases removing.

**A tranche's condition is the pattern the price has made at its own band** Figure 10.1 names a fixed list of four and says nothing about which one a given tranche gets. Each is decided by the band's own recent history, tested in a stated order so that exactly one matches: available now, where the close is inside the band; a failed breakdown back into the zone, where a session inside the lookback closed below the low edge and the close is back at or above it; the first close back above the zone after a dip, where the close is above the high edge and a session inside the lookback traded into the band; and the second day after a shock once the first day's low has held, where a session inside the lookback moved more than the shock multiple and the band is the one it landed on. Exactly one, asserted in both directions rather than left to the order a dictionary happens to yield, because a matcher keyed on the first thing that fits answers about everything that fits. A tranche whose band the price has made none of the four at reaches the zone, which is the absence of the four rather than a fifth pattern: a band the price has not come back to still needs a label, and the alternatives are an empty condition or a tranche the plan does not state. The lookback of 10 sessions and the shock multiple of 3 typical daily moves are proposals under the same rule as the setups below.

**Each traded exit sells an equal fraction of what is held, and the top of the ladder trails** An exit is a fraction of a position that already exists, which is scaling out rather than sizing, so it needs no capital figure and does not touch the rule that the plan places a position and never sizes one. Equal fractions over the traded exits, because any other split is a preference dressed as arithmetic, and the skipped exit takes none. The top of the ladder is a trailing rule rather than a price, which is what figure 10.1 already says and is why the fractions divide over the traded exits rather than over five.

**The volume profile claims a price region at the resolution of a day's range** What the profile says is where volume sat as a price region no finer than a day's range, never a price within a session, because each day's volume is spread across that day's high and low and the store holds no finer grain. A published caution holds that a daily volume profile is not a volume profile, and it is about a feature scored against a forward return; narrowing the claim is what puts this outside it. A session whose range exceeds three of its previous session's typical moves is kept in the profile and counted rather than set aside, since setting one aside would move bands and the plans built on them, and the count is what a reader is owed instead: over the 503 names that are members on 2026-09-14 and hold a profile as of it, 85 of 30,168 sessions exceed three typical moves, 532 exceed two and 24 exceed four, with 81 names holding one such session, 2 holding two and 420 none. The multiple is the event book's shock multiple, so the two move together. Ruled at 8.0.

**A moving average may widen a band that a tranche sits on, and may never anchor one** A band is eligible for a tranche only where one of its members is something other than a moving average, which `LadderSeries` enforces and `ABandAnchoredOnAnAverageAloneCarriesNoTrancheAndIsStillAStop` holds over a constructed band set. An average inside an eligible band still sets an edge where it is the outermost member, because a band keeps its full width and a fill can land anywhere in it. The two sentences are not in conflict once said together: what the earlier decision refuses is a band that sits at the price because an average follows the price, and a band carrying a swing, a retracement or a shelf does not. The size of the question was measured before it was answered, over the 223 zones that fired on 2026-09-15: an average widens 35 of them, and 3 would no longer hold the close if a zone were the range of its non-average anchors alone. Taking those edges away is a ladder rule change rather than a reading, so it is registered at 8.6 as the named version `zone edges from non-average anchors only` and scored against this one. Ruled at 8.0.

**A researched precondition attaches to a tranche and never creates one** A tranche can reasonably wait on something no compute component can see, a guide that does not imply a revenue decline being the case that started this. The ladder emits the tranche with its price condition, and a research pass may attach a fundamental precondition carrying its own source, which the claim checker treats like any other claim. The alternative, a fifth condition kind the ladder builder evaluates, would need the ladder to read the research store, which its matrix row does not carry and should not: a nightly component that reads research is a nightly component whose output changes when a paid pass runs. Nothing about a tranche's existence, its price, its stop or its order depends on the precondition; it is a sentence attached to a tranche that already exists.

**The event setups' triggers are proposals until resolved setups can score them** Three setups keyed to the print, each with a trigger, an entry, a stop and a target, and every figure in them is a proposal stated as one on the page, the way section 11 states the six reason thresholds. Twelve numbers invented at a planning pass and written as rules would be twelve constants nobody could later tell from measured ones, and this corpus has no basis to correct them until something has scored a setup. A breakout before the print triggers on a daily close above the immediate resistance band's high edge on volume above the fifty-day average, with the print inside the horizon and at least three sessions away; it enters at that close, stops on a daily close back below that band's low edge, and targets the next resistance band's low edge. A flush after the print triggers where the session after the print closes down by more than two typical daily moves and inside a support band; it enters on the following close once that session's low has held, stops on a daily close below the flush session's low, and targets the flush session's own open. A gap up after the print triggers where the session after the print opens above the immediate resistance band's high edge and closes above its own open; it enters at that close, stops on a daily close back inside the band, and targets the next resistance band's low edge. The book's horizon is 10 sessions from the trigger, and a setup unresolved at that cap is unresolved rather than anything else (owes: The event setups' triggers calibrated from resolved setups).

## How the picks improve

**A setup is scored from its entry, and a target reached before the entry is never a win** A setup starts on the first close at or below its entry zone's top edge, and a target reached before that close is `never entered`: its own column, never a win and never in a rate. The filler scored from the listing night from 5.5 until 8.1, so a name that ran straight up from the listing counted as a win for a purchase nobody could have made at the price the plan named, which is 105 of 147 stored wins over the operator's store on 2026-09-16. A first close that is at or below the zone's top edge and through the stop is entered and stopped on that session, because a fall through the whole plan is a trade that happened and lost. The cap of 63 sessions is counted from the listing night rather than from the entry, since the plan being scored is the one stored that night and it ages with it. Entry is read on closes, as the stop and the target already are, because a daily bar does not say what order a session's prices came in and reading a fill from a session's low would credit an order the store cannot show was filled. Ruled at 8.0, built at 8.1.

**An outcome once decided is never rewritten, and a setup still in play is scored with its plan scaled by its listing session's adjustment factor** A setup's outcome, being a win, a loss, never entered or unresolved at the cap, and a five or twenty-one session outcome with its return once that horizon has matured, are written once and never again. From 5.5 the filler wrote every row of every listing on every night, and two things moved a row that had already been decided. The corporate action refetch restates every stored close before an ex-date while the plan stored on the listing night keeps the scale it was written at, so a two-for-one split scored every setup listed before it as a loss against a stop at twice the price, and a dividend moved a close across a stop, a target or the entry zone's top edge by the share it paid; the actions feed carries dividends as well as splits, and 56 names in the operator's store on 2026-09-15 held a year the refetch had restated. And the bar store keeps one year, so once a listing's own session left it the close its returns and its entry are measured from was gone, and the next fill wrote its matured horizons back to not yet matured and scored its setup over a series starting later. A setup still in play is scored against its stop, its first traded target and its entry zone's top edge, each multiplied by the listing session's stored close over its raw close. That factor is one on the night a listing is written, because nothing has restated the newest session a night stores, so on a later night it is the restatement since, and it puts the plan on the scale the stored series now has, ex-date sessions included. A listing whose close the store no longer holds has nothing to measure from and stays as it stands. The base rate beside a decided row is the one figure the filler writes over it, because it is the universe's figure over every name-night and moves as the window fills. A later change to how a setup is scored applies to the setups decided after it and never to the record already written, as a changed rule opens a new window and keeps the old one. The entry rule's rewrite of every stored outcome was ruled to run first, on the night of 2026-09-16. Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-16, built at the 5.5 correction.

**Each setup family's correction for luck counts its own rules alone, at most nine a family** The maximum family size is a convention and no count of resolved setups measures it, so it is decided rather than carried: with five worthless candidates the chance that one clears an ordinary test is about 23 per cent, with nine about 37 and with twenty about 64. Eight was ruled at 8.0 as the size at which the correction stays a correction rather than a bar no candidate could clear, and the operator raised it to nine on 2026-10-02 to register the night's best three beside the pullback's eight, choosing "Raise the cap to 9 (Recommended)" over retiring a variant: each rule's share of the level falls from 0.05 over eight to 0.05 over nine, about 0.0056, a bar every rule can still clear, and nothing measured was lost, since no night had scored the freeze's rules. Each setup family counts its own rules alone, as ruled with phase 13's plan on 2026-10-01 and binding from the freezes: each family is a rule of its own with its own record and checkpoints, tried over its own question, so the divisor, the distinct trials Holm's first step counts and the graph the level passes along are each read over the candidates registered under the family's own evaluator, the pullback's being the swing filter's. The six live reasons are a family of their own, registered by section 11 before the first listing night, and registered candidates are the other; a candidate promoted to live joins the live family, which restarts the window the live reasons are measured over. It supersedes the two entries that set eight, both now under "Previously decided".

**A verdict tests a reason's wins against each of its setups' own break-even at a corrected threshold** The null hypothesis is that each resolved setup wins with the probability its own plan demanded, which makes the wins a sum of independent draws with different probabilities, a Poisson binomial. The exact tail is computed from the stored break-evens, because they are stored per row and the arithmetic over a few thousand rows is a dynamic program, and code owns every number the page shows. The binomial at the mean break-even is the conservative approximation and is named rather than used: it is at least as large in the upper tail for a count at or above the mean plus one, which is where a verdict that clears its bar sits, and a test asserts that bound over constructed setups in that range. The threshold is 0.05 divided by the family the reason belongs to, one-sided, since the question is whether a reason beat the bar its own plans set rather than whether it differed from it. The independence the test assumes does not hold, because listings cluster by sector and by date, which is why the minimum is 250 resolved setups over at least 60 distinct listing sessions rather than 250 rows however they arrive. Ruled at 8.0, built at 8.5.

**A reason's share, verdict and both floors are counted over the resolved setups that set a bar** A reason is scored over the setups it produced that resolved as a win or a loss and carry the break-even their entry close set, and over no others. An unresolved setup is left out because it is neither right nor wrong (see: An unresolved setup is never a win), and a loss that entered and stopped on one session is left out because it set no bar, so there is no probability its plan demanded for the test to draw it at. The count against the minimum, the sessions counted against the night floor, the share, the mean break-even, the exact tail and the verdict are all taken over that one set; a page draws a verdict exactly where the test was run, and the count it draws beside the share and inside the dashed outline is that set's count and says so. The row's resolved column still counts every win and loss, since it is a count and not a rate. The alternative was to open the page on every win and loss and test the subset that set a bar, which draws a share beside a denominator it was not computed over and, wherever the two counts straddle a floor, opens the page on a verdict the test withheld. Counting an unresolved setup as a losing draw would punish a reason for trades that never finished, and counting the session it arrived on would let a verdict show over nights on which nothing resolved. Built at the 8.5 correction.

**A stored break-even is measured from the close the setup was entered at, as a percentage beside the figures it is compared with** The bar a setup had to clear is risk over risk plus reward, and 8.2 measures the risk from the close the setup was entered at rather than from the entry zone the plan named, for the reason the return is measured from there (see: A setup is scored from its entry, and a target reached before the entry is never a win): a plan filled at the bottom of its zone risked less and stood to gain more than the same plan filled at the top, and one bar over both scores a trade nobody took. It follows that a setup with no known entry close carries no break-even, which is a setup nobody entered and one that entered and stopped on a single session, so the break-even and the setup horizon's return are present together and absent together, and the share of setups that cleared their bar is stated over the setups that set one rather than over every resolved row. Five of the 93 setups the operator's store had resolved on 2026-09-16 set no bar. It is stored as a percentage because the two figures it sits beside on that row and the share it is tested against are percentages, and a number written in one form beside a number written in another is compared wrongly once and then believed. That is a different form from the plan arithmetic the name page draws, whose break-even is a fraction because it is a number in a plan rather than one set against a rate, and the two are told apart by the column carrying the word and by this entry. Built at 8.2.

**An entry is admitted only inside the plan's own range, and a plan with no entry zone takes its whole range as its zone** A setup's entry close is admitted only where it sits at or above the plan's stop and at or below the lower of its entry zone's top edge and its first traded target, on the listing night and on every session after it, and a plan carrying a stop and a target and no entry zone takes that whole range as its zone. It narrows the entry rule over two shapes that rule's words did not address, and supersedes nothing (see: A setup is scored from its entry, and a target reached before the entry is never a win): a plan with no entry zone, which 8.1 scored from its listing close wherever that close sat, and an entry zone whose top edge sits above the target, which admitted a listing close between the two. Each wrote an outcome carrying a return and no break-even, because the share is undefined for an entry outside the range, and that breaks the pair the break-even is stored as (see: A stored break-even is measured from the close the setup was entered at, as a percentage beside the figures it is compared with). A listing close above the target is a target the price has already passed, which the entry rule calls `never entered` on any later session, so admitting it on the listing night alone scored one close two ways. Refusing such a plan was the other choice, and a refusal inside the forward-returns stage stops the night over a shape a stored plan can take. No stored plan takes either shape: read immutable from the operator's store on 2026-09-18, at schema 27, every one of the 1,928 listings whose plan carries a stop and a first traded target as text carries an entry zone's top edge at or above its stop and below its target, so the rule admits exactly the closes the entry rule admitted over every stored plan. Ruled at the 8.2 correction by the session correcting it, and not by the operator.

**The model writes no entry or exit proposal, and the loop scores only what code computes** The loop's eighth checkpoint stored the model's own entry and exit proposal beside the computed plan and scored it on the same rule, and that asks a model for prices, against the hard rule that no model is ever asked for a number, a date or an estimate. It is dropped rather than carved out. Two readings were available and neither is taken: a model choosing among computed levels by identifier, which is a ranking dressed as a choice and still puts a model in the path a purchase is decided on, and a decision superseding the rule for a stored second opinion never displayed as a fact, which would leave the rule true everywhere except the one place a number decides something. The proposal would also have been the slowest thing in the loop to earn a verdict, since it exists only for the name-nights a research pass reached while the rest of the loop scores every name-night. Ruled at 8.0.

**A condition is judged against the break-even its own plan demands** Every listing carries an entry, a stop and a target, so it states the hit rate it needs to be worth taking. The score for a condition is whether its setups reached the target before the stop more often than that. A forward return over a fixed window mostly measures whether the market rose, which is why the run page shows those against the universe base rate; this score needs no such correction because the bar moves with each setup.

**A rule's record is drawn under the rule's own heading, beside its reason or on a pick's card and never as the stock's own** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-05 with the phase 16 plan: a pick's card draws the record of the rule that picked it, and from 16.3 the operator's own record of the trades taken under it, under the rule's own heading, naming the rule and its index and never the stock, with the line "This is the rule's record over every stock it bought, not this stock's chance." A record is a property of the rule across every stock it ever picked, so a figure set beside a ticker with nothing saying whose it is would be read as that stock's chance of going up; set under the rule's heading and saying whose it is, it tells the reader what the rule picking the stock has done before. A reason's record keeps its place beside the reason: once it has enough resolved setups it shows the share that reached the target before the stop, the number resolved, and the break-even those setups demanded, always as the three together, which tells you which of tonight's reasons has a track record while you are reading. On a card a rule's record is drawn from the rules' minimum on and only its count against the minimum before, with the line that its settings were chosen on the same history so its figures read high, and no figure of a record is drawn as the stock's own.

**The record column stays empty until it has earned a number** Below the minimum the display shows how many setups have resolved against how many are needed, and no rate. That will be the state for the first year, because a list of about fifteen names a night accumulates a few hundred setups a year per reason. Lowering the minimum so the column fills sooner would put a figure on the screen that looks like evidence and is not, and a figure on a screen gets acted on.

**An unresolved setup is never a win** A setup that has reached neither its target nor its stop within the time cap is reported in its own column and excluded from the rate. This is the same rule as unexamined never counting as a pass in the harness, applied to trading outcomes.

**Every trade the live list recommended is shown, and their share waits for the minimum the reason records wait for** The Past picks screen follows every name the swing filter passed on a night it listed, from its first night, on the plan that night's trade gate read, and draws each one whatever became of it: its buy, stop and target, whether it is open or how it finished, the sessions it was held and its result in multiples of the risk it took. A trade is a fact about one recommendation, so there is no minimum below which it is hidden. The share that reached its target is different: over a handful of trades it looks like evidence and is none, and a figure on a screen gets acted on. So it is drawn only once the trades decided at their target or their stop reach the minimum the run page's reason records wait on, in resolved setups and in listing nights, read from the same constants, and always beside the share those trades needed to break even and the average result. A trade that ran out of time is counted and drawn and never in the share (see: An unresolved setup is never a win). A candidate's plan, a member the filter did not pass and an evening before the swing filter are not trades the list recommended and appear nowhere on it (see: Candidate conditions are registered before they are scored, and scored in shadow before they are shown). The operator ruled the screen on 2026-09-27.

**Candidate conditions are registered before they are scored, and a candidate's picks are shown on the Run page while its outcomes wait for a look** The register is an append-only table naming each candidate, its rule, its test and the date it was registered, with a stated maximum family size. Rows are never updated or deleted: a retirement is a new dated row naming what it retires, which is the same convention this document uses for superseded decisions. What pre-registration needs is that you cannot quietly change what you registered after seeing the result, and append-only with an insert timestamp gives that without keeping a fact outside the store. A candidate is evaluated and stored nightly like a live condition. The operator ruled on 2026-09-28 that its picks, the stocks it would list on a night, are shown: drawn in the Run page's comparison of tonight's picks and counted in its account of how the system learns, and nowhere else, never on tonight's list, on a name's page or on Past picks. Its outcomes, any figure computed from how its trades turned out, stay withheld until a look reads them, and a candidate is promoted only by its test at a look. Showing its picks does not weaken the test: the test reads outcomes at looks fixed when the candidate was registered, by the rule stated then, and a pick seen moves no threshold and no look; a reader who changes a candidate after watching its picks registers a new one, which is a new trial the lifetime count adds (see: Holm's level passes between the candidates by a graph fixed when they are registered, and its first step is 0.05 over the distinct trials read at a look or still running). What a reader gains is to see early which questions are really being tested: a version that picks what the live list picks on nearly every night can never show a difference. Nothing is adopted on the strength of the idea, and nothing is chosen after seeing which way the data went.

Supersedes **Candidate conditions are registered before they are scored, and scored in shadow before they are shown**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but that a candidate appears nowhere until its evidence clears the bar: its picks are shown, and its outcomes still are not.

**The significance threshold is divided by the family size, and the divisor is shown** Running several candidates and keeping whichever looks best is the standard way a self-improving system makes itself worse while appearing to learn. Showing the divisor beside the verdict means a later reader can tell how hard the test actually was.

**Adding a candidate later restarts the clock** Registering a new candidate after the family is set inflates the family, so the correction changes and affected verdicts are recomputed. Without this the register could be widened quietly until something passed.

**A registration names an evaluator the code carries, and its version is the pin of every source its evaluation runs through but the catalogue** A registered candidate carries the name of an evaluator, the parameters it will be run with, and that evaluator's version, so the row says what will run rather than describing it. A rule written only in prose is re-implemented by whoever scores it, and what they implement is whatever they read the words to mean, which is the thing pre-registration exists to stop: the registration and the thing tested come apart with nothing able to say they have. The version is a pin rather than a number somebody raises, because a number is a second statement of the same fact and drifts the first time a parameter is tuned in a hurry, where a pin cannot. It is taken over the evaluator's own source and every source that decides a value the evaluator reads or the verdict it returns, from a stored bar's text to the verdict: the money and statistic crossings, the indicator arithmetic and the stage that stores its readings, the builder that hands an evaluation a name-night's readings and names the session before, the shadow column that runs it, and the shared evaluator code that reads a registration's parameters; a family rule's evaluator pins its own rule's files and the stage that runs it besides. A sector heavyweights rule's evaluator is evaluated in its book and never over a listing, so its version pins every file its book's evaluation runs through, read off the compiled code, and the indicator arithmetic whose averages its trend gate reads, and none of the sources the listings' candidates share: a book is not replayed, so a version those sources moved would restart its record though nothing it reads had changed. The catalogue that lists the evaluators is not among them, on the operator's ruling of 2026-10-03 with phase 14's plan: "Take CandidateEvaluators.cs out of the shared evaluation sources in the same move, so one restart at 14.5 is the last a new family causes." It hands each name to the class carrying it, whose own source the pin covers, so it decides no verdict; while every evaluator pinned it, a family added to it moved every evaluator's version and restarted every registered rule's record. It left the sources in the move that added the sector heavyweights' evaluator, which moved every version once more, every family rule replayed and its result stated (see: A family rule registered again keeps its record from its first registration where a replay of its stored nights reproduces every trade, and restarts at the change otherwise). They are listed beside the version in `CandidateEvaluator`, and `register-append-only` holds the list to the pin, to every shipped file that computes a reading or runs an evaluation, and to the types an evaluation calls. What decides only whether a name-night is evaluated at all, being the register's standing rule, the gap stop and the staleness read, is outside it, because a change there produces a skip or a different set of name-nights, each named on its row, and never a verdict under a rule the register does not name. A pin over the evaluator's own file left the reading of the session before, the parse of a registration's parameters and the arithmetic of the readings free to change what a registered rule fires on while the row still matched; the ladder rules' code version covers every source a replay runs through, as the operator ruled on 2026-09-16, and a registered candidate is held to the same reach. The pin is taken with line endings normalised to LF and any leading byte order mark removed, so the same code pins the same on both machines and on a runner that checked the tree out with either ending, and less the one line carrying the version, because a pin of a file including its own pin never settles. What this costs is that every change to any of those shared sources but a comment or a blank line moves every evaluator's version (see: A comment or a blank line moves no pin, and every other change to a pinned source does), and each standing candidate is skipped and named as a failure on its stage's run log row until it is retired and registered again under the version the code carries, under the same name or another. That is the cost being bought: a row naming code that has moved is a row naming a rule nothing runs, and a night that evaluated it would write evidence about a different rule under the registered one's name. Ruled at the 8.3 correction, and narrowed to leave the catalogue out on 2026-10-04.

**A candidate stands by the last row naming it, and a name retired and registered again stands once** A candidate stands registered by the last row naming it, a registration naming it or a retirement naming what it retires, ordered by instant and then by row. Reading the first row would make a retirement permanent in a table that can only be appended to, and a set of every name registered less every name retired does the same by another route: a name retired and registered again is in both sets, so it dropped out of the divisor, the run page's count and the maximum of eight while the shadow column evaluated it every night, and one candidate past that maximum could stand. The same name may be registered again, which is how a candidate whose evaluator moved is registered under the code that now runs, and it stands once, in the divisor and against the maximum, because it is one condition being tried; which registration a night's evaluation belongs to is the one standing when that night started, which the register's own instants say. The divisor, the maximum, the shadow column and the run page read the rule from `CandidateFamily` alone, so no two of them can answer differently. Ruled at the 8.3 correction.

**A register row in the same second as the instant it is compared with is read as after it** `registered_at` is held to the second, as every instant this repository stores is, and a night's start and a window's opening are read off the clock with their fractions. Compared as they stand, a registration made a fraction of a second after a night started is stored in that second, reads as before the start, and is evaluated by a night that began before it was registered. So the question of what stood before an instant takes the instant to the second as well, and a row in that same second is read as after it: a registration there is not evaluated by that night and not counted in that window's divisor, and a retirement there has not yet withdrawn its candidate. The table cannot say which of two things in one second came first, and this is the reading under which no night evaluates a candidate registered after it started. The registrar's own refusals ask what stands as of the moment it read the register, the rows written in that second included, because a registration and its retirement a moment later are both already rows. Ruled at the 8.3 correction.

**Only a missing or moved evaluator fails the shadow column, and a name-night without the readings is a counted skip** A registered candidate the night cannot evaluate on a name is written into that row's `skipped` list with its reason whatever the cause, so the record of the hole is the same either way, and what differs is what the listings stage says about it. A candidate whose evaluator the code no longer carries, or whose evaluator's version is not the one it was registered under, can be evaluated on no name at all: that is a fault in the code, it holds on every row, and a person has to act on it by retiring the candidate and registering it again, so it fails the stage, is named once on the stage's run log row and puts the stage under failures on the run page. A name with no bar for the session, a name whose stored series has a gap, and a reading not available for the name are facts about that name on that night, and the index holds some of each on ordinary nights: a member the day's file carries nothing for, a member with a hole, a member too new to fill the longest average's window. Each is skipped on its own row with a reason saying which, counted by cause on the stage's line, and fails nothing. Counted as failures they would put the stage under failures on every night a candidate stood, and a stage that fails every night is one whose failure nobody reads, so the fault the failure exists for would arrive looking like every other night. The stage's line no longer says a candidate skipped on one name was not evaluated on any name-night, which was false for every candidate evaluated on the rest of the index. Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-16.

**A ladder rule's version is measured beside that rule's live window, and both count against a bound of eighteen** A version of a ladder rule is opened only while that rule's live window is open, under exactly the parameter names the rule is replayed from, and a live window is not closed while a version of its rule stays open. The live window is the one the night compares against the build's own hash, and a version replays through the same code the live rule runs through, so a version opened with no live window beside it is a measurement whose subject can move with nothing to stop the night: the code changes, the version's scores become scores of something else, and nothing says so. A parameter name the rule does not read is refused because the replay falls back to the live value for a missing name, which would store the live rule's plans under a version's name. Every open window counts against the caps, live ones included, and the caps are per rule: two windows of the merge distance, whose versions replay the level stage at 148 seconds on the night of 2026-09-14, and four of each other rule, whose versions replay the ladder stage at 5. That puts the fullest register the caps admit at one level replay and twelve ladder replays, 208 seconds on a 495 second night, inside the deadline of 7200, where the bound as 8.0 wrote it and 8.6 built it, four windows of every rule and fourteen at once with no live window required, admitted four level replays and ten ladder replays, a night of 1,137 seconds, and stated 688 as though it were the end. The alternative was a bound on projected seconds rather than on windows, which would move every time the night's stage durations did and leave a person unable to say in advance whether a version would be admitted; a count of windows can be read off the verb's own list. The caps are the operator's ruling in the third round of the phase 8 plan's review, approved on 2026-09-15: two windows of the merge distance, four of where the stop sits and four of the near-exit skip, each rule's live window counted and no extra replay, ten at once and a night of 673 seconds, with four of every rule rejected at 969. The zone edges rule arrived after that round, from 8.0's ruling on a tranche zone's edges, and carries the cap of the other rules that replay the ladder stage alone, which made fourteen; the trend rule arrived at 10.4 on the same terms and makes eighteen (see: The trend rule is a fifth ladder rule a version replays, and none of its three versions is live). The caps per rule are the ones ruled in 2026-09-15 and the sum is arithmetic over them, so a rule joining the set moves the total and settles nothing here. Three windows of the merge distance would put the night at 836 seconds on a projection from one night's stage durations. A version is opened only beside its rule's live window because that ruling counts the live window inside each cap and the plan's enforcement hashes each live rule against its open window. The caps are ruled by the operator on 2026-09-15; the zone edges cap and the live window requirement were written here at the 8.6 correction. The operator settled the caps and their sum of eighteen on 2026-09-28, with the night's deadline at an hour, so the bound is no longer proposed and waits on no count of the scorer's nights (see: A feed is tried three times with a doubling backoff, and the night has a two-hour deadline it cannot move).

Supersedes **A ladder rule's version is measured beside that rule's live window, and both count against the bound**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but the deadline of 900 seconds the fullest register was held inside and that the scorer's own nights would set the caps.

**A version's score counts only for a session after the New York date its window opened on** A score the version scorer writes is flagged `scored`, and counts toward a record, only where the session it scores comes after the date in New York, read through the clock, that its window's opening instant falls on; every other score is `in_sample` and counts toward nothing. The flag compared the session with the window's date in UTC, and a night runs at half past eleven UTC on its own session's date, so a window a person opened after reading that night's list and before midnight UTC counted the night they had just read, where one opened half an hour later on the same New York evening did not. A date is the unit because a version is written by someone who has seen what the market did, and the market's day is the New York session: anything opened on a session's date could have been fitted to it. The instant the session's night ran was the alternative and is not taken, because it counts a window opened that afternoon by someone who watched the session trade, and it moves with when a night happened to run, a night re-run by hand included, where a date belongs to the session alone. What it costs is the session a window opens on, which never counts even for a window opened before the market opened. Ruled at the 8.6 correction by the session correcting it, and not by the operator.

**A backfill scores only a night the store computed, at that night's own price scale, and never rewrites a score already stored** A backfill replays a past session from the bands, trend and typical move that session's night computed and from bars as they stand now, and three things decide whether what it stores is a plan that night could have produced. The bands and trend are read as of that session exactly: a session fetched by a backfill or by a later night as a missed one holds bars and no bands of its own, and reading the newest earlier set replayed its close against another night's bands, so a backfill of a session no night computed is refused and a name its night computed nothing for is left out and counted. The bars, the swing lows and the typical move are divided by the session's adjustment factor, its bar's close over its raw close, and rounded to the store's price places: a refetch after a split or a dividend rescales a name's whole stored year, its swings and its typical move while the stored bands keep the scale their night computed them at, and a night's own bar carries no later adjustment, so the factor is exactly what has moved since and is one on the night itself. And a score already stored for that window and session is kept, because the night wrote it from inputs that agreed when it ran and a later reading of the same session replacing it is a figure rewritten after the fact, which is what the operator ruled against for a decided setup on 2026-09-16 (see: An outcome once decided is never rewritten, and a setup still in play is scored with its plan scaled by its listing session's adjustment factor). A re-run of the night itself still writes its set again, since it recomputes the bands with it. Two alternatives were not taken: leaving out every name an action touched since the session, which after a quarter's dividends is most of the index, and refusing a backfill across any refetch, which is every backfill older than a few nights. Ruled at the 8.6 correction by the session correcting it, and not by the operator.

**A rule version change closes the window with the evidence that produced it and opens its replacement in the same write** Section 13's guardrail says a rule version change is written with the evidence that produced it and the version it replaced. A window closed through the verb took an optional name for what replaced it and no evidence, so a change stored no reason at all, and the name it did store was text nothing checked, which could name a version never opened. A change is one command: it closes the old window writing its evidence and the name of the version replacing it, and opens that version beside the rule's live window, in one transaction, with the rule's cap counted once the old window is closed, so a rule at its cap can still be changed and no replacement is named that the same write did not open. A close with nothing replacing it is refused without evidence as well, because ending a measurement changes what is measured. Opening a version takes none, as a registration takes none: it states what will be measured, and the evidence is what the measurement later produces. The alternative was to keep the close and require its replacement to be open already, which a rule at its cap can never satisfy. Ruled at the 8.6 correction by the session correcting it, and not by the operator.

**The ladder rules' code version pins every source a live ladder rule or its replay runs through, its comments and blank lines aside** The code version hashed into every window is the pin of every source file the compiled code runs through from where each side reads a ladder rule's inputs and applies it: the level builder's bands, the ladder builder's plan, the scorer's replay and the reads it replays from, and the hash the night compares. A test derives that set from the build's call graph and symbols and holds the scorer's list to it, so a rule or a helper that moves into a file the list does not name fails the check rather than leaving the code a window measures unpinned. The merge distance is one constant the level builder applies and the scorer hashes, so the value a live window carries is the value the night applies. A file is pinned whole wherever the walk reaches a method in it, which pins the swing and trend arithmetic that share files with the records both sides read; the indicator engine, the swing finder, the volume profile builder and the trend classifier are not reached and are not pinned, because they write the stored rows both sides read, and a change there moves the live rule's inputs and every version's alike, so a window still compares two rules over one set of inputs. The cost is stated rather than narrowed: an edit to any line of code in a pinned file is a new code version, and every night with a live window open then stops at the rule versions step, before the close and the queue, until each open window of every rule is closed and opened again, because one code version is hashed into every rule's window. An edit to a comment, a decision citation among them, or to the blank lines between is not, since the pin reads neither (see: A comment or a blank line moves no pin, and every other change to a pinned source does). A pin narrowed to the arithmetic would miss the reads that decide what a replay is given, so the cost is made visible instead: `version --list` names each live window the build no longer hashes to, and the runbook says to close the windows before such an edit merges. Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-16, and the comments and blank lines set aside on the operator's ruling of 2026-09-28, which reads all three pins through one rule, so a pin blind to comments is the one pinning rule and not a second beside the evaluator's.

Supersedes **The ladder rules' code version pins every source a live ladder rule or its replay runs through**, moved below with its reasoning, all of which stands but that a comment or a decision citation is a new code version.

**A version of a ladder rule is refused at values its replay would not apply as given, or at its rule's live values** The replay rounds a merge distance multiple to the four places a price carries, counts a near-exit skip in whole typical days, and reads a flag as zero or not zero, so a version at 2.5 typical days replays two, a flag of 0.5 replays as set, and a multiple written to five places replays another multiple: each stores the plans of a value its row does not name, which is the outcome the refusal of an unread parameter name exists to prevent. A multiple is therefore a number above zero, since at zero no two candidates join and two at one price stay two bands, written to at most four places; a skip is a whole number of typical days from zero; and a flag is one or zero. A value no price can hold, not a number or an infinity among them, is refused at the open rather than stopping every night after it, and a multiple the open admits that still overflows a price on a name whose typical move is large stops the rule versions step naming the version to close. A version at its rule's live values is refused, because it would store the live rule's plans under a version's name, and a parameter named twice on the command line is refused rather than read as either value. Two versions at one value are not refused: a flag rule has one value besides its live one, so that refusal would hold it at two windows under the four its cap allows, and two such windows store the same plans under two names rather than a plan either did not produce. Ruled at the 8.6 correction.

**A live reason is added or retired only by a change to section 11 and the code together, and the register holds candidates alone** The six live reasons are section 11's and the code's (see: Each setup family's correction for luck counts its own rules alone, at most nine a family), and none is a row in the candidate register. A register row that withdrew a live reason would shorten tonight's list with no edit to the document, which is the code and the architecture disagreeing with nothing to say so. So a live reason is retired, and a candidate promoted to live, by one change to section 11's table and `ShortlistSeries.Reasons` together; the decision that change writes names the record that produced it, which is its evidence, and section 11's prior text in CHANGELOG is the version it replaced. A live reason is retired only once its record holds 400 resolved setups, the higher floor section 17 states. No page computes that floor: the count it is read against is the resolved count the run page draws beside the reason. The register refuses a live reason's name as a registration, since a candidate under it would give a later retirement two meanings, and refuses to retire a live reason's name that does not stand registered, naming the floor. A candidate that does stand registered is retired like any other even where section 11 and the code already carry its name, because a promotion changes those first and the candidate still has to leave the candidate family: refusing that retirement would keep it in the divisor and in one of the eight places for good. No component computes a candidate's record yet, so none can be promoted on the build as it stands (owes: A candidate's shadow record computed, and a promotion written against it). Decided at the 8.7 correction on 2026-09-18, under the operator's ruling of 2026-09-16 that every judgement the phase 8 sign-off review carried is corrected before the sign-off handoff. The content is that review's judgement, that the refusal the building session made stands and the promoted candidate it trapped is let out, and is not an operator ruling on it.

**A reason's record reads only the rows written under the threshold the code carries, and the rows written under another are kept** Section 13.3 says a rule or threshold is not changed during a window it is measured over, and that a change starts a new window and keeps the old one. A ladder rule's windows are rows (see: A ladder rule's version is measured beside that rule's live window, and both count against a bound of eighteen). A reason's threshold is a constant in `ShortlistSeries`, and the listing row a reason fired on already stores the value it was evaluated under, `multiple` for unusual volume and `horizon` for earnings soon, so the window is read off the row rather than kept beside it. A reason's record, its track and its verdict, counts a fired reason only where the row's stored threshold is the one the code carries: a calibration that changes a threshold restarts that reason's record at nothing, and every row written before it stays stored as written. Counting every row whatever threshold it was evaluated under would mix setups from two rules into one verdict with nothing on the page saying so, which is the measurement whose subject moved that the guardrail exists to prevent. A row stating no threshold for a reason that has one is read as written under another, since it cannot say which window it belongs to; every listing row the operator's store held on 2026-09-18 states one, 3,530 of unusual volume at 2 and 3,530 of earnings soon at 20. A threshold is a named numeric constant in `ShortlistSeries` written onto its reason's rows under its own name, and `read-surface` fails one that is not. Decided at the 8.7 correction on 2026-09-18, under the operator's ruling of 2026-09-16 that every finding the phase 8 sign-off review raised is corrected before the sign-off handoff.

**Tonight's list selects the stocks worth buying or paying attention to that evening, and the improvement loop exists to make that selection better** The operator ruled on 2026-09-21: "The purpose was to show selection of stocks as of that day which are worth buying or paying attention towards. There is no point in having an improvement loop otherwise." The corpus had held two positions at once. Section 1 said the tool finds which stocks sit at a price their own chart has made significant and is not a screen, and the superseded entry refused any change that decides which stock is better than another. Section 13 meanwhile scores every listing as a trade with an entry, a stop and a target, which is already a judgement that one setup is worth taking and another is not. This settles which stands: the list is a selection, and a change that makes it select better is in scope, including one that orders names against each other. What the ruling does not move is stated here so it is not read as moved. The nightly run stays arithmetic, with no model call and no per-name request (see: The nightly run is arithmetic only). No reading of the fundamentals fires a reason or gates a tranche (see: No reading of the fundamentals fires a reason, gates a tranche or draws a panel). The tool does not trade, and the plan places a position and never sizes one (see: The plan places a position and never sizes one). A reason's record is shown beside the reason and never beside the ticker (see: A reason's record is displayed, beside the reason and never beside the name). A condition is registered before it is scored and scored in shadow before it is shown, and the register is never edited (see: Candidate conditions are registered before they are scored, and scored in shadow before they are shown). What the ruling does not settle is how the list selects: what "worth buying" is measured against, whether the list may rank by a score, the cap on how many names are drawn, the trend guard, what at entry zone means, whether a trend label must hold before it gates a plan, how a day of market-wide volume is treated, and how a changed condition goes live. Those are for the next phase's planning pass to rule, and settling them here would be that pass written under a ruling's heading. Until it rules on the order, tonight's list keeps the order it is built with (see: Tonight's list orders by the reasons that fired and then band strength, which are facts about the chart), whose reasoning rested on the line this entry removes. Supersedes **Improving what surfaces a name is in scope; ranking names against each other is not**, moved below with its reasoning.

**A price that has come down into a narrow buying zone since the previous session is a candidate condition** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 that "at entry zone" is replaced, as a registered candidate, by arrived and narrow, and that the live reason stays unchanged beside it until the candidate is promoted. Exactly: the previous session's close was above the nearest buying zone's high edge, tonight's close is inside that zone with its low edge at or below the close and its high edge at or above it, and the zone's width, its high edge less its low edge, is no more than 1 of the name's typical daily moves. The multiple 1 is proposed. A close that went from above the zone to below its low edge in one session fell through the zone and is not an arrival. The live reason fires on a close anywhere inside a zone, and on 2026-09-18 225 of the 347 names with a buying zone had their close inside their nearest one, which is standing in the floor rather than arriving at it. Over the nine nights 2026-09-09 to 2026-09-21 the candidate would have fired 14, 9, 2, 2, 8, 5, 3, 6 and 3 times. Evidence: Osler (2000) scores a support or resistance level by whether the price arrives at it and reverses, which is the arrival reading; Chung and Bellotti (2021) find that a level's prior bounces and its age bear on whether it holds, so each verdict records the band's strength and the sessions since its newest member without testing either; Kavajecz and Odders-White (2004) find levels coincide with depth on the limit order book. Judgement: no study found sets the width multiple. One candidate rather than two, because registering arrival and width apart would grow the family the level is divided across. (see: Candidate conditions are registered before they are scored, and scored in shadow before they are shown)

**Unusual volume measured against the night's own median is a candidate condition** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 that unusual volume relative to the night is registered as a candidate, and that the live reason and its multiple are not changed, because that is a live threshold with a measured record. Exactly: a name's volume ratio, tonight's volume over its fifty-day average, is above 2 times the night's median ratio and above 2 on its own. The multiple 2 is proposed. The median is taken before any name is evaluated, over the members on tonight's session that have a fifty-day average, leaving out any name with no volume tonight, and it is recorded on every verdict so a night can be replayed; it makes no request and no model call. The fifty-day average includes tonight, so above 2 times it is about 2.04 times the prior forty-nine sessions' mean. On 2026-09-18 the median name traded 2.00 times its own average and the live reason fired on 252 of 503. Of the two forms measured, the one ruled fires 8, 4, 8, 5, 8, 17, 8, 18 and 5 times on 2026-09-09 to 09-21, where the live reason fired 8, 4, 8, 4, 8, 17, 8, 252 and 5 by the stored rows, so it differs from the live reason on one of the nine nights and its record will mostly re-test the live reason's. The bare form, above 2 times the median alone, fires on names at 1.6 to 1.9 times their own average on a quiet night. Evidence: Gervais, Kaniel and Mingelgrin (2001) class a day as high volume when it is in the top tenth of the stock's own fifty-day window, forty-nine reference days and the day itself, and the effect holds when each day is ranked by the stock's share of market volume, which is a control for a market-wide day; Campbell and Wasley (1996) measure abnormal volume against a market-adjusted expectation. So each verdict also records the rank of tonight's volume within its fifty-day window. Judgement: the multiple 2 has no research basis. Breakout on volume also reads volume against the fifty-day average and did not share the flood, firing 7 times on 2026-09-18 against 16 the night before, so it takes no candidate.

**A crossing is a candidate condition only where the close sits half a typical move past the edge it crossed** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22, over counts re-derived at 10.3 from the nine stored nights 2026-09-09 to 09-21, that the margin is 0.5 of the name's typical daily move, measured from tonight's close to the edge it crossed: the high edge for a crossing upward and the low edge for a crossing downward, with the typical move the one the ladder reads. At 0.5 the condition fires 18 to 46 names a night, a mean of 30.1 and 271 over the nine nights, which reproduces the 2026-09-20 measurement of the tight setting at 20 to 48; at 0.25 it fires 38 to 89, a mean of 64.0 and 576; and the live reason, which fires on any edge crossed, fires 112 to 176. Power did not choose between them: at 0.5 about 1,896 setups are listed a block and about 1,138 of those resolve with a bar, against the 250 the single-read figure is stated at, and 0.25 holds about twice that. What chose is that 0.5 is the stricter reading of the defect the margin answers, 19% of 1,132 recorded crossings having finished within a tenth of a typical move of the edge and one having closed 9 cents past a level and listed, and that at 0.25 this one reason would draw a large part of the twenty rows the page holds. The margin is settled rather than proposed from here, so it is registered with the candidate and changing it is a new registration (see: Nothing a candidate or a version is registered with changes while it runs, and a proposed number is settled only by a new registration). Not settled by research: no study found sets the margin. Osler (2003) finds stop-loss orders cluster just beyond round numbers and rates move rapidly once they cross them, which bears on what follows a crossing and not on how far past it counts.

**The three candidates are registered at one instant** A candidate's window is tested at a level divided across the candidates standing when it opened (see: Each setup family's correction for luck counts its own rules alone, at most nine a family), so a candidate registered alone before the others would be tested at the whole level rather than a third of it. Registration is one command, run once by the operator after the crossing margin is settled, writing the three rows at one instant, so every window opens with three standing. A later registration does not raise the level an earlier window opened at. Evidence: Sullivan, Timmermann and White (1999) show that simple technical trading rules on the DJIA and the S&P 500 lose their apparent profit once the universe of rules tried is accounted for, which is why what was tried is counted.

**A candidate is judged by a sign-flip test over blocks of 63 sessions, with at least eight blocks** The unit of the test is a block of 63 consecutive calendar sessions keyed on the listing date, and the verdict is a block sign-flip randomization test. Exactly: s_j is the sum, over the setups listed in block j whose outcome window has closed, of the win less the setup's null win probability; T is the mean of the s_j over their standard deviation divided by the square root of q, the count of non-empty blocks; the p-value is the share of sign vectors in {-1,+1}^q, the identity included, whose T is at least the observed. Every sign vector is enumerated, since q is at most 16 at every look. It is the same sign-flip scheme as the restricted wild cluster bootstrap and is never described as equivalent to it. The Poisson-binomial p-value of the live family's test is shown beside it, labelled, and never decides anything. A permutation of outcomes across setups is not the test, because it leaves the sum of wins and the sum of null probabilities unchanged and so always returns 1. The operator ruled L = 63 and q >= 8: setups listed less than 63 sessions apart resolve over shared bars and are not independent, the one-sided floor of a sign-flip test is 1/2^q, so 6 blocks is the least that can reach a third of 0.05, and 8 is the ruled minimum. The length is registered with the candidate and never shortened by the data, only non-empty blocks count, and the block count is shown against its minimum wherever the resolved count is. Eight blocks is 504 sessions, about two years, plus up to 63 for the last to resolve; the 60-session floor stays and no longer binds.
