Evidence: Kolari and Pynnonen (2010) show that event-date clustering makes standard tests over-reject even at low cross-sectional correlation, and Lopez de Prado (2018, chapter 4) that outcomes whose windows overlap are not independent; Canay, Romano and Shaikh (2017) that a randomization test needs a group of transformations leaving the null unchanged, sign changes for a location null, valid with a fixed number of large clusters; MacKinnon, Nielsen and Webb (2023) describe the close relation between randomization inference and the restricted wild cluster bootstrap with Rademacher weights; Cameron, Gelbach and Miller (2008) that the wild cluster bootstrap with the null imposed holds size where cluster-robust Wald tests over-reject, in simulations of up to 30 clusters; Ibragimov and Mueller (2010) that groups are consecutive observations and the choice of group cannot be delegated to the data; Cameron and Miller (2015) that with few clusters p-values are discrete. No moving-block or stationary bootstrap is the verdict (Kuensch 1989). Judgement: the floor of 8 blocks. The live family's own test is not changed by this decision, and whether it should take this one is an open question. (see: A verdict tests a reason's wins against each of its setups' own break-even at a corrected threshold)

**A candidate's verdict is read only at looks fixed when it is registered, with each look's boundary found over every sign vector its blocks allow** A fixed-level test repeated as setups accumulate promotes a worthless condition far more often than its level says: at a 35% break-even and a third of 0.05, one read at 250 setups has a size of 1.28%, while reading after every three resolved setups reaches 4.9% by 500, 7.4% by 1,000 and 9.9% by 2,000, and three worthless candidates read nightly have about a 21% chance that one is promoted by 1,000. So each register row carries its looks at registration: at 8, 12 and 16 non-empty blocks, information fractions 0.5, 0.75 and 1.0, triggered by the block count alone and never by where the figure stands, with the level spent across them by a Lan-DeMets function of the O'Brien-Fleming type. Each look's boundary is found on the sign-flip null by enumerating every arrangement of the signs of the blocks the look holds, conditional on what those blocks came to: the arrangements that crossed at an earlier look stay marked, and the boundary is the lowest statistic at which the share of arrangements crossing by this look stays within the level the spending function has released. Nothing is drawn and no seed is taken. It replaces the simulation the superseded entry called for, for two reasons: a look holds at most sixteen blocks, so the arrangements are enumerable whole and an estimate of them would be a worse answer to the same question; and a simulation of independent setups would assume the independence within a block that the blocks exist because the setups do not have, while an enumeration over the blocks' own sums holds its size whatever the setups inside a block did to each other. Independent increments are not established under overlapping windows, which is why the joint distribution across the looks is enumerated rather than taken from the canonical form. Under independence at 500, 750 and 1,000 setups the crossing win shares would be 42.0%, 39.6% and 38.4%, overall size 1.52% and power 79.6% against a 4.6-point excess. The first look cannot promote: the level released at a fraction of 0.5 is 0.00071, below the floor of 1/256 that 8 blocks allow, so it can only retire; the earliest promotion is at 12 blocks, 756 sessions, about three years, and the maximum at 16, 1,008 sessions, about four years, each plus up to 63 to resolve. Evidence: Armitage, McPherson and Rowe (1969) on repeated significance tests on accumulating data; Lan and DeMets (1983) on spending functions chosen before monitoring; O'Brien and Fleming (1979); FDA (2019) guidance on adaptive designs, that analysis times chosen from results inflate error and that the maximum is not extended. Judgement: the three looks and their spacing.

**The nightly running figure is monitoring and never the verdict** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 that the running figure may show on the run page every night, labelled as monitoring. The verdict field shows the last look's result, the looks remaining and what triggers the next, and a nightly figure never changes it. FDA (2019) recommends limiting access to interim results; the operator chose to show it knowingly.

**A look counts only the setups whose whole outcome window has closed** A setup resolved so far is not a random draw of its setups: in a driftless daily walk at a 35% break-even, wins take 22.9 sessions on average to resolve and losses 16.0, so an early record undercounts wins. The store shows it: setups listed 2026-09-09 to 09-18 hold 109 wins and 246 scored losses, 30.7%, against a median break-even of 39.1%. So a look's cut-off listing session is at least the entry window plus 63 sessions back, and it is stored with the verdict. Evidence, by analogy: Hampson and Jennison (2013) on pipeline subjects at interim analyses, and Lindon and Kallus (2026) on outcome-dependent censoring. Judgement: the simulation's figures.

**A setup's null win probability is calibrated from its own plan, and its planned break-even is shown beside it** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 to calibrate. A setup's planned break-even, its risk over its risk and reward, assumes an exit exactly at the stop or the target, and both are judged on closes, so a close lands past either and the realized exit is not the planned one. Judgement from a simulation: a plan with no edge wins about 38.3% of its resolved setups at a stop one typical move away against a planned 35%, and about 30.5% at three moves, where the 63-session cap works the other way, so a worthless condition with tight stops could clear the planned break-even. So each setup's null win probability is simulated from its plan under the name's trailing volatility, with the 63-session cap and the round-trip cost, and used in the sign-flip test and in the Poisson-binomial beside it; the planned break-even is shown beside it. Before registration the store is measured for realized loss over planned risk and realized gain over planned reward, mean and 90th percentile, by stop distance in typical moves, over live listings and never a candidate's outcomes, and the calibration is pinned. The yardstick's idea stands: each setup carries its own bar (see: A condition is judged against the break-even its own plan demands). Evidence: Broadie, Glasserman and Kou (1997) and Janssen and van Leeuwaarden (2007) give the limiting expected overshoot of a zero-drift Gaussian walk checked at discrete times as about 0.5826 of one step's standard deviation, which is a benchmark and not a measurement of these names; Lopez de Prado (2018, chapter 3) frames an outcome by a profit barrier, a stop and a time barrier, which the yardstick matches. Realized loss in multiples of planned risk is reported beside every verdict and tested nowhere.

**The calibrated null carries a round trip of ten basis points, and thirty is shown as a sensitivity** A cost c raises a setup's break-even by c over its risk and reward, about a tenth of a point to a point at a small investor's costs and up to about seven points at thirty basis points with tight stops. Ten basis points round trip is pinned into the calibration before registration, and thirty is shown beside it. Close-based scoring assumes a fill at the exact close, which no order type guarantees, and the direction of that bias is unknown. Stop-outs on earnings sessions are reported apart, as a hypothesis. Evidence: Hagstromer on the S&P 500's effective spread, about 3.2 basis points in December 2015; Schwarz et al. on retail round trips from about 7 basis points; Frazzini, Israel and Moskowitz on institutional large-cap costs of about 18 to 30 basis points round trip; Arratia and Dorador (2019) that overnight gaps above 0.1% occur on about 78% of days with a mean near zero; Dubinsky et al. (2019) that earnings-day variance is about six times other days over 2000 to 2015, about 2.4 times in standard deviation. Judgement: the choice of ten.

**A candidate counts a setup entered and stopped in one session as a loss** A session that closes through both the zone and the stop is scored as a loss with no figure and no break-even, because the fill was somewhere in the zone and the store does not say where, and the live family's verdict keeps only setups that set a bar, so it drops them. The store holds 8 such losses of 254, and dropping them moves the win share from 30.0% to 30.7%. A candidate's record counts them as losses, with the null taken from the zone's high edge, the worst fill in the zone, and shows their count beside the verdict. Judgement: the high edge. Whether the live family should do the same is an open question.

**Holm's level passes between the candidates by a graph fixed when they are registered, and its first step is 0.05 over the distinct trials read at a look or still running** The operator ruled on 2026-09-27, on the review the lifetime count had made due at eight registered candidates, and in an addendum to the plan for the fundamentals item: keep Holm's method and change what it counts. The method stands: Holm's step-down, each candidate spending its level across its own looks by its own spending function, and a graph carried in each register row passing a promoted candidate's level in equal shares to those still standing and a retired one's to no one, with only the sign-flip p-value corrected. The budget changes. The level at Holm's first step is 0.05 over the distinct trials, shared across every one of them for the life of the system rather than given fresh to each window, so the error over the system's life is bounded where the per-window level let it grow with every window. A distinct trial is a rule whose results a look has read, or that is still running. A registration of the same rule after a correction, where only the version it reads changed and no threshold or condition moved, is the same trial as the rule it replaces, and a rule with any threshold or condition different from every earlier one is a new trial. A candidate retired with no result of its own ever read is not counted, as the three phase 10 candidates were retired, their retirement rows saying so: a rule that was never read cannot have been chosen on its luck. A promoted candidate was read at the look that crossed. A look is read at the count as of the night it was read, so a new trial moves the bar of the looks not yet read and never of one already read. The count, 6 on 2026-09-27: no candidate had reached a look, every retirement was taken unread, and the six standing are six rules, one of them version 2's live filter under the name the plan at the nearest bands from version 3; the register's sixteen names are fifteen rules. So each trial starts at 0.05 over 6, 0.0083, which the first look at half the information releases as 0.00019, below the 1 in 256 that eight blocks can reach, so the first look can retire a candidate and never promote one; the second releases 0.0023 in all and the last 0.0083. A new trial makes 7, starting at 0.0071, the first look releasing 0.00014 and the second 0.0019; a re-registration with only its version moved changes neither; a candidate retired unread leaves the count and raises the bar of the rest; and a trial read at a look stays counted for the life of the system. At every look the levels the counted trials hold sum to 0.05. A verdict keeps the bar it was read at, so trials registered after others were read are read at the larger count while the earlier verdicts keep theirs: six read at 0.05 over 6 and six registered after them, read at 0.05 over 12, come to at most 0.075. The count and the level it sets stand on the run page beside the family's divisor, and beside every verdict the step, its level and the count. Rejected: the planning pass's first proposal, a promotion crossing at the lower of its graph's level and 0.05 over the trials, which kept a fresh level for each window beside a second bar rather than changing what the one level counts. Evidence: Holm (1979) and Romano and Wolf (2005) on step-down control at the same level with more power, the first step as strict as Bonferroni; Maurer and Bretz (2013) on passing a rejected hypothesis's level by a pre-specified graph under group-sequential designs, with sequential p-values as Liu and Anderson (2008) give them, cited as Maurer and Bretz cite it; Harvey, Liu and Zhu (2016) and Bailey and Lopez de Prado (2014) on counting every trial; Tian and Ramdas on online error control. (see: Each setup family's correction for luck counts its own rules alone, at most nine a family) (see: The three phase 10 candidates are retired when the swing family registers, and each retirement says no result of theirs was read)

**The graph steps promoted candidates in the order their promotions were written, and candidates crossing on one read in the order they were registered** The operator ruled on 2026-09-25, on the second phase 12 sign-off review's finding that the run page stepped the graph in the candidates' name order, so two promoted candidates were drawn in name order whatever order their promotions were written in. A promoted candidate's level passed to those standing when its promotion was written, so the promoted step first, in the order their promotion rows were written, instant then row, which is the order their levels passed in (see: Holm's level passes between the candidates by a graph fixed when they are registered, and every verdict shows the lifetime count). Candidates whose records cross on one read and are not yet promoted step after them, in the order their first registration rows were written, an order fixed when they are registered and never moved by their results. The order changes no verdict: a candidate crossing at the lower level crosses at the raised one, and the level the others are left at is the same either way, every candidate standing in a window holding an equal share at every step. It decides which of two is drawn at the graph's earlier step and at the lower level, and the level each record is read at on the page. Rejected: the name order, which a rename would move; and the smaller sign-flip p-value first, the order of Holm's own procedure, which depends on the results and can change from one read to the next.

**A candidate is retired by a futility guideline at its first two looks or by reaching its last look** A candidate whose observed win share is strictly below its null share at its first or second look is retired, and so is one that reaches its maximum look without crossing, each by a new dated register row naming what it retires. The guideline binds nothing: the promotion boundary is computed as if it did not exist. Under independence it retires 61.8% of worthless candidates by the second look and a candidate with a true 4.6-point excess 7.3% of the time; read nightly it would retire that candidate 12.1% of the time, so it is never read nightly. Evidence: FDA (2019) on nonbinding futility, and Lan and DeMets (1983) on conditional power. (see: A candidate stands by the last row naming it, and a name retired and registered again stands once)

**Nothing a candidate or a version is registered with changes while it runs, and a proposed number is settled only by a new registration** The calibration's method, inputs and cap, the cost and its sensitivity, the spending function, the looks, the graph, the method each look's boundary is found by, and the paths and the seed the calibration walks are fixed in the register row or pinned in code before the registration command runs, because the running figure is shown every night and a choice made after it has been seen is a free parameter. A proposed number is settled only by a new registration or a new version, with its own window and counted in the lifetime count, and any reading that motivates it happens at a look. The trend confirmation's two nights may be settled from flip-back counts, which are labels and not outcomes, and a changed count is a new version. The margin by which version A must beat version B is fixed at 5 points before scoring and has no settling row. Every threshold phase 10 chose was chosen on per-night counts, with no forward return and no setup outcome read, which is what keeps three an accurate count of what was tried. Evidence: Harvey, Liu and Zhu (2016) that significance hurdles must rise with how much was tried; Sullivan, Timmermann and White (1999). This entry supersedes the one that named a boundary simulation's draw count and seed among what is fixed: a look's boundary is enumerated rather than simulated, so it has neither, and what the calibration draws is what carries a count and a seed (see: A candidate's verdict is read only at looks fixed when it is registered, with each look's boundary found over every sign vector its blocks allow).

**The trend rule is a fifth ladder rule a version replays, and none of its three versions is live** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 that the trend guard lands as rule versions and not as a direct edit, because it changes the ladder's shape. Version A: a close below both the 50-day and the 200-day average is a downtrend, whatever the swings say. Version B: the same, only where the 50-day is below the 200-day. Version C: entering downtrend removes buying zones the first night the label reads downtrend, and leaving it restores them only once the new label has held for 2 consecutive nights, read from the stored labels of the nights before; the 2 is proposed. Each is replayed beside the live rule, scored on its own and promoted by ruling. The trend rule takes the cap of four every rule but the merge distance has, and its live window with A, B and C fills it. On 2026-09-18 version A reaches 129 range names closing below both averages, 86 of them carrying buying zones, and version B 35, 30 with zones; the 94 A adds have the 50-day at or above the 200-day. If both clear, B is kept unless A clears by 5 points. Stored listings keep the plan as it stood that night. Evidence, as a prior and not a verdict, since each is a monthly portfolio sort and not a stop-and-target setup: Jegadeesh and Titman (1993) find two-to-twelve-month losers keep underperforming, Jegadeesh (1990) and Lehmann (1990) that one-month losers reverse, George and Hwang (2004) that nearness to the 52-week high predicts returns and does not reverse, and Avramov, Kaplanski and Subrahmanyam (2021) that the distance between the 21-day and 200-day averages predicts returns, more strongly on the long side. B's names resemble intermediate-term losers, where removing zones is supported, and the names A adds are mostly intermediate-term winners in a sharp pullback, where both momentum and short-term reversal argue the zones may be right, which supports B over A. Judgement: version C's one-sided rule and its 2 nights, for which no study was found. Trend labels changed on 210 of 3,523 night-to-night pairs on 2026-09-09 to 09-18, 6.0%, and returned the next night 35 times on 25 names. (see: A ladder rule's version is measured beside that rule's live window, and both count against a bound of eighteen)

**A trend version is judged by the candidates' test on its difference from the live rule** A version's promotion reads the block sign-flip test on the paired difference, the version's setups less the live rule's per block, at looks fixed when it opens, over closed windows and on the calibrated null. Choosing between A and B is a Reality Check with the live rule as the benchmark, computed on the same enumerated sign vectors, because picking the better of several on the data that scores them needs the best to be tested against the benchmark and not alone. The record of the setups A removes and B keeps is reported under the live rule and tested nowhere. Evidence: White (2000). (see: A candidate is judged by a sign-flip test over blocks of 63 sessions, with at least eight blocks)

**A version's record is read from the blocks frozen as each completed** A version's record is read at 8 blocks of 63 sessions and again at 12 and 16, which is 1,008 sessions and about four years, while `version_score` and `ladder` are kept one year back from the newest stored session. A record computed from those two tables when it is read could therefore hold 3 whole blocks at most against a floor of 8, so its verdict would be withheld for as long as the window stayed open and the page would say it was waiting rather than that it could never arrive. So the one number per block the record reads, the version's excess and the live rule's over that block, is computed on the night the block completes and written to `version_block`, which no retention reaches, and the per-night detail goes on being dropped at a year. The reader joins neither dropped table. The block origin is written with the first block and never recomputed, because retention moves the earliest scored session forward and an origin read again would re-cut the blocks under a record already being read. It is the shape `forward_return`'s calibrated bar and the listing's band strength already have, and the three alternatives were measured off the operator's own store at 399.5 bytes a `version_score` row and 1,386.8 a `ladder` row, projected to 18 windows, 502 names and 1,008 sessions: freezing one row per block is 288 rows and 27 KB, freezing one row per setup would be 7.6M rows and 349 MB, and raising the retention to four years would be 9.6M rows and 4.0 GB against a store of 221 MB on a spinning disk. A block is frozen 63 sessions after its last, so the oldest session it needs is 125 behind the night it completes against a window of about 252, which is 127 sessions of margin. Nothing past the last look is frozen, because the last look is never extended and a seventeenth block is read by nothing. Found at the phase 10 review of 2026-09-23 and ruled by the operator in the prompt that corrects it. (see: A listing records the band strength the old order read, and the three orders are compared over the nights that recorded it) (see: A trend version is judged by the candidates' test on its difference from the live rule)

**A version's record belongs to the window its scores were written under and never to the version's name** Every reader of a version keys on the rule, the version and the instant its window opened, which is the key `version_score` already carries. A version is changed by closing its window and opening another, and the runbook's remedy for a pinned source that moved is to close a window and open it again under the same name, so one name carries rows of several windows and did so twice in the four days to 2026-09-23. Keyed on the name alone, a session scored under two windows is counted twice in the labels a region draws and fed twice into the blocks a record is read over, and the record's origin is taken from the older window. The instant and not the date it falls on, because the remedy run after midnight New York opens a second window on the same date as the first. The surface draws the instant for the same reason. Found at the phase 10 review of 2026-09-23. (see: A version's record is read from the blocks frozen as each completed)

**The order tonight's list is drawn in is compared against the order it replaces, declared before any record is read** Three orders are measured over the twenty each would draw: fired count then band strength, the order the list had before phase 10, which is the benchmark; fired count then the plan's reward to risk, which the operator ruled; and reward to risk alone. Switching needs a Reality Check over both challengers to reject at 0.05, paired by night over the rows each order draws, with the metric and the row count fixed first and the same blocks as the candidates' test, and the chosen order's own p-value is never reported alone. Evidence: White (2000) that when the best of several is picked on the same data the right null is that the best is no better than the benchmark; Diebold (2015) on paired loss differentials with dependence-robust errors, from which the Diebold-Mariano comparison is described; Hansen's SPA is known here from secondary sources only. Research does not settle which tiebreak is better: Chung and Bellotti (2021) find levels with more prior bounces more likely to bounce again, decaying with time, while Osler (2000) found the published strength ratings bore no consistent relation to bounces, the only significant differences being negative.

**A listing records the band strength the old order read, and the three orders are compared over the nights that recorded it** The comparison of the three orders needs every order to hold 8 blocks of 63 sessions with a setup whose whole outcome window has closed, which is about two years, and bars, bands and plans are dropped a year back, so an order recomputed from them would lose its oldest nights before the comparison could ever be read. The listing row is kept, so from 10.1 it records the highest strength of the name's bands on its own night, 0 where the night stored none, and its plan already keeps the first tranche and the first traded exit the reward to risk reads. The comparison reads the nights whose rows recorded the strength, up to the night the run page shows, which leaves out every night written before the 5.4 correction as well. On each night each order draws its first 20 fired rows, and a drawn row whose plan computes no reward to risk has no stop or no traded target, so it is no setup and adds nothing. A setup's whole outcome window has closed once 63 sessions have traded after its listing, the setup cap being counted from the listing session, and the blocks are 63 exchange sessions each, counted from the first recorded night and keyed on the listing's session. The measure, fixed here before any record is read, is the excess the candidates' test sums: per block, the wins among the closed-window setups an order drew less their calibrated null win probabilities, with each challenger's difference from the benchmark taken block by block. The run page states each order's setups, its closed-window setups and its blocks against the floor, and draws no comparison until every order holds 8. Judgement: recording the strength rather than each order's draws, because the draws can be rebuilt from the fired count, the strength and the plan the row keeps, and positions stored beside them would be a second statement of the rule the orders apply. (see: The order tonight's list is drawn in is compared against the order it replaces, declared before any record is read)

**Power is stated for the looks that exist as well as for a single read** A single read at 250 independent setups, at a 35% break-even and one-sided 0.05 over three, needs a 9.45-point excess for 80% power and 6.81 for 50%, and has 23.8% power against 4.6 points and 10.2% against 2.9; at design effects of 1.25, 1.5, 2 and 3 the 80% figure rises to 10.72, 11.66, 13.16 and 16.00 points and the power against 4.6 falls to 18.1%, 15.7%, 13.6% and 10.4%. The looks that exist hold far more setups than 250 and are limited by blocks rather than setups. The first has no power by construction. Judgement from a simulation of block sums at the later looks, with the level split conservatively between them: at design effect 1 the arrived-and-narrow candidate, about 161 resolved setups a block, detects a 4.6-point excess 98% of the time, 80% at design effect 2 and 65% at 3; the volume candidate, about 340 a block, 92% to 100%; a crossing candidate at either margin, over 1,000 a block, about 100%. That assumes the edge is steady across blocks, and heterogeneity would lower it. The figures are re-derived on the calibrated null before registration, re-read at every look with the excess observed, and each verdict shows its estimated design effect, the block-robust variance of the summed excess over the sum of p(1 - p), labelled noisy near 8 blocks. Evidence: Osler (2000) found published levels bounced 60.8% of the time against 56.2% for arbitrary ones, and five days after publication 1.7 points less often than on the day, leaving roughly 2.9 points above arbitrary if that baseline held; the design effect is the actual variance over the variance under simple random sampling (Kish, as Cameron and Miller 2015 give it).


**The three phase 10 candidates are retired when the swing family registers, and each retirement says no result of theirs was read** Arrived and narrow, volume against the night and crossed by a margin were registered on 2026-09-23 to ask whether a tightened reason should replace a live one on tonight's list. From phase 12 no reason chooses the list, so the question they were registered to answer no longer exists. The live filter and its five variants are six, and with the three standing the family would be nine, above its maximum of eight then (see: Each setup family's correction for luck counts its own rules alone, at most nine a family). Adding the six evaluators also edits files every registered evaluator's version pins, so the three would be skipped on every name-night as moved. The operator ruled on 2026-09-24 that the three are retired by the command that registers the six, at the same instant, and that each retirement row states that no result of the candidate it retires was read before it: a retirement taken after looking would be a selection on outcomes, and the row saying so is what shows this one was not. So the session that builds phase 12 reads neither the three candidates' records region nor their shadow outcomes before the command runs. Nine candidates will then have been registered in all, and the review of the lifetime count at eight stays with the decision that names it (see: Holm's level passes between the candidates by a graph fixed when they are registered, and every verdict shows the lifetime count).

**A candidate whose evaluator a code change moved is registered again unchanged, every one at one instant** A candidate's evaluator version is the pin of every source its evaluation runs through, so a change to any of them moves every standing candidate's version, and from the next night each is skipped and named a failure until it is retired and registered again. Nothing about the candidate was decided again, so it is registered again unchanged: its name, rule, test, evaluator and parameters are the row that stood, retired on the evidence given and registered at the version the code now carries. All of them at one instant, for the reason a family registers at one, since each candidate's level is divided across the candidates the first night evaluated beside it, and twelve commands typed one after another land at twelve instants. A candidate whose evaluator the code no longer carries is a retirement rather than a registration again, so it refuses the command whole. The operator asked on 2026-09-26 how the swing family is registered again after the 3.4 correction moved its evaluator, on the recommendation of one command in place of twelve.

**A comment or a blank line moves no pin, and every other change to a pinned source does** The three pins the corpus holds a stored row to, the swing filter's code version, the ladder rules' and each candidate evaluator's, are taken by one function over the files each lists, and it reads each line with the white space at its ends trimmed and leaves out every line that is a comment and nothing else and every line holding nothing. The operator ruled it on 2026-09-28, planning the window in which breakouts were removed and the night's deadline moved: a decision superseded renames its citations, and a citation sits in a comment in a pinned file, so a correction to the record alone moved every pin, stopped every night with a ladder window open at the rule versions step, and cost the family's clocks a restart for a change no answer can show. A comment is read by a person and never by the code, and a blank line by neither, so leaving them out leaves every answer a pinned file gives where it was. A comment written after code on its own line is still read, with the code it sits beside, because telling where such a comment starts means reading the code's strings, and a pin that guessed would be one that could hide a change to a string; no pinned file carries one. What is given up is that a comment reworded to say something false about the code moves nothing, which was already so of every file no pin lists. A test holds it both ways over a constructed source and over the ladder rules' own files with every comment reworded: a comment reworded, a citation renamed, a comment added and a blank line added move no pin, and a changed constant, a comment written after code and a changed string holding two slashes each move it.

**A shape acceptance restarts the live filter's edge clock, and after one acceptance while the list is live each further one states the blocks it restarts** The shape clock is due every sixty ordinary nights, and the live filter's first look is at eight blocks, about two years, with no promotion before twelve, about three. An acceptance moves the live settings the live filter's candidate was registered with, and nothing a candidate is registered with changes while it runs (see: Nothing a candidate or a version is registered with changes while it runs, and a proposed number is settled only by a new registration). So an acceptance retires the live filter's candidate and registers the accepted settings in the same write, and that candidate's clock starts again. Unbounded, an operator accepting every proposal would never reach an edge verdict, and the loop meant to say whether the filter works would never answer. The operator ruled on 2026-09-24: the settings ruled from phase 12's counts before the family registers are acceptance zero and restart nothing; one further acceptance, at the first trigger after the list goes live, costs the restart and nothing more, about one block of sixty-three sessions; after that shape is frozen, its proposals still computed and shown, and accepting one requires the command to state the non-empty blocks the live filter's clock has run, which the run page draws beside every proposal and the command refuses at any other count, so what an acceptance costs is a number read before it rather than a consequence found after. A rejection is never bounded.

**A rule correction taken before the family's first scored night opens a filter version and registers the family again at one instant, and is no shape acceptance** The operator ruled on 2026-09-26 that the switch to section 10's plan for the swing trade is a rule correction taken before the family's first scored night and does not count as the one shape acceptance the bound allows. The shape command's correction closes the open version and opens the next with the trade input the ruling names and every other setting as it stood, and in the same write retires every standing swing family candidate and registers the six the code writes for the new version, stating the non-empty blocks the live candidate has run, which it restarts, or is refused; it is refused too where no version is open, where the version already reads the input named, and where no live candidate stands. The bound counts the retirements a shape acceptance writes and nothing else: a correction, and a registration again after a code change moved an evaluator, retire the live candidate as well, and counting every retirement of it read the registration again of 2026-09-26 as a second acceptance. Version 2's opening on 2026-09-25 remains the acceptance version 1 had left while the list is live (see: Version 2 of the swing filter lowers the market floor to 45% before the list's first live night, and nothing else moves), so every later acceptance still states the blocks it restarts (see: A shape acceptance restarts the live filter's edge clock, and after one acceptance while the list is live each further one states the blocks it restarts).

**The shape proposer moves one setting a gate, nearest first, and never applies what it proposes** At the shape clock's trigger the shape proposer recounts every ordinary night's stored gate readings under other settings, which it may do because it moves counts and reads no outcome, and moves one setting a gate: trend and strength's floor, tried from 0.40 to 0.95 by 0.01; the setup's dry-up ceiling, from 0.50 to 2.00 by 0.05; and the trade's reward to risk floor, from 1.00 to 4.00 by 0.10. It takes the gates in the funnel's order, holding every gate before one at what it has just proposed, and for each tries the values nearest the one held first, taking the first that puts the gate's median count over the ordinary nights inside its band and the lower of two at the same distance. One setting a gate, because a gate moved on two settings at once reaches its band by many pairs and choosing among them is a choice the counts do not make; the nearest, because the smallest move that answers the band changes least of what the ruled settings meant. The pullback's depth, the base's tightness, the breakout's volume, the stop's distance and the market's breadth floor are not moved: each says what the setup or the market is rather than how many pass, and each stays the operator's to rule. A gate no value in its range brings inside its band, and the trigger, which has no threshold, are named as findings rather than forced, since a range that cannot reach a band says the gate or its band is wrong, and that is a ruling. The proposer writes one proposal a version, on the night the trigger is crossed, states the crossing on its run log row and writes neither a version nor the register; after a rejection the version stays open and the next proposal for it waits on sixty more ordinary nights, so a rejected proposal is not asked again of the same evidence. The operator's `shape` command is the only writer of a version and of a proposal's decision: an acceptance, of a proposal or of settings the operator rules, closes the open version and opens the next, named by its place in the order opened, and a rejection writes its reason and nothing else (see: A shape acceptance restarts the live filter's edge clock, and after one acceptance while the list is live each further one states the blocks it restarts).

**A variant of the swing filter is registered as a whole rule and runs on unchanged when the live settings move** Each of the five variants registered beside the live filter states every threshold of the filter, the one it varies and the ones it shares, as numbers in its register row. An acceptance that moves the live settings therefore changes nothing any variant tests, and its record stays a record of its own rule against its own null. What a variant loses is its reading as the live filter with one thing changed, so each variant's entry on the run page names the filter version it was defined against and, once the live filter has moved, the dimensions in which the two now differ. A variant crossing its boundary proposes its own whole rule as the live one. The variants are not registered again when the live settings move, because that would restart six clocks to keep a label current.

**The swing filter's shape is calibrated over its ordinary nights, a night one cause pushes past a quarter and twice its usual share is left out, and each band spans a third to three times what the ruled filter passes** The operator ruled on 2026-09-24 that tonight's list becomes a swing filter, so the six reasons stop choosing it and no reason's threshold is calibrated to a share of the index; what is calibrated is how many names each of the filter's gates passes. The shape clock counts, over the nights stored under the open filter version, how many members pass each gate after the market and every gate before it, with the market gate held open, since the market opens or closes the whole list and is not calibrated by count, and how many of those no exclusion removes. A night is an event night where a gate counted that way, or a reason, whose own median share over every night in the window is below a quarter passes or fires for more than a quarter of the index and for more than twice that median, or where the index's median volume against its fifty-day average is 1.8 or more; the market gate is never read for it. The medians are over every night in the window, event or not, so a night's classification never depends on the classification it decides. The operator ruled on 2026-09-25 that a flood is also more than twice its measure's own median: under the ruled settings trend and strength passes about 23% of the index, and a quarter alone marked eight ordinary sessions of the stored year, seven of them in the July rally, for passing 25 to 29%. An event is one cause pushing something far above its own normal share, and twice its median says so directly for every gate and reason alike, where excepting one broad gate by name would leave the next to be found the same way. Over the stored sessions this marks 2026-09-18 alone, the quarterly expiry on which unusual volume fired on half the index against a median near 2% and the index traded at twice its average. A gate counted alone was the first reading and marks ordinary nights, the setup gate passing 27 to 28% of the index on three of the twelve stored nights against a median of 24%, so the count through the funnel is the one read. An event night is counted, marked on the run page and read by no median. Each gate's median count over the ordinary nights is held against a band fitted to what the ruled filter passes, a third to three times its median over the 52 ordinary sessions of the stored year it was measured on, the low end rounded down and the high end up: 38 to 347 through trend and strength over a median of 115.5, 14 to 126 through the setup over 42, 6 to 56 through the trigger over 18.5, 1 to 12 through the trade over 4, and the list 1 to 9 over 3. The operator ruled the width: shape calibration exists to correct a threshold set wrong and not to follow the market, and the stored nights show the uptrend count falling from 109 to 66 in two weeks, an ordinary change of regime that half to twice would have announced as a mis-setting; with one cheap acceptance while the list is live, a wide band spends it on a real one. A proposal is still written at the trigger, and a band decides only whether it moves a gate. Before a version is open the filter runs on section 17's proposed values and no night counts toward the sixty ordinary nights the calibration waits on; from the first version, the nights under it do. The reasons' shares stay on the run page as context. This supersedes the entry that calibrated each reason to a target share, whose obligation is discharged with it (owes: The swing filter's shape calibrated from its ordinary nights).

**A swing filter row carries both swing plans, each scored from the night's close, and a candidate's setups are scored on the plan its own trade gate reads** Every swing filter row stores the swing trade at the nearest bands and section 10's plan for it beside the ladder's first tranche, both entered at the night's close, and the forward return filler scores each plan a row carries the setup's way with no zone: stopped on a close below its stop, won on a close at or above its target, and unresolved after 63 sessions, the setup's cap, with its planned break-even and calibrated bar written on the night it resolves as the listing's setup has them. A plan whose night's own close does not sit between its stop and its target is one the night could not enter at its close, and it is counted as not scorable rather than scored from a later fill. Each plan's outcome inside 20 sessions is stored beside it as context and read by no verdict: a swing trade of a few weeks is mostly decided inside a month, and a quicker read kept out of every test cannot become a second one. A swing family candidate's verdict names the plan its trade gate read, and its record reads that plan's outcomes, so the variant at the nearest bands is judged on the trade it would have taken and every other candidate on section 10's; the near misses read the plan their version's trade gate reads. Rows written before section 10's plan was stored carry the nearest bands' plan alone, and a verdict written before any verdict named its plan is read on that plan, which is the one every candidate standing then read (see: The swing filter's trade gate reads section 10's plan for the swing trade, and the plan at the nearest bands is the variant in the reward to risk variant's place).

**A gate's near misses are the setups it alone rejected, each group read against its own break-even and null and withheld below the block floor** Beside the setups the swing filter admitted, each gate and each exclusion has a group of its own: the rows the one gate or exclusion rejected while every other gate passed and no other exclusion applied, over the rows the open filter version stored. Each group is read the way a candidate's record is, its share of setups reaching target before stop against the mean of its own planned break-evens and of its calibrated bars, and no figure is drawn below eight non-empty blocks. A group is a population and not a test: nothing is promoted or retired on it, and it says which gate is costing setups and which is earning its place. A member the setup gate rejects has no band to stop below and so no plan to score, and the market gate's rows are whole nights, every member passing everything else on a night the market closed. The groups read the gate answers the nights stored and never evaluate a gate again, so no setting moved since changes a figure they state.

**A starting point is proposed from the deepest setting of a plateau on the edge and never its best variation, and nothing is registered before the operator approves it** Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-30 over the sweep's first run, superseding the plateau's centre with its depth and the raw average with the edge, in three reviews of the plan: every surviving condition is carried, the conditions enter the fine search as dials, a band difference is fixed with the night's own builder, stage 2 searches by sampling and measures depth one dial at a time in place of filling the grid, across the designs the edge comes first and then the depth, and a grid end on a dial of three or more values is looked beyond. The operator's aim stands as ruled on 2026-09-29: a starting point the history supports and not a variation fitted to it. The sweep replays the swing filter over every session the store and the pulled history hold from 2019-01-02 across 46,656 designs and nine dials, with one open trade a stock inside every variation and every result read as an edge (see: The sweep ranks on the edge over the same plan entered on every member, with one open trade a stock and seven conditions tested in steps). Stage 1 reads every design at three values of each dial and ranks the designs by the median edge of their viable settings, a setting with at least 300 scored trades beating both its break-even and no skill in at least 6 of the 8 years, a design with fewer than 100 viable settings ranking below every other by its share. Stage 2 takes the five strongest distinct rows of the conditions' crossing and searches each: every coarse setting under its combination and a sample of the grid balanced over every value of every dial, sized from the measured time against a budget of 4 hours a design; the plateau's line is the best edge among the evaluated settings meeting four floors less a margin of 0.05 of the risk, fixed before any depth is measured; a setting's depth is the fewest single steps it can move on one dial in one direction before leaving the plateau, counted to 6, and the grid does not limit it beyond off, at either end of a two-value dial or at a dial's own end; the 100 highest-edge settings meeting the floors are measured, the deepest is proposed with ties to the higher edge and then to the setting nearest the live rule, a proposal trailing the live rule's edge in any of the last three years is passed over, every one-step and two-step single-dial move is measured and a deeper one taken until none is, and a grid end the depth runs into on a dial of three or more values is looked beyond by up to two values at the spacing of the last two tested and within what the rule allows, a dial that cannot be extended or whose end still bounds the depth named as a limit. Across the designs the proposals within the margin of the highest edge are kept and the deepest of them is the starting point. The four floors, each proposed and the operator's to rule: beating its break-even in 6 of the 8 years and with its best year removed, 300 trades in 22 of the 30 blocks of 63 sessions, and a stock listed on 60% of nights. A variant moves one dial one or two steps, a condition among them, or changes one structural choice, and passes four tests read on the edge: on the plateau, open with neither side winning more than 5 of the 8 years, picking at least a quarter of its stocks where the starting point does not, and holding 30 trades in every year; up to six are proposed, and where fewer than three pass the strongest three by edge are reported with what each fails. The report states the starting point at margins of 0.03 and 0.08 beside the ruled one, and the margin is the operator's to rule. The history holds only the departures the provider still serves bars for, so the report states the share of index-nights with no bar served and, above 3%, says plainly that the results read better than the market was. The sweep proposes and registers nothing: the freeze that registers the starting point and its variants is one registration at one instant on the operator's word (see: The variations a sweep explores are exploration, and count toward no correction of the live family).

**The sweep ranks on the edge over the same plan entered on every member, with one open trade a stock and seven conditions tested in steps** Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-30 over the first run's report, which ranked on the raw average result, scored a stock's repeated listings as separate trades, never showed it read each session as it stood, and searched the live rule's dials alone. The edge subtracts, from each trade's result, the average result of the same plan entered at the same night's close on every member the index held with a bar and no gap, the stop and target at the same distances in each member's own typical moves, scored on real closes under the same exit: the operator chose that over the calibrated no-skill walk, which has no drift and so averages minus the round trip whatever the market did, so ranking on it would repeat the raw average; the raw average is shown beside the edge everywhere, and the calibrated bar stays in the viability test. One open trade a stock holds inside every variation, as the pages read it (see: A stock holds one open trade on each rule's list, and it is free the night after its trade ends): a walk over each design's candidates in name and session order keeps a listing unless the stock's kept trade is still open on its session, one open-until marker a name for each exit, and the listings kept off are counted beside each record; a table that sums candidates by cell cannot apply the rule, since a looser setting can add an earlier listing that blocks a later one. Point in time is proved before the first stage: 25 name-sessions a scored year, 13 of them candidate sessions and 12 member-sessions with a year of bars, drawn with a stated seed, and every name-session the live list listed, each rebuilt with the night's own components over a scratch store holding that name's year of bars and compared exactly on the averages, ATR, RSI and fifty-day volume, the year's swings, every band and the live label; Wilder's ATR and RSI are seeded where the night's year begins on each session and the swings read are the year's, which is the difference the first run's whole-history seed would have shown; any difference stops the run and the report lists every one. Seven conditions are tested, each read as the data stood on the session: the close over the highest high of 252 sessions, the sector's rank by the median 126-session return of its members labelled that night, with a name carrying no sector kept in and reading no rank rather than removed, which would flatter the condition through survivorship; the turn-up day's volume over the fifty-session average before it; Wilder's RSI below a level since the reference high and up on the trigger's session; the newest earnings surprise within a window and of a size, from the surprises pulled back to 2018 where a pull stored any and from the calendar's year otherwise, flagged as reading survivors; the tightness; and the pullback's length with the largest gap down inside it. Step (b) adds each of the 31 settings alone to the 10 strongest distinct designs at their coarse centres and keeps it on a design where the edge rises in 6 of the 8 years, 2 of them among the last 3, with 300 scored trades left; a condition survives where any setting is kept on 6 of the 10, no best setting chosen, since a setting with no effect passes one design about one time in eight and six of ten independent designs would happen about once in two thousand, the designs being correlated and every count printed. Step (c) crosses every survivor on and off at the middle of its tested settings with the ten designs and each one's structural neighbours short of the exit over the coarse settings, and the five strongest distinct rows go to stage 2 with every survivor as a dial. The report counts, for each design carried, the name-sessions each kind of band support lists at its proposal and the listings that differ.

**The stepped plan's result is counted on the risk its plan stated, and a fill nearer its stop than the stop setting's floor is no trade** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-01 over the sweep's run of that day, which proposed no starting point. A result is counted in multiples of the risk, and the sweep read the risk from the fill. A plan bought at the close is filled at its own entry, but the stepped plan is bought where a close first sits in its zone, anywhere down to its stop, while its stop setting reads the distance from the entry its plan named. A fill a hair over the stop left a risk near nothing: one trade read as 1,474 times its risk and another as a loss of 22,744 times it, and a few such trades carried every design that run ranked highest, each of which stood at or under nought without its five largest results. So the stepped plan's result is its return over the distance its plan stated, the entry it named to its stop, and a fill nearer its stop than the low bound of the setting's stop option is no trade under that setting, counted as the scorer counts a setup never entered: listed, holding its night and the stock through its end, and carrying no result. No other plan's counting changes, since each is filled at its own entry. Building it found a second fault in the same place: a trade that ran out of sessions carried its return and not its fill, so it was given no result, while the benchmark it is read against counts a member still open at a hold's cap, and the shorter the hold the more trades the average left out. It is counted at its last close, from the fill the scorer's own entry rule gives, and never as a win. Every record the page states is stated again without its 5 largest results by size, a large loss leaving as a large win does, and the page counts for each kind of plan the results beyond 20 times the risk, so a figure a few trades carry is seen where it is read. The first run's proposal bought at the close on section 10's plan, and Past picks divides a return by the listing's own buy to its stop, so neither carried the first fault; the second touched every design of both runs, least at the longest hold. The run of 2026-10-01 stays as written, since a finished run is never written again, and what it ranked is not relied on.

**The variations a sweep explores are exploration, and count toward no correction of the live family** Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-29 with the sweep. The family's correction divides its level among the trials registered and judged on live evidence after their registration, which is what makes a crossing mean something. No variation the sweep reads over history is judged on that evidence: every checkpoint judges only the trades after the freeze, and the explored history is read by no look. Counting the 918 million variations among the trials would leave no level any candidate could reach, and the freeze fixes the family before any evidence it is judged on exists, which is the condition the correction protects. At each later promotion the variants are based again around the new live rule and the sweep is run again over the added years to choose the next open questions, and never to judge one.

**The sweep reads the live store read-only in short reads and writes nothing to it, pausing for every night** Ruled by the operator on 2026-09-29, reading the live store directly and never a copy since nothing is frozen by a run. It opens the store read-only and never immutable, since nights write to it between its reads, and reads one statement a name for its bars, its pulled bars and the membership and earnings dates, closing each before it computes anything; the store's journal mode is DELETE, so a writer's commit waits at most for the one statement in flight, milliseconds against the six hundred seconds every connection the worker opens waits. The history it replays ends at the newest session the store's bars hold when computing starts, fixed for the run, so the nights added meanwhile change nothing it reads. Its progress, its results and its report are files in a folder beside the store and never rows in it. It is one process that looks after itself: it saves its work in chunks, the candidates by blocks of sessions, stage 1 by blocks of 50 designs and stage 2 by design; on a weekday it pauses from 23:00 UTC, less the chunk about to start, until the night has taken its lock and let it go, and whenever the night's or a drain's lock is held, reading a lock let go clear on two polls in a row before it goes on; a chunk that fails is tried once more, and failing again stops the run and writes its report saying where and why; and a run whose measured time projects past five days stops after stage 1 and reports. Started again it reads the history again and goes on from the first chunk it lacks, computing the candidates again from the start where the history read differs from the one they were computed over.

**The base is today's rule with a reward-to-risk floor of 2 until the ideas' run says otherwise** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-01 with the amended brief for the ideas' run. Within today's kind of rule the strongest single change the saved history showed was the reward-to-risk floor raised to 2, a gap inside the noise and the best of the changes tried, so it is the base each new idea is added to and not a result, and today's rule stands beside every figure the run states. The operator froze the pullback at that base on 2026-10-02 (see: The pullback freezes at the base with its reward-to-risk floor raised to 2, and the ideas' run waits until phase 13 is finished), so the base is the live rule; a starting point the ideas' run proposes away from it is a new freeze of its own.

**A new idea is added to the base one at a time and kept only where it is better in six of eight years** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-01 with the amended brief, after the sweep's run of that day found nothing steady past the base. The ideas' run tests kinds of idea the sweep's grid does not hold: market switches, a stop touched inside the day, the night's best three, a trailing stop, a stop no closer than one typical move, and the base's own exit held for less, the last two holds added on the operator's word once the counting was corrected. Each is added to the base alone, every setting fixed before any result is read, and passes where it is better than the base in at least 6 of the 8 scored years with 2 of the last 3, no lower over the last three together and still higher without the five largest results by size, with at least 1,000 trades and, for every idea but a market switch, a stock listed on at least 40% of the nights, the two floors the operator's rulings. The switches that pass are tested together; the exits are alternatives, so of those that pass the one with the higher edge is taken; and the starting point is the base with every idea taken, tested as a whole, the ideas added one by one in order of their gain in edge where the whole fails. Where none passes the starting point is the base, and where the base is not better than today's rule by the same test it is today's rule. An idea with no effect passes the yearly half by luck in 34 of the 256 ways the years can fall, so the report states the ideas tried and passed beside what luck alone would pass. Nothing is registered: a starting point the run proposes is frozen only on the operator's go.

**A market switch is judged on the year's total result, since the edge subtracts what every member made that night** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-01 with the amended brief. The edge reads a trade against the same plan entered on every member that night, so a switch that keeps the list off a falling market takes the bad nights out of the trades and out of the benchmark alike, and the edge cannot show what it saved. A switch is judged instead on each year's total result in risks, a night it kept off counting nothing, by the same yearly rule, the last three years together, the total without the five largest results by size and the floor of trades, and it must raise the plain result a trade as well; no floor of nights is read, since keeping off is what a switch is for. Its edge is stated beside the total.

**The frozen families are read with the pullback's ideas one at a time, and nothing they show is frozen or registered** The operator ruled on 2026-10-02: "After the rerun finishes, run the ideas' run on the breakout and the earnings drift as frozen, each idea added alone with the same pass test: the six market switches, the stop sold on touch, the stop at least one typical move under the buy, the best three a night, and holds of at most 10 and 20 sessions; plus the trailing stops of 2 and 3 typical moves for the drift only. State the tries, the passes and the luck figure for each. Nothing is frozen or registered." Each family's base is its live rule at the setting it froze at, a level of its own sweep's grid, replayed over the history that sweep reads with the market check at the live filter's floor, five a night in the family's own order and one open trade a stock, its trades read as an edge over the same plan entered on every member that night. Each idea is added to it alone, every setting fixed before any result is read, and judged by the pullback's test against the base: a market switch on the year's total result, every other idea on the edge with the floor of nights. The touched stop reads each session's open and low against the stop the closes before it set, the breakout's under its trail and the drift's before its target; the best three are the first three in the family's own order once its own open trades have kept a stock off; the holds cut the family's own exit short; and the trails are read on the drift alone, in its target's place, since the breakout's plan already trails. Each new exit's benchmark is the same plan on every member that night under that exit. The report states each family's tries, its passes and what luck alone passes of that many, an idea with no effect passing the yearly half in 34 of the 256 ways the years can fall, and says where an idea changes nothing the rule makes. Nothing it shows is frozen or registered: a family's rule changes only by a freeze of its own on the operator's word.

**The context checks are read on the frozen families one at a time, and nothing they show is frozen or registered** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03: "Every check below is tested before it is used, and joins as a variant or through the ideas' run, never as an untested gate." The checks that survived the data checks are read in one run by hand, from main's own build: the earnings drift's revenue growth, as a filter and as an order of its picks, and the pullback's order by how far its RSI fell during the dip. Each is added alone to its family as frozen, replayed over the history its sweep reads, and judged against it: the drift's by its own test (see: The drift's context ideas are judged by the year tests, the test without the five largest and a thousand trades with no floor of nights), the pullback's by the pullback's (see: A new idea is added to the base one at a time and kept only where it is better in six of eight years). The report states each family's tries and what luck alone passes of that many, two for the drift and one for the pullback, and nothing it shows is frozen or registered; a check joins a family only as a variant registered on the operator's go. No model reads anything the run reads, and no news is read: the provider's sentiment was not shown to be a fixed word list's score (see: Rules choose the stocks, and the AI writes the reports) (see: Six context checks are not adopted, each for the reason it states).

**A drift print's revenue growth is its quarter's revenue as first filed against the same quarter a year before** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03: "Revenue growth from the report date: both quarters as the first filing states them, fiscal fourth quarters the year less nine months, checked against 30 sampled releases, the share of picks filed after the buy stated." No period end is stored with a print, so its quarter is the newest its filer's facts state ending within the 100 days before the report, and the year before's the quarter ending within a week of a year before it, a fiscal year of 52 or 53 weeks moving an end by up to a week. Of the 21,113 pulled prints whose filer states a quarter before them, 97.7 per cent were reported within 60 days of its end and 98.0 within 100; past 100 the quarter found is mostly the one before a quarter the facts do not state, so a print with none in the span reads no growth rather than the wrong one. Both quarters are read as first filed whatever day that was, since a release states the quarter's revenue on its report day and each of the 29 releases found among the 30 sampled at 14.2 stated the figure the filing first filed; a filing coming days after the buy is the release's figure filed late, and the share of each idea's trades reading one filed after the buy is stated beside its figures. A year before that is nothing or less reads no growth.

**The drift's context ideas are judged by the year tests, the test without the five largest and a thousand trades with no floor of nights** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03: "Drift ideas judged by the year tests, the without-the-five-largest test and 1,000 trades, no night floor." A context idea on the earnings drift passes where it is better than the drift as frozen in six of the eight years with two of the last three, the last three together no lower, still better without its five largest results by size, and on at least a thousand trades; it is held to no share of the nights it lists on, since the drift lists only after a strong reported beat and leaves most nights empty by its nature, so a floor of nights fails every idea on it whatever its edge.

**The pullback's RSI-fall order keeps the night's first three by how far its RSI fell from its high session** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-03, from the brief's orderings: "since 'best three a night' was the one idea that passed, test other orderings of each swing family's picks against its own order: for the pullback, how far its RSI fell during the dip." The pullback's names on a night are ordered by Wilder's fourteen-session RSI on the session of the high the pullback came down from less the night's, the furthest fall first, a name whose high session or night holds no RSI after every one holding one, and the list's own order within each; the night's first three are kept, the count the ideas' run passed, so the order is judged against the base as the best three in the list's own order were, and those stand beside it in the report. The three settings are fixed before the run: the fourteen sessions, the high the pullback's own, and the three kept.

**A rule's record is replayed at its one setting by the sweep's own code over the pulled history, after costs on every index** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-05 with the phase 16 plan. The brief asked for each rule's own history from its sweep on its index, after costs: the share that won, the average result in risks, the typical worst dip before a trade ended and the typical holding time, with the trade count and the years covered. The sweep stores neither the dip nor the holding time: its figures for each setting are the twelve a year its measures hold, written to files under the sweep's own folder rather than to the store; the register's evidence column is empty on every registration; and the S&P 500's sweeps of phases 12 to 14 ran before costs. Copied from the sweep, two of the four figures would be dashed on every card and "after costs" would not hold on the S&P 500. So a command replays each rule a card can name, each family's live or provisional rule on each index, at its one setting over the pulled history with the sweep's own walk, each trade after its cost at the published table, and stores the four figures with the trades, the history's first and last session and whether membership was as it stood or survivors only. A rule that sets itself no target, as the trailing exit does, has no win and states no share won, and the heavyweights' results are in percent against their size cut. It costs one replay a rule rather than a search, and the remedy that registers or freezes a rule runs it. The settings were chosen on that same history, so the figures read high, and wherever they are drawn they say so.

**The operator's own record states its average result once twenty of its trades in a family and index have ended** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-05 with the phase 16 plan. The trades the operator records as taken are counted per family and index, won, lost and open, a trailing rule's as ended rather than won or lost, since it sets itself no target. The average result in risks is drawn once twenty of them have ended, and before that a dashed outline saying how many of the twenty have, because the rules' own threshold for a record is set for a rule's every pick and a personal record would not reach it for years, while three or four trades' average says more about the weeks they were taken in than about the operator's choices. The record is read beside the rule's own listed trades on the nights the operator took one, which separates the operator's choices and fills from the market of those weeks. Nothing decides from it.

**Every change to a rule waits for the operator's approval on the Loop page, and the automatic mode ships off** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-08 as the first ruling of phase 17's brief: "Every change waits for the operator's approval. Build the automatic mode behind a setting, ship with approval on, and never switch it without my word." The loop proposes and tests; it adopts nothing. A proposal that passes the tester is put to the operator on the Loop page, one a family an index a month at most, the strongest that passed, and becomes the rule's new settings only through a decision row the operator's press writes and a worker verb that applies it before the next night. The automatic mode is a setting, `EquityBrief:Loop:Adopt`, shipped as `on approval`, held there by a check reading the shipped settings, and read as `automatic` only where the operator has set it so by hand; the verb that applies a change refuses a proposal with no decision row unless the setting reads so. The brief asked for the mode to exist so the choice stands written where it can be made, and for it to ship off so nothing the operator has not read changes what the night lists.

**Each family on each index is proposed for, tested on and approved separately, on its own index's history alone** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-08 as the second ruling of phase 17's brief: "Each family on each index is its own rule ... Each is proposed for, tested on and approved separately, only on its own index's history, and nothing is carried to another index without its own proposal and approval. For a provisional rule, an approved change updates its provisional settings and its card stays provisional until its freeze." The S&P 500, 400 and 600 run each family as a rule of its own, and a result on one index says nothing of another's: phase 14's first test found the swing families' edges fall on the 1,500 where they held on the 500, and phase 15's sweeps found the same dial passing on one index and failing on the other. So an engine's proposal names one family on one index, the tester judges it on that index's setups alone against that index's current rule, the Loop page shows beside it whether the same idea was proposed on the other indices and how it tested there as information and never as evidence, and an approval changes that index's rule and no other's. The learned score alone is fitted across the three indices with the index as a reading, as the plan rules, since one index's breakout holds too few setups to fit on; its proposals are still made and tested per index, and the pooled fit is named on the Loop page as the one reading of another index's history a proposal rests on.

**A change is adopted only on test years the proposal never saw, and a search is judged as a procedure run year by year** Ruled with phase 17's plan on 2026-10-08. The brief's standing rule, "Nothing is judged on the data it was built from", and its tester, learning on the years before a test year and scoring on that year for every year from 2022, cannot hold for a setting a search chose over every stored year: every sweep that chose a live or provisional setting, and every search phase 17's Part 0 runs, reads 2019-01-02 to the newest pulled session, so scoring its setting on 2022 to 2026 judges it on the years it was picked on, and the corpus already says of those records that the figures read high (see: A rule's record is replayed at its one setting by the sweep's own code over the pulled history, after costs on every index). So the tester judges a search as a procedure: for each test year the search runs on the setups that ended before that year's first session, the setting it chooses is scored on that year against the index's current rule, and the proposal put to the operator is the procedure run on all finished data, with its fold stability stated, the same dial within a grid step chosen in at least three of five folds. The incumbent carries the same bias the other way, chosen on the test years it is compared over, so a challenger is understated against it, and the Loop page says so beside each test year. Nothing an engine builds reads a test year while it is built: the engines are handed a view of the ledger cut at the fold's end that refuses any read past it.

**The operator's taken trades never feed a pick, a rule, an engine or a test** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-08 among the standing rules phase 17 keeps, and held since 16.3 by `rules-choose-the-stocks` over every pick-making component. A trade the operator took is the operator's choice and fill on a pick the rules made, so a rule learning from it would learn the operator's hand and not the market's, and a test reading it would judge a rule on trades it did not choose. The ledger, the three engines, the tester, the alarm and the monthly run join the components the check holds, so none declares a read of the taken trades or the operator's record and no source under their folders names either table; the card's part of setups like tonight's pick reads the ledger's setups and no taken trade.

**A proposal passes the tester on a block sign-flip test of its total edge after costs against the current rule, corrected within a run and held to a fixed bar across runs** Ruled with phase 17's plan on 2026-10-08 in place of the brief's rule, which asked for better in at least 4 of the test years including the latest, still better with the five largest removed, at least 300 test trades, and a correction for every proposal tried in that run and all runs so far. Under the null the year rule alone passes a proposal with probability 5 in 32 at five periods and 1 in 4 at six, about the corpus's own luck rate of 37 in 256 a try; no heavyweights rule reaches 300 test trades, the S&P 500's holding 226 over 2022 to 2026 and the 400's and 600's 298 and 279, while every pullback and drift rule clears it; and a correction counted over all runs so far rises without end, a one-sided bar of 2.71 standard errors at the first run, 3.45 at the twelfth and 3.86 at the sixtieth, and the online methods that avoid it need independent tests, which reusing the same test years every month breaks. So the gate is the corpus's own test: the paired difference in total edge after costs between the candidate and the index's current rule, summed over 63-session blocks keyed on the entry session, closed windows only, studentised as a candidate's record is and enumerated over every sign vector (see: A candidate is judged by a sign-flip test over blocks of 63 sessions, with at least eight blocks), with a Romano-Wolf step-down across the proposals tested for the rule in the run (Romano and Wolf 2005) and the bar fixed at 0.05 over 12 a run, so the expected false approvals a rule over any twelve runs stay at or under 0.05 under any dependence, which is the luck figure shown beside the approvals, 0.05 a rule a year and 0.75 a year over the fifteen rules. Beside the gate stand three screens: better in at least 60 per cent of the complete test years holding 50 trades a side with the latest complete year among them, the partial year shown and not counted; still positive with the five largest absolute results removed from each side, or the five largest paired differences for an exit that keeps the trade set; and at least 300 test trades for a swing family, or at least 36 monthly paired book returns for the heavyweights, whose unit is the month. Power is stated on the Loop page: at this bar the pullbacks and drifts detect a difference of about 0.09 to 0.11 risks a trade four times in five and the breakouts about 0.14 to 0.17, so the breakout tests are near powerless and the page says so.

**A declined proposal is put again once a new complete block has been added since the decline and it passes with that block** Ruled by the operator on 2026-10-08, changing the plan's recommendation that a declined proposal wait for a new complete test year: "a declined proposal is put again once a new complete 63-session block has been added since the decline and it passes again with that block included." A decline is recorded with the operator's reason where one is given, and the proposal is not put again on the evidence it was declined on; it returns only where the record has grown by a whole block of 63 sessions since the decline and the tester passes it with that block among its test setups, so the operator reads it again only when something has changed.
