What changed is the sentence saying the overnight queue starts writing synthesis once it moves into the local lane, which a section resting on documents cannot be at night.

**A paid call is priced from the provider's own token counts at the rate its own timestamp falls in** A cap denominated in money needs a price for every call, and the price is the provider's arithmetic rather than this system's: its published rates, per model, for cached prompt tokens, uncached ones and output, halved outside two fixed peak windows. Read from the provider's page on 2026-09-13 at 14:57 UTC and captured before anything rested on it, the windows are 01:00 to 04:00 and 06:00 to 10:00 UTC on weekdays, and the rates are stated beside the models in the feed that calls them. A call's price is its response's own counts at those rates, doubled where the response's own creation instant falls at peak, because the provider's clock decides what it bills and this machine's does not. Thinking is billed as output and the provider counts it inside the completion, so nothing is added for it. A model with no price in the build is refused at startup rather than called, because a call recorded as costing nothing is spend the cap cannot see, and a response carrying no counts is refused for the same reason. The prices are the provider's and move when the provider moves them; the page says so, and the feed's rates are what a change edits. The provider's name, its models, its rates and its wire sit in its one feed file, because the scan that keeps a model off the night reads every shipped file for the provider's name and carves only the files that are model feeds. (see: Code owns every number) (see: Queued work runs off-peak, and every schedule is written in UTC)

Superseded at 6.7 by **The research model is named only in configuration, and a call is priced at the configured rates its own timestamp falls in**. The pricing rule stands entire: a call's own counts at the rate its own creation instant falls in, reasoning inside the output, a model with no price refused at startup, a response with no counts refused. What changed is where the provider, its models and its rates are written. This entry put them in the provider's one feed file, and the operator ruled on the day it landed that the research model is to be switchable to any model, which a feed named for one provider carrying that provider's rates as constants is not.

**Two models for two jobs, and the research model is DeepSeek V4** A local model on the operator's GPU writes prose from numbers, which is small and free. DeepSeek V4 does research, which is a large-context job a local model does badly. Chosen on cost: a research pass is a few cents rather than a few tens of cents, which puts a year of normal use in the tens of dollars. The tier, Pro or Flash, is left to the fixture comparison rather than decided here, because the difference between them is a few dollars a year and the only thing worth choosing on is the quality of the two-cases section.

Superseded at 6.7 by **Two models for two jobs, and which research model answers is configuration**. The two jobs and the reason for splitting them stand, and so does the choice of provider on cost, which is now the value the shipped configuration holds rather than this entry's name. What the name cost was that changing model would have contradicted a decision, so a setting the operator is meant to change could only have been changed by writing a new entry.

**The night runs at a fixed UTC instant set after the provider posts the day's bulk file** Superseded 11 September 2026, at the phase 5 sign-off, by the decision that keeps the instant while nights succeed and moves it on a night refused for a file not yet posted. What was superseded is how the instant is settled: this entry set it at the posting hour plus a margin, with the hour measured from live fetches, and a scheduled fetch can only bound that hour from above, so the measurement it waited for could not arrive. The form, a UTC instant registered with whatever scheduler the machine has, stands in the decision that replaced it. The reasoning was: Section 14 scheduled the night with Task Scheduler after the US close, which named one platform's mechanism and a local time, against two hard rules at once. The schedule is a UTC instant, registered with whatever scheduler the machine has, and the application neither knows nor asks which. The instant is the provider's posting hour plus a margin, and the posting hour is measured from live fetches rather than taken from documentation, because the first thing a live feed can be asked is when the file it fetches actually appears. This entry read "measured from live fetches at 2.1" until the phase 5 sign-off, which is where the fetch first ran and not where the hour is settled: 2.1 and 2.6 each bound it, and what settles it is nights that ran on the schedule, carried as an operating row rather than at any checkpoint (owes: The provider's posting hour for the day's bulk file, measured from live fetches). The rule this decision states is unchanged, so the entry is corrected rather than superseded; what was stale is a pointer inside its reasoning and not the ruling it makes. The form is decided here and the hour is a measurement, which is why the hour is not in this entry: a number nobody has measured, written into a decision, is the shape that gets cited later as though it were settled.

**News arrives in one dated feed request and is attributed to names locally** Superseded 10 September 2026, at 5.0, by the decision on one dated query paged to cover the day, because 2.5's measurement showed one day of news exceeding one request at the provider's limit. The name said one request and the day needs several, which is what was superseded; the attribution to names locally stands in the decision that replaced it. It was deleted rather than moved here in the commit that superseded it, and was restored at the phase 5 sign-off with its reasoning as it stood. The reasoning was: The feed is queryable by date with no ticker, which was probed on the operator's key at 1.5 rather than assumed: one request over a date range returns articles for the whole market, every row carries a `symbols` array naming the tickers it is about, and the response shape is identical whether or not a ticker was asked for. So a night makes one request and fans the rows out to names in code, and the per-name cost of news is zero calls rather than one call per name. The alternative, a request per ticker, would put five hundred calls on the nightly path for the same articles and would break the rule that a night costs the same for fifty names as for five hundred. The row also carries the article text, so the source document a claim rests on arrives with the row rather than needing a second fetch, which is what makes admissibility judgeable at all without spending again.

**The stop rule depends on the trend state** In a range the stop is the range floor; in an uptrend it trails the last higher low; in a downtrend there are no tranches. Kaminski and Lo, Journal of Financial Markets, 2014, show a stop reduces expected return under a random walk and can add value where returns trend, so one stop rule for both cases is wrong in one of them.

Superseded at 5.0 by **The trailing stop is the higher of the band beneath and the last swing low**. The reasoning stands entire and what changed is the trailing half: "it trails the last higher low" names a price and not a floor, and 4.5 found that three of the four fixture names are in an uptrend and the literal reading puts their stop inside the band the first tranche sits on. Found by the phase 4 sign-off, which read the code against the corpus and found the corpus had no such rule.

**Three source lists, not one, each with a review date** Filings and company releases need no list at all, since they are fetched by ticker. A company-news list governs reporting about a company. A separate industry list governs theme material, because the publishers who write about memory contract pricing are not the ones who write about quarterly earnings. Splitting them keeps a theme search from returning earnings coverage and the reverse. Every list carries a review date, because sites are acquired, paywalled and abandoned, and a list nobody revisits silently narrows what the system can see.

Superseded at 1.7 by **Two source lists govern the open web, and the licensed feed is a channel rather than a list**. Its body always described two lists and said filings need none; the name said three, which is contradiction J. The measurement added the second half, that the lists govern the search tool rather than the licensed feed.

**The earnings trade is a second book** It has its own setups, its own stops and a horizon of days, and it never merges with the position book, because holding a momentum entry through a print is the thing the separation exists to prevent.

Superseded at 4.0 by **The second book is keyed to a dated event, and an earnings print is the only kind on file**. The reasoning is intact and carries over whole: the separation is about carrying a momentum entry through a date, which the body said and the name did not. What the name cost was a question nobody could answer from it, being whether the book may carry an event that is not a print, and section 10 had to defer that to 4.0 in the same paragraph that cited this name. The replacement answers it in the name: the book is keyed to a dated event, and the kinds it can carry are the kinds the calendar holds.

Superseded, kept with the reasoning that made them right at the time. `no-superseded-citation` fails any citation resolving to a name below this line.

**The fixture is diffed on the facts file, not on a rendered page** Superseded 8 September 2026. The reasoning was that prose varies between runs and layout changes constantly, so diffing rendered output would fail for reasons that do not matter and pass over reasons that do. That reasoning is intact and carries into the decision that replaced it. What fell was the scope: the facts file is written by the facts assembler at 4.3, and phases 1 through 3 produce expectations with no facts file in existence, so as written the rule was false for three phases and a session reading it would have taken a decision that does not apply yet for one that does. It fell to The fixture is diffed on each stage's serialised output, of which the facts file is one.

**Fundamentals hand-entered per quarter in a per-name file** Superseded 3 September 2026. The reasoning was that four updates a year per name, by an operator who reads every print anyway, beat a model extracting numbers from a press release on both cost and accuracy. It fell to the universe decision: at five hundred names that is two thousand edits a year. The provider carries reported quarters, balance sheets, ratings and the earnings calendar, and the filings archive carries segment tables and guidance, so the file is no longer needed. An optional overlay file for a name you follow closely remains available for anything a provider cannot supply.

**One self-contained HTML file per report, written nightly** Superseded 3 September 2026. The reasoning was that such a file opens anywhere and diffs cleanly. It fell to the single-page application decision, which removed nightly rendering entirely. The property it protected is preserved by the exporter.

**A 250-bar level window** Superseded 4 September 2026. It was written into v0.1 as a limit and then contradicted by v0.1's own reference report, which used fifty bars. The window is now sixty sessions, which is what the reference did, and the moving averages read the full stored year.

**Five years of stored history** Superseded 3 September 2026. The reasoning was that several years of nights would let condition thresholds be calibrated across different market moods. It fell to two facts: the report needs one year, and a few weeks of nights across five hundred names already gives tens of thousands of name-nights, with the run page recording the distribution as it happens.

**Nothing is pre-warmed** Superseded 5 September 2026. The reasoning was that researching names in anticipation would pay for nights you did not open them, which is the trade rejected everywhere else in the design. It fell to hardware: a machine with enough memory to run a large model locally makes anticipation free, so the objection was to spending money in advance rather than to doing work in advance. Paid research is still strictly on demand.

**A quarterly research budget allocated across names** Superseded 4 September 2026, the same day it was proposed. The reasoning was that research refreshes slowly, so a fixed number of passes a year could be bought and allocated by a rule. It was a schedule wearing different clothes, and it was rejected because it would pay for names nobody opens. Research now runs only on demand.

**A three-tier split, with researched sections only for watch-list names** Superseded 4 September 2026. The reasoning was that per-company research could not be automated at scale. That was wrong: the sources are reachable, and the constraint was cadence and spend rather than availability. Any name can be researched; the question is only whether you asked for it.

**A crossing is a candidate condition only where the close sits a margin of the typical move past the edge it crossed** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 that the candidate requires the close to sit at least 0.25 of the name's typical daily move beyond the edge it crossed, measured from tonight's close: the high edge for a crossing upward and the low edge for a crossing downward, with the typical move the one the ladder reads. The margin 0.25 is proposed, and before anything is registered the counts at 0.25 and at 0.5 go back to the operator, because 0.25 lands at 38 to 87 a night on 2026-09-16 to 09-18 where the 2026-09-20 measurement of the tight setting gave 20 to 48, which 0.5 reproduces. The defect it answers: 19% of 1,132 recorded crossings finished within a tenth of a typical move of the edge, and one closed 9 cents past a level and listed. Not settled by research: no study found sets the margin. Osler (2003) finds stop-loss orders cluster just beyond round numbers and rates move rapidly once they cross them, which bears on what follows a crossing and not on the margin.

Superseded on 2026-09-22 by **A crossing is a candidate condition only where the close sits half a typical move past the edge it crossed**. The condition this entry states is unchanged, and so is the reason for having a margin at all. What changed is the number: the counts it sent back to the operator were re-derived at 10.3 and the operator settled the margin at 0.5, so the margin is no longer a proposal and no longer carries an open question about which of the two it should be.

**The numbers section shows the dividend the provider files, on the newest filing alone** The forward annual rate, the forward yield as the provider states it, the payout ratio, the ex-dividend date and the pay date, all from the provider's SplitsDividends part, as the analysts' ratings are read (see: The fundamentals row carries the analysts' ratings the provider files, on the newest filing alone). No yield is computed at tonight's close. A name the provider files no dividend for gets one line saying so. It is presentation in an existing table, which the fundamentals ruling leaves standing.

Superseded on 2026-09-24 by **The numbers section draws the dividend the provider files from the newest filing alone, and nothing for a company paying none**. Everything this entry ruled stands but one sentence: a name the provider files no dividend for drew one line saying so, and on the operator's ruling of 2026-09-24 it draws nothing, while a row holding no dividend part still says so.

**Section 4 of the architecture defines the report, and nothing outside the corpus does** The eleven sections, what produces each, and whether each costs money are stated there. Sections are added, removed or reordered by amending that table, and `architecture-conformance` checks the code against it. A hand-written example illustrates the rules and is never the thing to be reproduced: where an example and a rule disagree, the rule is right.

Superseded on 2026-09-24 by **Section 4 of the architecture defines the report region by region, and nothing outside the corpus does**. Everything this entry ruled stands but two statements: the report's eleven sections, which the page outgrew region by region while the table stood unchanged, and `architecture-conformance` checking the code against the table, which no check did, so nothing read the table against the page until the operator found it out of date.

**A reason's threshold is calibrated to a target share of the index over ordinary nights and a night a usually quiet reason floods is left out** The operator ruled on 2026-09-23 that section 11's thresholds are calibrated to a target rather than against a distribution no rule turns into a number. Each reason's median nightly share of the index is to be at or below 2%, about ten names, and the share firing any reason at or below 6%, about thirty, both proposed: a reason firing on a large share of the index has setups whose base rate is the universe's, so its measured edge comes out near zero by construction, and the list draws twenty, 4% of the index, so a target far above that leaves the tie-breaker choosing. A night on which a reason fires for more than a quarter of the index, while that reason's own median nightly share is below a quarter, is an event session: it is counted, marked on the run page and left out of the sample, because its setups share one cause and are closer to one observation than to hundreds. The ruling first named any reason above a quarter, and the eight nights from 2026-09-09 to 2026-09-18 marked every one of them by it, at entry zone firing on 39 to 47% of the index each night, so no night would have been ordinary and the calibration could never have started; the operator ruled the same evening that a reason above a quarter on its ordinary nights is flooding by its threshold, which is what the calibration moves, and marks no session. Once sixty ordinary nights of listings written under the current code are stored, each threshold is moved once, to the value that puts its median nightly share at its target, as a new decision citing the measurement and opening a new window (see: A reason's record reads only the rows written under the threshold the code carries, and the rows written under another are kept), and a reason no threshold brings to its target is reported as such rather than forced (owes: The six reason thresholds calibrated from the nights they fired on).

Superseded on 2026-09-25 by **The swing filter's shape is calibrated over its ordinary nights, and a night one cause floods is left out**. What this entry protected stands for the gates: a count that floods says little, a night one cause floods is counted and left out, and a median is read over the nights a rule was written under. What changed is what is calibrated, from each reason's share of the index against a target to each gate's count through the funnel against a band, because the reasons no longer choose the list, and the event rule's medians, now over every night in the window rather than over the ordinary ones.

**The swing filter's shape is calibrated over its ordinary nights, and a night one cause floods is left out** The operator ruled on 2026-09-24 that tonight's list becomes a swing filter, so the six reasons stop choosing it and no reason's threshold is calibrated to a share of the index; what is calibrated is how many names each of the filter's gates passes. The shape clock counts, over the nights stored under the open filter version, how many members pass each gate after the market and every gate before it, with the market gate held open, since the market opens or closes the whole list and is not calibrated by count, and how many of those no exclusion removes. A night is an event night where a gate counted that way, or a reason, whose own median share over every night in the window is below a quarter passes or fires for more than a quarter of the index, or where the index's median volume against its fifty-day average is 1.8 or more; the market gate is never read for it. The medians are over every night in the window, event or not, so a night's classification never depends on the classification it decides, and over the stored nights this marks 2026-09-18 alone, the quarterly expiry on which unusual volume fired on half the index and the index traded at twice its average. A gate counted alone was the first reading and marks ordinary nights, the setup gate passing 27 to 28% of the index on three of the twelve stored nights against a median of 24%, so the count through the funnel is the one read. An event night is counted, marked on the run page and read by no median. Each gate's median count over the ordinary nights is held against a band, proposed: through trend and strength 50 to 100 names, through the setup 20 to 60, through the trigger 8 to 40, through the trade 5 to 35, and the list 5 to 30, each step keeping between about a fifth and two thirds of what reaches it, so the list can land inside its band whichever gate binds. Before a version is open the filter runs on section 17's proposed values and no night counts toward the sixty ordinary nights the calibration waits on; from the first version, the nights under it do. The reasons' shares stay on the run page as context. This supersedes the entry that calibrated each reason to a target share, whose obligation is discharged with it (owes: The swing filter's shape calibrated from its ordinary nights).
Superseded on 2026-09-25 by **The swing filter's shape is calibrated over its ordinary nights, a night one cause pushes past a quarter and twice its usual share is left out, and each band spans a third to three times what the ruled filter passes**. What this entry protected stands: the count through the funnel with the market held open, the medians over every night in the window, an event night counted and read by no median, and sixty ordinary nights under one version. What changed is that a flood must also be more than twice its measure's own median, and that the bands, proposed at about a fifth to two thirds of what reaches each gate, are fitted to a third to three times what the ruled filter passes.

**Arrival is the first night a name's trigger fires, and a setup it has held for several nights does not stop it** A pullback's trigger is a close above the previous session's high, or a close back inside or above the band after a session below its low edge; a breakout's is the first close above the band it cleared. The operator's text first read arrival as the setup not having passed the night before, and that and the trigger almost never hold together, because a name falls into its zone on a down day and closes back above the prior high on a later one. The operator ruled on 2026-09-24 that the setup is a place and the trigger the event: arrival is the trigger not having fired the night before, read off that night's stored gate result, so a name may sit in its pullback for several nights and lists on the first night it closes back above the previous high. The pullback's own window, a high made within the last twenty sessions, already bounds how long it can have sat there. For a breakout the rule is the one it already had.
Superseded on 2026-09-25 by **Arrival is a trigger that first fired within the last three sessions, and the trade is read from tonight's close**. What stands: the setup is a place and the trigger the event, each read off the stored results, a session that cannot say fails the gate, and a breakout's trigger is the break. What changed is the window, from the session before alone to the three sessions ending tonight.

**The swing filter's setups are scored on the swing trade's own plan from the listing close, and their first twenty sessions are context** The operator ruled on 2026-09-25 that the trade gate reads the swing trade's own plan, so that plan is the one the filter's setups are scored on. Every swing filter row carrying one, which is every row whose setup found a band, is scored the setup's way with no zone: entered at the night's close, stopped on a close below the setup band's low edge, won on a close at or above the low edge of the nearest resistance band above the close, and unresolved after 63 sessions, the setup's cap, with its planned break-even and calibrated bar written on the night it resolves as the listing's setup has them. A plan whose night's own close does not sit between its stop and its target is one the night could not enter at its close, and it is counted as not scorable rather than scored from a later fill. The same plan's outcome inside 20 sessions is stored beside it as context and read by no verdict: a swing trade of a few weeks is mostly decided inside a month, and a quicker read kept out of every test cannot become a second one. This is where the plan's twenty-session outcome lands under the ruled input, the setup the filter trades being the swing plan's and not the ladder's first tranche. The swing family's records are read over these outcomes, each candidate's setups being the rows it fired on.
Superseded on 2026-09-26 by **A swing filter row carries both swing plans, each scored from the night's close, and a candidate's setups are scored on the plan its own trade gate reads**. What this entry ruled stands: a plan scored the setup's way from the night's close with no zone, over the setup's cap with its bars written as it resolves, a plan the night's close cannot enter counted as not scorable, and twenty sessions stored as context and read by no verdict. What changed is that each row carries two swing plans and both are scored, and that a candidate's setups are read on the plan its own trade gate reads rather than on one plan for every candidate.

**The swing filter opens loose on the swing trade's own plan, and each of its five variants moves one setting to its other side** The operator ruled on 2026-09-25, from 12.2's counts and a re-measure under the three-session arrival window over the store's 53 sessions, that version 1 opens on the strength floor at 0.50, the pullback 1 to 5 typical moves deep, the dry-up below 1.5, the stop 0.5 to 4 typical moves below the entry, a reward to risk of 1.5, the trade gate reading the swing trade's own plan and arrival inside three sessions, with the market floor at 50% and the tightness, the breakout's volume and the earnings window at section 17's proposed values. Section 17's values as proposed listed no name on the median session and at most one, empty on 49 of 52 ordinary sessions, which leaves the shape clock nothing to tune and the edge clock almost nothing to score; the ruled values list a median of three names, from none to eight, empty on 5 of 52, a list that selects. The list will rarely approach the twenty the page can draw, and that is the filter's selectivity rather than a fault. The trade gate reads the swing trade's own plan because it is the trade the list recommends, entry at tonight's close, stop at the setup band's low edge and target at the nearest resistance band's low edge, where the ladder stages a position over tranches and scoring from the first tranche's zone would measure a different trade; it also passed more names and left fewer sessions empty. The live filter is the loose side and the family tests the other, since tightening is the direction worth testing: the family is the live filter and five variants, each a whole rule with every threshold stated and one setting moved to its other side, a reward to risk of 2 against the live 1.5, a pullback 1 to 3 moves deep against 1 to 5, the market gate off against on, strength in the top third, 2/3, against 0.50, and one-session arrival against three, six candidates tested at 0.05 over 6 (see: A variant of the swing filter is registered as a whole rule and runs on unchanged when the live settings move). The dry-up's variant first proposed gave its place to one-session arrival, because the counts show arrival moving the list more than any other setting, while the dry-up at a live 1.5 is near its loosest. Version 1 still takes one further acceptance at the first trigger after the list goes live, so none of these numbers is final (see: A shape acceptance restarts the live filter's edge clock, and after one acceptance while the list is live each further one states the blocks it restarts).
Superseded on 2026-09-26 by **The swing filter's trade gate reads section 10's plan for the swing trade, and the plan at the nearest bands is the variant in the reward to risk variant's place**. What this entry ruled stands: version 1's settings as ruled, the live filter the loose side and the family testing the tighter, each variant a whole rule with one thing moved, and one-session arrival in the dry-up's place. What changed is the plan the trade gate reads, from the swing trade at the nearest bands to section 10's plan for it, and the variant at a reward to risk of 2, whose place the plan at the nearest bands takes.

**Holm's level passes between the candidates by a graph fixed when they are registered, and every verdict shows the lifetime count** The operator ruled on 2026-09-22 to keep a per-window level and show the lifetime count. The level at Holm's first step is 0.05 over the three candidates, each spent across that candidate's looks by its own spending function. A graph carried in each register row passes a promoted candidate's level in equal shares to those still standing, each then tested at its raised level with its own spending function, and a retired candidate's level passes to no one. Only the sign-flip p-value is corrected. Under independence the first promotion's power is unchanged and the second and third reach 32.4% and 42.1% within a read. The per-window level lets lifetime error grow, six candidates each at a third of 0.05 bounding it at 0.10, so beside every verdict stand the step, its level, and the count of candidates ever registered, and the rule is revisited when that count reaches 8. Evidence: Holm (1979) and Romano and Wolf (2005) on step-down control at the same level with more power, the first step as strict as Bonferroni; Maurer and Bretz (2013) on passing a rejected hypothesis's level by a pre-specified graph under group-sequential designs, with sequential p-values as Liu and Anderson (2008) give them, cited as Maurer and Bretz cite it; Harvey, Liu and Zhu (2016) and Bailey and Lopez de Prado (2014) on counting every trial; Tian and Ramdas on online error control. (see: The candidate family is at most eight and the threshold is divided by it)
Superseded on 2026-09-27 by **Holm's level passes between the candidates by a graph fixed when they are registered, and its first step is 0.05 over the distinct trials read at a look or still running**. What this entry ruled stands: Holm's step-down, each candidate's spending across its own looks, the graph passing a promoted candidate's level to those still standing and a retired one's to no one, and the step and its level beside every verdict. What changed is the budget, from 0.05 over the candidates a window opened with to 0.05 over the distinct trials read at a look or still running, shared for the life of the system, and the count beside every verdict, from the names ever registered read against the eight at which this rule was revisited to the distinct trials, which is the revisit's outcome.
